.

38.1
Algebras and CSPs

Let
{\bf A}
and
{\bf B}
be algebras with the same signature, and let
R\leq{\bf A}\times{\bf B}
be a subalgebra.
The relation
R
can be viewed as the edge relation of a bipartite graph with colour classes
A
and
B
. Note that if
{\bf A}={\bf B}
and
\Clo({\bf A})=\Pol(\mathfrak{A})
, then the relations
R
that arise in this way are precisely the binary relations on
A
that are primitively positively definable in
\mathfrak{A}
. For example, if
\Clo({\bf A})=\Pol(K_{3})
,
then
E(K_{3})\leq{\bf A}^{2}
and the corresponding bipartite graph is drawn in Figure
13
.

Figure 13:
Illustration of
E(K_{3})\leq{\bf A}^{2}
, where
\Clo({\bf A})=\Pol(K_{3})
, as a bipartite graph.

The importance of the set-up of
R\leq{\bf A}\times{\bf B}
for CSPs is that we may imagine
A
as the possible values for a variable
x
in an instance of the CSP, and
B
as the possible values for a variable
y
in the CSP, and
R
represents a binary constraint between
x
and
y
. The advantage of this perspective is that many important definitions are very intuitively phrased in the language of bipartite graphs.

We start with the following fundamental definition from universal algebra which is highly relevant for the universal-algebraic approach to CSPs, in particular in Section
53
.

Definition 38.2
.

Let
k\geq 1
and
{\bf A}_{1},\dots,{\bf A}_{k}
be
\tau
-algebras.
Then
R\leq{\bf A}_{1}\times\cdots\times{\bf A}_{k}
is called
subdirect

if
\pi_{i}(R)=A_{i}
for every
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
.

If
{\bf A}
is the polymorphism algebra of a finite digraph
H
, then
there is a link between the notion of subdirect subalgebras of
{\bf A}^{2}
and arc-consistency. Let
G
be a finite digraph and let
L(x)\subseteq V(H)
be the list for
x\in V(G)
at the final stage of the evaluation of
\AC_{H}(G)
. Note that
for every
x\in V(G)
, the set
L(x)
is a subuniverse of
{{\bf A}}
.
Also note that every
(x,y)\in E(G)
we have that

E(H)\cap(L(x)\times L(y))
is subdirect in
L(x)\times L(y)
.

Also note that

E(H)
is subdirect in
{\bf A}^{2}
if and only if
H
has no sources and no sinks.
Digraphs without sources and sinks are also called
smooth
.

Let
x,y\in V(G)
and let
L(x)
and
L(y)
be the lists computed by the arc-consistency procedure.
Recall from Exercise
43
that
L(x)
and
L(y)
are subuniverses of the polymorphism algebra
{\bf A}
of
H
. Note that
E(G)\cap(L(x)\times L(y))\leq{\bf A}^{2}
is subdirect!

Exercices.

147.

Show that a digraph
G=(V,E)
is rectangular if and only if
E
,

when regarded as a bipartite graph with color classes
A
and
B

as described in this section, is a disjoint union of
bicliques
, i.e.,

if
a\in A
has a path to
b\in B
, then
(a,b)\in E
.

38.2
Pseudovarieties and Varieties

Varieties are a fascinatingly powerful concept to study classes of algebras.
The fundamental result about varieties is
Birkhoff’s theorem, which links varieties with equational theories
(Section
38.3
).
By Birkhoff’s theorem,
there is also a close relationship between varieties and the concept of an
abstract clone
(Section
38.4
).

If
\cal K
is a class of algebras of the same signature, then

•

\PPP({\cal K})
denotes the class of all products of algebras from
\cal K
.

•

\PPPfin({\cal K})
denotes the class of all finite products of algebras from
\cal K
.

•

\SSS({\cal K})
denotes the class of all subalgebras of algebras from
\cal K
.

•

\HHH({\cal K})
denotes the class of all homomorphic images of algebras from
\cal K
.

Note that closure under homomorphic images implies in particular closure under
isomorphism. For the operators
\PPP
,
\PPPfin
,
\SSS
and
\HHH
we often omit the brackets when applying them
to single singleton classes that just contain one algebra, i.e., we write
\HHH({\bf A})
instead of
\HHH(\{\bf A\})
.
The elements of
\HS(\bf A)
are also called the
factors

of
\bf A
.

A class
\cal V
of algebras with the same signature
\tau
is called
a
pseudovariety
if
\cal V
contains all homomorphic
images, subalgebras, and direct products
of algebras in
\cal V
, i.e.,
\HHH({\cal V})=\SSS({\cal V})=\PPPfin({\cal V})=\cal V
.
The class
\cal V
is called a

variety
if
\cal V
also contains all (finite and infinite)
products of algebras in
\cal V
.
So the only difference between pseudovarieties
and varieties is that pseudovarieties need not be closed under direct products of infinite cardinality.
The smallest pseudovariety (variety) that contains an algebra
\bf A

is called the pseudovariety (variety)
generated
by
\bf A
.

Lemma 38.3

(HSP lemma)
.

Let
{\bf A}
be an algebra.

•

The pseudovariety generated by
{\bf A}
equals

\HSPfin({\bf A})
.

•

The variety generated by
{\bf A}
equals
\HSP({\bf A})
.

Proof.

Clearly,
\HSPfin({\bf A})
is contained in the pseudovariety generated by
{\bf A}
, and
\HSP({\bf A})
is contained in the variety generated by
{\bf A}
.
For the converse inclusion, it suffices to verify that

\HSPfin({\bf A})
is closed under
\HHH
,
\SSS
, and
\PPPfin
.
It is clear that
\HHH(\HSPfin({\bf A}))=\HSPfin({\bf A})
.
The second part of Lemma
38.1
implies that
\SSS(\HSPfin({\bf A}))\subseteq\HS(\SP^{\fin}({\bf A}))=\HSPfin({\bf A})
. Finally,

\PPPfin(\HSPfin({\bf A}))\subseteq\HHH\PPPfin\SSS\PPPfin({\bf A})\subseteq\HSPfin\PPPfin({\bf A})=\HSPfin({\bf A})\;.

The proof that
\HSP({\bf A})
is closed under
\HHH
,
\SSS
, and

\PPP
is analogous.
∎

Pseudo-varieties are linked to primitive positive interpretability from Section
17.2
.

Theorem 38.4
.

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be a finite structure with polymorphism algebra
{\bf C}
.
Then
\mathfrak{B}\in\PP(\mathfrak{C})
if
and only if there exists
{\bf B}\in\HSPfin({\bf C})
such that
\Clo(\bf B)\subseteq\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
.

Proof.

We only prove the ‘if’ part of the statement here; the proof of the ‘only if’ part is similarly easy.
There exists a finite number
d\geq 1
, a subalgebra
\bf D
of
{\bf C}^{d}
,
and a surjective homomorphism
h

from
\bf D
to
\bf B
.
We claim that
\mathfrak{B}
has a primitive positive interpretation
I

of dimension
d
in
\mathfrak{C}
.
All operations of
{\bf C}
preserve
D
(viewed as a

d
-ary relation over
\mathfrak{C}
),
since
{\bf D}
is a subalgebra of
{\bf C}^{d}
.
By Theorem
21.6
,
this implies that
D
has a primitive positive definition

\delta(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})
in
\mathfrak{C}
, which becomes the
domain formula
\delta_{I}
of
I
.
As coordinate map we choose the mapping
h
.
Since
h
is an algebra homomorphism, the kernel

K
of
h
is a congruence of
{\bf D}
.
It follows that
K
, viewed as a
2d
-ary
relation over
C
, is preserved by all operations from
{\bf C}
.
Theorem
21.6
implies that
K
has a
primitive positive definition in
\mathfrak{C}
. This definition becomes
the formula
=_{I}
.
Finally, let
R
be a relation of
\mathfrak{B}
and
let
f
be a function symbol from the signature of
{\bf B}
. By assumption,
f^{\bf B}
preserves

R
. It is easy to verify that then
f^{\bf C}

preserves
h^{-1}(R)
.
Hence, all polymorphisms of
\mathfrak{C}

preserve
h^{-1}(R)
, and the
relation
h^{-1}(R)
has a primitive positive definition
in
\mathfrak{C}
(Theorem
21.6
), which becomes
the defining formula for the atomic formula
R(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})
in
I
.
This concludes our construction
of the primitive positive interpretation
I
of
\mathfrak{B}
in
\mathfrak{C}
.
∎

Primitive positive bi-interpretability can also be characterised with the varieties and pseudo-varieties generated by polymorphism algebras.
The following is a special case of Proposition 25 in
[
26
]
(where it is proved for a must larger class of countable structures).

Proposition 38.5
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
be structures with finite domains. Then the following are equivalent.

•

there are polymorphism algebras
{\bf A}
of
\mathfrak{B}
and
{\bf B}
of
\mathfrak{B}
such that

\HSPfin({\bf A})=\HSPfin({\bf B})
;

•

\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
are primitively positively bi-interpretable.

Proof.

For the forward implication,
we assume that there is a
d_{1}\geq 1
,
a subalgebra
{\bf S}_{1}
of
{\bf A}^{d_{1}}
, and a surjective
homomorphism
h_{1}
from
{\bf S}_{1}
to
{\bf B}
.
Moreover, we assume that there is a
d_{2}\geq 1
,
a subalgebra
{\bf S}_{2}
of
{\bf B}^{d_{2}}
, and a surjective homomorphisms
h_{2}
from
{\bf S}_{2}
to
{\bf A}
.
The proof of Theorem
38.4
shows that
I_{1}:=(d_{1},S_{1},h_{1})

is an interpretation of
\mathfrak{B}
in
\mathfrak{A}
,
and
I_{2}:=(d_{2},S_{2},h_{2})
is an interpretation of
\mathfrak{A}
in
\mathfrak{B}
. Because the statement is symmetric it
suffices to show that the (graph of the) function
h_{1}\circ h_{2}\colon(S_{2})^{d_{1}}\to B
defined by

(y_{1,1},\dots,y_{1,d_{2}},\dots,y_{d_{1},1},\dots,y_{d_{1},d_{2}})\mapsto h_{1}(h_{2}(y_{1,1},\dots,y_{1,d_{2}}),\dots,h_{2}(y_{d_{1},1},\dots,y_{d_{1},d_{2}}))

is primitively positively definable
in
\mathfrak{B}
.
Theorem
21.6
asserts that this is equivalent to showing that
h_{1}\circ h_{2}

is preserved by all operations
f^{\bf B}
of
{\bf B}
.
So let
k
be the arity of
f^{\bf B}
and let
b^{i}=(b^{i}_{1},\dots,b^{i}_{d_{1}})
be elements of

(S_{2})^{d_{1}}
, for
1\leq i\leq k
. Then indeed

\displaystyle f^{{\bf B}}((h_{1}\circ h_{2})(b^{1}),\dots,(h_{1}\circ h_{2})(b^{k}))

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle h_{1}\big(f^{\bf A}(h_{2}(b^{1}_{1}),\dots,h_{2}(b^{k}_{1})),\dots,f^{\bf A}(h_{2}(b^{1}_{d_{1}}),\dots,h_{2}(b^{k}_{d_{1}}))\big)

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle(h_{1}\circ h_{2})(f^{\bf B}(b^{1},\dots,b^{k}))\;.

For the backwards implication, suppose that
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
are primitive positive bi-interpretable via an interpretation

I_{1}=(d_{1},S_{1},h_{1})
of
\mathfrak{B}
in
\mathfrak{A}

and an interpretation
I_{2}=(d_{2},S_{2},h_{2})
of
\mathfrak{A}
in
\mathfrak{B}
.
Let
{\bf A}
be a polymorphism algebra of
\mathfrak{A}
.
The proof of Theorem
38.4
shows that

S_{1}

induces an
algebra
{\bf S}_{1}
in
{\bf A}^{d_{1}}
and

h_{1}
is a surjective homomorphism from
{\bf S}_{1}
to an algebra
{\bf B}
satisfying

\Clo({\bf B})\subseteq\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
.
Similarly,
S_{2}
is the domain of a subalgebra
{\bf S}_{2}
of
{\bf B}^{d_{2}}

and
h_{2}
is a homomorphism from
{\bf S}_{2}
onto an algebra
{\bf A}^{\prime}

such that
\Clo({\bf A}^{\prime})\subseteq\Pol(\mathfrak{A})
.

We claim that

\HSPfin({\bf A})=\HSPfin({\bf B})
. The inclusion ‘
\supseteq
’
is clear since
{\bf B}\in\HSPfin({\bf A})
.
For the reverse inclusion it suffices to show that
{\bf A}={\bf A}^{\prime}
since
{\bf A}^{\prime}\in\HSPfin({\bf B})
.
Let
f\in\tau
be
k
-ary; we show that
f^{{\bf A}}=f^{{\bf A}^{\prime}}
. Let
a_{1},\dots,a_{k}\in A
. Since
h_{2}\circ h_{1}
is surjective onto
A
, there are

c^{i}=(c^{i}_{1,1},\dots,c^{i}_{d_{1},d_{2}})\in A^{d_{1}d_{2}}

such that
a_{i}=h_{2}\circ h_{1}(c^{i})
.
Then

\displaystyle f^{{\bf A}^{\prime}}(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})\;=\;

\displaystyle f^{{\bf A}^{\prime}}(h_{2}\circ h_{1}(c^{1}),\dots,h_{2}\circ h_{1}(c^{k}))

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle h_{2}\big(f^{{\bf B}}(h_{1}(c^{1}_{1,1},\dots,c^{1}_{d_{1},1}),\dots,h_{1}(c^{k}_{1,1},\dots,c^{k}_{d_{1},1})),\dots,

\displaystyle\quad\;f^{{\bf B}}(h_{1}(c^{1}_{1,d_{2}},\dots,c^{1}_{d_{1},d_{2}}),\dots,h_{1}(c^{k}_{1,d_{2}},\dots,c^{k}_{d_{1},d_{2}}))\big)

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle h_{2}\circ h_{1}(f^{\bf A}(c^{1},\dots,c^{k}))

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle f^{\bf A}(h_{2}\circ h_{1}(c^{1}),\dots,h_{2}\circ h_{1}(c^{k}))

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle f^{\bf A}(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})

where the second and third equations hold since
h_{2}
and
h_{1}
are
algebra homomorphisms, and the fourth equation holds
because
f^{\bf A}
preserves
h_{2}\circ h_{1}
,
because
I_{2}\circ I_{1}
is pp-homotopic to the identity.
∎

Exercices.

148.

Show that an algebra has the empty algebra as a subalgebra

if and only if the signature does not contain constants

(i.e., function symbols of arity 0).

149.

Let
B
be a subuniverse of an algebra
{\bf A}
generated by
S\subseteq A
.

Show that an element
a\in A
belongs to
B
if and only if

there exists a term
t(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})
and elements
s_{1},\dots,s_{k}\in S

such that

a=t^{{\bf A}}(s_{1},\dots,s_{k})
.

150.

Show that the operators
\HS
and
\SH
are distinct.

151.

Show that the operators
\SP
and
\PS
are distinct.

38.3
Birkhoff’s Theorem

Birkhoff’s theorem provides a characterisation of varieties in terms of sets of
identities
.
A sentence in a functional signature
\tau
is called a (
\tau
-)
identity

if it is of the form

\forall x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\colon s=t

where
s
and
t
are
\tau
-terms over the variables
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
(such sentences are also called
universally conjunctive
). We follow the usual notation in universal algebra and sometimes write
such sentences as

s\approx t.

If
{\mathcal{K}}
is a class of
\tau
-algebras, then we say that
{\mathcal{K}}

satisfies

s\approx t
(or:
s\approx t

holds in

{\mathcal{K}}
), in symbols

{\mathcal{K}}\models s\approx t
, if every algebra in
{\mathcal{K}}
satisfies
s\approx t
.

Theorem 38.6

(Birkhoff
[
18
]
; see e.g.
[
64
]
or
[
41
]
)
.

Let
\tau
be a functional signature,
let
{\mathcal{K}}
be a class of
\tau
-algebras, and let
{\bf A}
be a
\tau
-algebra. Then the following are equivalent.

1.

All identities that hold in
{\mathcal{K}}
also
hold in
{\bf A}
;

2.

{\bf A}\in\HSP({\mathcal{K}})
.

If
{\bf A}
has a finite domain, and
{\mathcal{K}}=\{{\bf B}\}
for some algebra

{\bf B}
with a finite domain, then this is also equivalent to

3.

{\bf A}\in\HSPfin({\bf B})
.

Proof.

To show that
2. implies 1., let
s(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\approx t(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})

be an identity that holds in
{\mathcal{K}}
.
Then
s\approx t
is preserved in
products

{\bf A}=\prod_{i\in I}{\bf B}_{i}
of algebras
{\bf B}_{i}\in{\mathcal{K}}
. To see this, let

a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in A
be arbitrary.
Since
{\bf B}\models\phi
we have

s^{{\bf B}_{i}}(a_{1}[i],\dots,a_{n}[i])=t^{{\bf B}_{i}}(a_{1}[i],\dots,a_{n}[i])
for all

j\in I
, and thus
s^{{\bf A}}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})=t^{{\bf A}}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})
by the definition of products.
Since
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}
were chosen arbitrarily,
we have
{\bf A}\models\phi
.
Moreover, universal sentences are preserved by taking subalgebras.
Finally, suppose that
{\bf B}
is an algebra
that satisfies
s\approx t
, and
\mu
is a surjective homomorphism from
{\bf B}
to
some algebra
{\bf A}
. Let
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in A
. By the surjectivity
of
\mu
we can choose
b_{1},\dots,b_{n}
such that
\mu(b_{i})=a_{i}
for all
i\leq n
. Then

\displaystyle s^{{\bf B}}(b_{1},\dots,b_{n})=t^{{\bf B}}(b_{1},\dots,b_{n})\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\quad\mu(s^{{\bf B}}(b_{1},\dots,b_{n}))=\mu(t^{{\bf B}}(b_{1},\dots,b_{n}))

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\quad t^{{\bf A}}(\mu(b_{1}),\dots,\mu(b_{n}))=s^{{\bf A}}(\mu(b_{1}),\dots,\mu(b_{n}))

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\quad t^{{\bf A}}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})=s^{{\bf A}}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})\;.

We only show the implication
from 1. to 3. (and hence to 2.) if
{\bf A}
and
{\bf B}
have finite domains and
{\mathcal{K}}=\{{\bf B}\}
; the proof of the general case is similar (see Exercise
153
).
Let
a_{1},\dots,a_{k}
be the elements
of
{\bf A}
,
define
m:=|B|^{k}
and
C:=B^{k}
. Let
c^{1},\dots,c^{m}
be the elements of
C
; write

c_{i}
for
(c^{1}_{i},\dots,c^{m}_{i})
.
Let
{\bf S}
be the smallest subalgebra of
{\bf B}^{m}
that contains

c_{1},\dots,c_{k}
; so the
elements of
{\bf S}
are precisely those of the form

t^{{\bf B}^{m}}(c_{1},\dots,c_{k})
, for
a
k
-ary
\tau
-term
t
. See Figure
14
.

Figure 14:
Illustration for the proof of Birkhoff’s theorem

.

Define
\mu\colon S\to A
by

\mu(t^{{\bf B}^{m}}(c_{1},\dots,c_{k})):=t^{{\bf A}}(a_{1},\dots,a_{k}).

Claim 1:

\mu
is well-defined.
Suppose that

t^{{\bf B}^{m}}(c_{1},\dots,c_{k})=s^{{\bf B}^{m}}(c_{1},\dots,c_{k})
; then
t^{\bf B}=s^{{\bf B}}
by the choice of
S
, and by assumption we have
t^{{\bf A}}(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})=s^{{\bf A}}(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})
.

Claim 2:

\mu
is surjective.
For all
i\leq k
, the element
c_{i}
is mapped to
a_{i}
.

Claim 3:

\mu
is a homomorphism from
{\bf S}
to
{\bf A}
.
Let
f\in\tau
be of arity
n
and let
s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\in S
. For
i\leq n
, write
s_{i}=t_{i}^{{\bf S}}(c_{1},\dots,c_{k})
for
some
\tau
-term
t_{i}
(see Exercise
149
). Then

\displaystyle\mu\big(f^{\bf S}(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})\big)=

\displaystyle\,\mu\big(f^{{\bf S}}(t_{1}^{\bf S}(c_{1},\ldots,c_{k}),\ldots,t_{n}^{\bf S}(c_{1},\ldots,c_{k}))\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\mu\big(f^{\bf S}(t_{1}^{\bf S},\ldots,t_{n}^{\bf S})(c_{1},\ldots,c_{k})\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\mu\big((f(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}))^{\bf S}(c_{1},\ldots,c_{k})\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\big(f(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\big)^{\bf A}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,f^{\bf A}\big(t_{1}^{\bf A}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}),\ldots,t_{n}^{\bf A}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,f^{\bf A}(\mu(s_{1}),\ldots,\mu(s_{n})).

Therefore,
{\bf A}
is the homomorphic image of the subalgebra
{\bf S}
of
{{\bf B}}^{m}
, and so
{\bf A}\in\HSPfin({\bf B})
.
∎

Theorem
38.6
is
important for analysing the constraint satisfaction problem for a structure
\mathfrak{B}
, since it can be used to transform the ‘negative’
statement of not interpreting certain finite structures, which is equivalent to not having a certain finite algebra in the pseudo-variety generated by a polymorphism algebra of
\mathfrak{B}
, into a ‘positive’
statement of having polymorphisms satisfying non-trivial identities. We will learn several concrete identities that must be satisfied in later sections, e.g., in Section
43.5
, Section
44.2
, Section
63.2
, and Section
63.3
.

38.3.1
The Free Algebra

In the following we extract an
important idea from the proof of Birkhoff’s theorem and present it in different words which will be useful later. Fix a functional signature
\tau
and a class of

\tau
-algebras
{\mathcal{K}}
.

Definition 38.7
.

Let
{\bf F}
be a
\tau
-algebra generated by
X\subseteq F
. We say that
{\bf F}
has the
universal mapping property

for
{\mathcal{K}}
over
X
if
for every
{\bf A}\in{\mathcal{K}}
and
f\colon X\to A
there exists a (unique) extension of
f
to a homomorphism from
{\bf F}
to
{\bf A}
.

Proposition 38.8

(Uniqueness)
.

Suppose that

{\bf F}_{1},{\bf F}_{2}\in{\mathcal{K}}

have the universal mapping property for
{\mathcal{K}}
over
X_{i}
, for
i\in\{1,2\}
. If
|X_{1}|=|X_{2}|
then

{\bf F}_{1}
and
{\bf F}_{2}
are isomorphic.

Proof.

Fix any bijection between
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
; the bijection has a unique extension to an isomorphism between
{\bf F}_{1}
and
{\bf F}_{2}
.
∎

Proposition 38.9

(Existence)
.

For every
class
{\mathcal{K}}

of
\tau
-algebras and
and for every set
X
there
exists a
\tau
-algebra
{\bf F}\in\SP({\mathcal{K}})
which has the universal mapping property for

\HSP({\mathcal{K}})
over
X
.

Definition 38.10

(Free algebra)
.

The up to isomorphism unique
algebra
{\bf F}\in\SP({\mathcal{K}})
with the universal mapping property for
\HSP({\mathcal{K}})
over
X
is called
the
free algebra for
{\mathcal{K}}
over
X
, and will be denoted by
{\bf F}_{\mathcal{K}}(X)
.

Lemma 38.11
.

Let
{\bf F}
be free for
{\mathcal{K}}

over
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
and let
s(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
,
t(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
be
\tau
-terms. Then the following are equivalent.

1.

{\mathcal{K}}\models s(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})\approx t(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})

2.

s(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})\approx t(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
holds in
{\bf F}
;

3.

s^{{\bf F}}=t^{{\bf F}}
;

4.

s^{{\bf F}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=t^{{\bf F}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
.

Proof.

1.\Rightarrow 2.:

If
s\approx t
holds in every algebra of
{\mathcal{K}}
, then it also holds in products and subalgebras of algebras in
{\mathcal{K}}
, and hence also in
{\bf F}
.

2.\Rightarrow 3.
and
3.\Rightarrow 4.
hold trivially.

4.\Rightarrow 1
.
Let
{\bf A}\in{\mathcal{K}}
.
If
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in A
, then the map that sends
x_{i}
to
a_{i}
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
can be extended to a homomorphism from
{\bf F}
to
{\bf A}
, and
since
s^{{\bf F}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=t^{{\bf F}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
we have
s^{{\bf A}}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})=t^{{\bf A}}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})
. Since
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in A
were chosen arbitrarily, this shows that
{\bf A}\models s(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})\approx t(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
.
∎

Remark 38.12
.

Note that if
{\mathcal{K}}:=\HSP({\bf B})
, and
B
and
X
are finite, then
{\bf F}_{\mathcal{K}}(X)\leq{\bf B}^{B^{X}}
is finite as well.

38.3.2
Equational Theories

Birkhoff’s theorem provides for every class
\mathcal{K}
of
\tau
-algebras a characterisation of the class of all
\tau
-algebras that satisfies all identities satisfied by
{\mathcal{K}}
. Conversely, there is for every set of
\tau
-identities
\Sigma
a syntactic characterisation of the set of all
\tau
-identities that are satisfied in all algebras that satisfy
\Sigma
(i.e., we have a proof-theoretic characterisation of the set of all universal conjunctive consequences of
\Sigma
; the this can be seen as a special case of the completeness theorem of first-order logic for universally conjunctive sentences in an algebraic signature
\tau
).

Theorem 38.13
.

A
\tau
-identity
f\approx g
is implied by

\Sigma
(i.e., holds in all algebras that satisfy
\Sigma
) if and only if
(f,g)
is contained in the smallest equivalence relation
E
which

•

contains
(r,s)
for every
(r\approx s)\in\Sigma
,

•

is compatible: if
f\in\tau
is a function symbol of arity
n
and
(r_{1},s_{1}),\dots,(r_{n},s_{n})\in E
,
then
(f(r_{1},\dots,r_{n}),f(s_{1},\dots,s_{n}))\in E
,

•

is fully invariant: if
(r(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}),s(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}))\in E
and

t_{1},\dots,t_{n}
are
\tau
-terms, then

(r(t_{1},\dots,t_{n}),s(t_{1},\dots,t_{n}))\in E
.

Exercices.

152.

Prove Proposition
38.9
.

153.

Show the implication from 1. to 2. in Birkhoff’s theorem in full generality.

38.4
Abstract Clones

Clones (in the literature often
abstract clones
) relate to operation clones in the same way as (abstract) groups relate to permutation groups: the elements of a clone correspond to the operation of an operation clone, and the signature contains composition symbols to code how operations compose. Since an operation clone
contains operations of various arities, a clone will be formalized
as a multi-sorted structure, with a sort for each arity.

Definition 38.14
.

An (abstract)
clone

{\bf C}
is a multi-sorted structure with sorts
\{C^{(i)}\;|\;i\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}\}
and the
signature
\{\pi_{i}^{k}\;|\;1\leq i\leq k\}\cup\{\comp^{k}_{l}\;|\;k,l\geq 1\}
. The elements of the sort
C^{(k)}
will be called the
k
-ary operations
of
{\bf C}
.
We denote a clone by

{\bf C}=(C^{(1)},C^{(2)},\dots;(\pi_{i}^{k})_{1\leq i\leq k},(\comp^{k}_{l})_{k,l\geq 1})

and require that

\pi_{i}^{k}
is a constant in
C^{(k)}
,
and that

\comp_{l}^{k}\colon C^{(k)}\times(C^{(l)})^{k}\to C^{(l)}
is an
operation of arity
k+1
.
Moreover, it holds that

\displaystyle\comp_{k}^{k}(f,\pi_{1}^{k},\dots,\pi_{k}^{k})

\displaystyle=f

(20)

\displaystyle\comp^{k}_{l}(\pi_{i}^{k},f_{1},\dots,f_{k})

\displaystyle=f_{i}

(21)

\displaystyle\comp_{l}^{k}\big(f,\comp_{l}^{m}(g_{1},h_{1},\dots,h_{m}),\dots,\comp_{l}^{m}(g_{k},h_{1},\dots,h_{m})\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\comp^{m}_{l}\big(\comp^{k}_{m}(f,g_{1},\dots,g_{k}),h_{1},\dots,h_{m}\big)

\displaystyle\,.

(22)

The final equation generalises associativity in groups and monoids, and we therefore refer to it by
associativity
.
We also write
f(g_{1},\dots,g_{k})
instead
of
\comp^{k}_{l}(f,g_{1},\dots,g_{k})
when

l
is clear from the context.
So associativity might be more readable as

f(g_{1}(h_{1},\dots,h_{m}),\dots,g_{k}(h_{1},\dots,h_{m}))=f(g_{1},\dots,g_{k})(h_{1},\dots,h_{m})\,.

Every operation clone
{\mathscr{C}}
gives rise to an abstract
clone
{\bf C}
in the obvious way:
\pi^{k}_{i}\in C^{(k)}
denotes the

k
-ary
i
-th projection in
{\mathscr{C}}
,
and
\comp^{k}_{l}(f,g_{1},\dots,g_{k})\in C^{(l)}
denotes
the composed operation
(x_{1},\dots,x_{l})\mapsto f(g_{1}(x_{1},\dots,x_{l}),\dots,g_{k}(x_{1},\dots,x_{l}))\in{\mathscr{C}}
.
Conversely, every abstract clone arises from an operation clone - this will follow from Proposition
42.3
.

Example 39
.

An algebra
{\bf A}
satisfies

f(x_{1},x_{2})\approx f(x_{2},x_{1})
if and only if

\displaystyle\Clo({\bf A})\models\comp^{2}_{2}(f^{\bf A},\pi_{1}^{2},\pi_{2}^{2})=\comp^{2}_{2}(f^{\bf A},\pi_{2}^{2},\pi_{1}^{2}).

In the following, we will also use the term ‘abstract clone’ in situations where we want to stress that we are
working with a clone and
not
with an operation clone.
The notion of a
homomorphism
between clones is just the usual notion of homomorphisms for algebras, adapted to the multi-sorted case. Since we didn’t formally introduce homomorphisms for multi-sorted structures, we spell out the definition in the special case of clones.

Definition 39.1
.

Let
{\bf C}
and
{\bf D}
be clones. A function

\xi\colon C\to D
is called a
(clone) homomorphism
if

1.

\xi
preserves arities of operations, i.e.,
\xi(C^{(i)})\subseteq D^{(i)}
for all
i\in{\mathbb{N}}
;

2.

\xi((\pi^{k}_{i})^{{\bf C}})=(\pi^{k}_{i})^{{\bf D}}

for all
1\leq i\leq k
;

3.

\xi(f(g_{1},\dots,g_{n}))=\xi(f)(\xi(g_{1}),\dots,\xi(g_{n}))
for all
n,m\geq 1
,
f\in C^{(n)}
,

g_{1},\dots,g_{n}\in C^{(m)}
.

We say that
\xi
is a
(clone)
isomorphism
if
\xi
is bijective and
both
\xi
and
\xi^{-1}
is a homomorphism.

Example 40
.

We write
\Proj
for the abstract clone
of an algebra with at least two elements all of whose operations are projections; note that any such algebra has the same abstract clone (up to isomorphism), and that
\Proj
has a homomorphism into any other clone.

Example 41
.

All abstract clones of an algebra on a one-element set are isomorphic, too, but of course not isomorphic to
\Proj
.
Any clone homomorphically maps to this trivial clone.

Example 42
.

Using Proposition
23.9
, it is easy to see that
there exists a clone homomorphism from
\Pol(K_{3})
to
\Proj
.

The following definition plays an important role throughout the later sections in this text.

Definition 42.1

(Star composition)
.

Let
l,m\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
n=lm
.
We write
f*g
as a shortcut for

\displaystyle\comp^{l}_{n}(f,\comp^{m}_{n}(g,\pi^{n}_{1},\dots,\pi^{n}_{m}),\dots,\comp^{m}_{n}(g,\pi^{n}_{(l-1)m+1},\dots,\pi^{n}_{n}))\,.

Note that if
f\colon A^{l}\to A

and
g\colon A^{m}\to A
, then

f*g
denotes the operation from

A^{lm}\to A
given by

(x_{1,1},\dots,x_{l,m})\mapsto f\big(g(x_{1,1},\dots,x_{1,m}),\dots,g(x_{l,1},\dots,x_{l,m})\big).

42.1
Clone Formulation of Birkhoff’s Theorem

One can translate back and forth between varieties and abstract clones.

Definition 42.2

(
\Var({\bf C})
)
.

For any abstract clone
{\bf C}
, the variety
\Var({\bf C})
is defined as follows.
We use the elements of
C
as a functional signature
\tau
, where the elements of
C^{(n)}
are
n
-ary function symbols.
We consider the set
\Sigma
of
\tau
-identities defined as follows. If
f\in C^{(k)}
and
g_{0},g_{1},\dots,g_{k}\in C^{(m)}
are such that

{\bf C}\models(g_{0}=\comp^{k}_{m}(f,g_{1},\dots,g_{k}))
, then
we add the identity

g_{0}(y_{1},\dots,y_{m})\approx f(g_{1}(y_{1},\dots,y_{m}),\dots,g_{k}(y_{1},\dots,y_{m}))
to
\Sigma
.
Moreover, we add the identities

\pi^{n}_{i}(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})\approx y_{i}
to
\Sigma
.
Then
\Var({\bf C})
denotes the class of
\tau
-algebras that satisfy
\Sigma
.

Conversely, to every variety
\mathcal{V}

we may associate the clone
\Clo({\mathcal{V}}):=\Clo({\bf F}_{\mathcal{V}}(\{x_{1},x_{2},\dots\}))
of the algebra

{\bf F}_{\mathcal{V}}(\{x_{1},x_{2},\dots\})
which is free for
{\mathcal{V}}
over countably many generators.

Proposition 42.3
.

Let
{\bf C}
be an abstract clone.
Then
\Clo(\Var({\bf C}))
is isomorphic to
{\bf C}
.

Proof.

Let
{\mathcal{V}}:=\Var({\bf C})
, and let
\Sigma
be the set of
\tau
-identities that defines
{\mathcal{V}}
.
Let
{\bf F}:={\bf F}_{\mathcal{V}}(\{x_{1},x_{2},\dots\})
and let
{\bf D}:=\Clo({\bf F})
.

Claim 1.
The map
\xi
that sends
f\in C^{(n)}
to
f^{{\bf F}}\in D^{(n)}
is a clone homomorphism
{\bf C}\to{\bf D}
:

•

It clearly preserves arities.

•

If
i\in[n]
then
\Sigma
contains

\pi^{n}_{i}(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})\approx y_{i}
.
Since
{\bf F}\models\Sigma

we have
(\pi^{n}_{i})^{{\bf F}}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})=a_{i}
for all
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in F
.
Hence,
\xi((\pi^{n}_{i})^{{\bf C}})=(\pi^{n}_{i})^{{\bf F}}=(\pi^{n}_{i})^{{\bf D}}
.

•

If
n,m\geq 1
,
f\in C^{(n)}
,
g_{1},\dots,g_{n}\in C^{(m)}
, and

g_{0}=f(g_{1},\dots,g_{n})
, then

\Sigma
contains

g_{0}(y_{1},\dots,y_{m})\approx f(g_{1}(y_{1},\dots,y_{m}),\dots,g_{n}(y_{1},\dots,y_{m}))
, and
since
{\bf F}\models\Sigma

it follows that

\xi(g_{0})=\xi(f)(\xi(g_{1}),\dots,\xi(g_{n}))
.

Claim 2.

\xi
is surjective. Every element of
D
is of the form
t^{{\bf F}}
, for some
\tau
-term
t
. It can be shown by induction over the term structure that there exists
s\in\tau
such that
s^{{\bf F}}=t^{{\bf F}}
, and hence
\xi(s)=t^{{\bf F}}
.

Claim 3.

\xi
is injective.
Suppose that
\xi(f)=\xi(g)
for some
f,g\in C^{(n)}
. Then

f^{{\bf F}}=g^{{\bf F}}
and hence
{\bf F}\models f(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})\approx g(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
.
By Lemma
38.11
,
f(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})\approx g(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
holds in all algebras of

{\mathcal{V}}
.
By Theorem
38.13
we have that
(f(y_{1},\dots,y_{n}),g(y_{1},\dots,y_{n}))
is in the smallest compatible fully invariant equivalence relation
E
that contains
\Sigma
.
We show by induction on the structure of
E
that
if there are

h\in\tau
and
\tau
-terms
t_{1},\dots,t_{k},s
with variables from
y_{1},\dots,y_{m}
such that
E
contains

(s,h(t_{1},\dots,t_{k}))

and
\xi(q)=s,\xi(p_{1})=t_{1}^{{\bf F}},\dots,\xi(p_{k})=t_{k}^{{\bf F}}
,
then

q=\comp^{k}_{m}(h,p_{1},\dots,p_{k}).

In particular, if
t_{1}=y_{1},\dots,t_{k}=y_{k}
, and
s=h^{\prime}(y_{1},\dots,y_{k})
, so that
E
in fact contains
(h^{\prime}(y_{1},\dots,y_{k}),h(y_{1},\dots,y_{k}))
,
then

h^{\prime}=\comp^{k}_{m}(h,\pi^{k}_{1},\dots,\pi^{k}_{k})=h
,
because
\xi(\pi^{n}_{i})=(y_{i})^{{\bf F}}
.

•

If

s
is of the form
g_{0}(y_{1},\dots,y_{m})
and

t_{i}
is of the form
g_{i}(y_{1},\dots,y_{m})

for each
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, then the statement is true by the definition of
\Sigma
.

•

If
s
is of the form
h(s_{1},\dots,s_{k})
for

\tau
-terms
s_{1},\dots,s_{k}
,
and
(s_{1},t_{1}),\dots,(s_{k},t_{k})\in E
, then
choose
q_{1},\dots,q_{k}
such that

\xi(q_{i})=s_{i}^{{\bf F}}
, which exist by the surjectivity of
\xi
.
We further distinguish for each
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
whether
t_{i}
or
s_{i}
is a variable
of the form
h^{\prime}(r_{1},\dots,r_{u})
.
If one is of the form
h^{\prime}(r_{1},\dots,r_{u})
, then

(s_{i},t_{i})\in E
and the inductive assumption implies that
p_{i}=q_{i}
.
If both
s_{i}
and
t_{i}
are a variable, then it must be the same variable
y_{i}
,
and since
\xi(\pi^{m}_{i})=(y_{i})^{{\bf F}}
we have
that
p_{i}=\pi^{m}_{i}=q_{i}
in this case as well. Hence,
q=\comp^{k}_{m}(h,q_{1},\dots,q_{k})=\comp^{k}_{m}(h,p_{1},\dots,p_{k})
.

•

If
s
is of the form
h^{\prime}(p_{1},\dots,p_{k})

and
(h^{\prime}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}),h(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}))\in E
, then

h^{\prime}=h
by the inductive assumption.
Hence,
q=\comp^{k}_{m}(h^{\prime},p_{1},\dots,p_{k})=\comp^{k}_{m}(h,p_{1},\dots,p_{k})
.

Since
(f(y_{1},\dots,y_{n}),g(y_{1},\dots,y_{n}))\in E
, we therefore have
f=g
.
This proves the injectivity of
\xi
.
∎

Proposition
42.3
in particular shows the following, which can be seen as an analog of Cayley’s theorem for clones.

Corollary 42.4
.

Every abstract clone is isomorphic to an operation clone.

Proposition
42.3
has a converse; to state it, we need the following definition.

Definition 42.5
.

Let
{\mathcal{V}}
,
{\mathcal{W}}
be varieties with signatures
\sigma
and
\rho
, respectively.
An
interpretation
of
{\mathcal{V}}
in
{\mathcal{W}}
is a map
I
from
\sigma
to
\rho
-terms such that
{\mathcal{V}}
contains

\{I({{\bf A}})\mid{\bf A}\in{\mathcal{W}}\}

where
I({{\bf A}})
is the
\sigma
-algebra with domain
A
and the operation
I(f)^{{\bf A}}
for
f\in\sigma
.

The following lemma is straightforward from the definitions.

Lemma 42.6
.

Let
{\mathcal{V}}
and
{\mathcal{W}}
be varieties. Then there is an interpretation of
{\mathcal{V}}
in
{\mathcal{W}}
if and only if there exists a
clone homomorphism from

\Clo({\mathcal{V}})
to
\Clo({\mathcal{W}})
.

Proposition 42.7
.

Let
{\mathcal{V}}
be a variety.
Then
\Var(\Clo({\mathcal{V}}))
and
{\mathcal{V}}
mutually interpret each other.

Proof.

Let
\sigma
be the signature of
{\mathcal{V}}
and let
\rho
be the signature of
{\mathcal{W}}:=\Var(\Clo({\mathcal{V}}))
.
Let
{\bf F}:={\bf F}_{\mathcal{V}}(\{x_{1},x_{2},\dots\})
.
The identities that hold in every algebra of
{\mathcal{V}}
are precisely those that hold in
{\bf F}
by Lemma
38.11
.
Then the map that sends
f\in\sigma
of arity
k
to

f^{{\bf F}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})
, viewed as a
\rho
-term, is an interpretation of
{\mathcal{V}}
in
{\mathcal{W}}
.

Conversely, every

f\in\rho
has been introduced for an element of
{\bf F}
which equals
t^{{\bf F}}(x_{i_{1}},\dots,x_{i_{n}})
for some
i_{1},\dots,i_{n}\in{\mathbb{N}}
and some
\sigma
-term
t(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
.
The map
J
that sends
f
to
t(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})

is an interpretation of
{\mathcal{W}}
in
{\mathcal{V}}
.
∎

The following proposition links the existence of clone homomorphisms with the language of algebras, and in particular identities and (pseudo-) varieties.

Proposition 42.8
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{D}
be operation clones on finite sets.
Then the following are equivalent.

1.

There is a surjective clone
homomorphism from
{\mathscr{C}}
to
{\mathscr{D}}
;

2.

there are algebras
{\bf A}
and
{\bf B}

with the same signature
\tau
such that

\Clo({\bf A})=\mathscr{D}
,

\Clo({\bf B})=\mathscr{C}
,
and all universal conjunctive
\tau
-sentences
that hold in
{\bf B}
also hold in
{\bf A}
;

3.

there are algebras
{\bf A}
and
{\bf B}

with the same signature such that

\Clo({\bf A})=\mathscr{D}
,

\Clo({\bf B})=\mathscr{C}
,
and
{\bf A}\in\HSPfin({\bf B})
(equivalently,

{\bf A}\in\HSP({\bf B})
).

Moreover, the following are equivalent.

•

There is a clone isomorphism between
{\mathscr{C}}
and
{\mathscr{D}}
.

•

there are algebras
{\bf A}
and
{\bf B}

with the same signature such that

\Clo({\bf A})=\mathscr{D}
,

\Clo({\bf B})=\mathscr{C}
,
and
\HSPfin({\bf A})=\HSPfin({\bf B})
(equivalently:

\HSP({\bf A})=\HSP({\bf B})
).

In the study of the complexity of CSPs,
the equivalence between (1) and (3) in the above
is the most relevant, since (3) is related to
our most important tool to prove NP-hardness
of CSPs (because of the link between pseudovarieties and primitive positive interpretations from Theorem
38.4
), and since (1) is the universal-algebraic property that will be used in the following (see e.g. Theorem
43.15
below).
The following lemma is central for our applications
of abstract clones when studying the complexity of CSPs; it applies to all operation clones
{\bf F}
on a finite set.

Lemma 42.9
.

Let
{\bf C}
be a clone and let
{\bf F}
be the clone that has finitely many elements of each sort
such that there is no clone homomorphism from
{\bf C}
to
{\bf F}
.
Then there is a primitive positive sentence in the language
\tau
of (abstract) clones that
holds in
{\bf C}
but not in
{\bf F}
.

Proof.

Let
{\bf E}
be the expansion of
{\bf C}
by constant symbols such that every element
e
of
{\bf E}
is named by a constant
c_{e}
.
Let
V
be the set of atomic sentences that hold in
{\bf E}
.
Let
U
be the first-order theory of
{\bf F}
.
Suppose that
U\cup V
has
a model
{\bf M}
. There might be elements in
{\bf M}
outside of
\bigcup_{i}M^{(i)}
.
But the
\tau
-reduct of the restriction of
{\bf M}
to
\bigcup_{i}M^{(i)}
must be isomorphic
to
{\bf F}
, since each of the
M^{(i)}
is finite; we identify it with
{\bf F}
. Note that for all constants
c_{e}
we have that
c_{e}^{{\bf M}}\in{\bf F}
.
Since

{\bf M}
satisfies all atomic formulas that hold in
{\bf E}
,
we have
that the mapping
e\mapsto c_{e}^{\bf M}
, for
e
an element of
{\bf E}
,
is a homomorphism from
{\bf C}
to
{\bf F}
, in contradiction to our assumptions.

So
U\cup V
is unsatisfiable, and by compactness of first-order logic there
exists a finite subset
V^{\prime}
of
V
such that
V^{\prime}\cup U

is unsatisfiable.
Replace each of the new constant symbols in
V^{\prime}

by an existentially quantified variable;
then the conjunction of the resulting sentences from
V
is a primitive positive sentence,
and it must be false in
{\bf F}
.
∎

A set of identities
\Sigma
is called
trivial
if there exists an algebra
{\bf A}
that satisfies
\Sigma
and
\Clo({\bf A})
is isomorphic to
\Proj
.

Corollary 42.10
.

Let
{\bf A}
be an algebra. If there is no clone homomorphism from
\Clo({\bf A})
to
\Proj
, then there exists a non-trivial finite set of identities that holds in
{\bf A}
.

Remark 42.11
.

Recall from Remark
21.1
that
there are uncountably many clones on a three-element set. In fact, there are uncountably many even when considered up to homomorphic equivalence
[
30
]
.

42.2
Clone Homomorphisms and Primitive Positive Interpretations

Clone homomorphisms can be linked to pseudovarieties of algebras, and pseudo-varieties of polymorphism algebras can be linked to primitive positive interpretations; in this section, we present shortcuts that directly link the existence of clone homomorphisms of polymorphism clones with primitive positive interpretations. The proofs will be merely combinations of previous results, but the combinations are often easier to cite and this will be convenient later in the text.

Corollary 42.12
.

A finite structure
\mathfrak{A}
has a primitive positive interpretation in a finite structure
\mathfrak{B}

if and only if there exists a clone homomorphism from
\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
to
\Pol(\mathfrak{A})
.

Proof.

The proof is a straightforward combination of Theorem
38.4
with Proposition
42.8
.
Let
{\bf B}
be a polymorphism algebra of
\mathfrak{B}
.
If
\mathfrak{A}
has a primitive positive interpretation in
\mathfrak{B}
then by Theorem
38.4

there exists
{\bf A}\in\HSPfin({\mathfrak{B}})
such that

\Clo({\bf A})\subseteq\Pol(\mathfrak{A})
.
Then Proposition
42.8

implies that there exists a surjective clone homomorphism from
\Clo({\bf B})
to
\Clo({\bf A})
,
which is a clone homomorphism from
\Pol(\mathfrak{B})

to
\Pol(\mathfrak{A})
. Conversely,
suppose that there exists a clone homomorphism from
\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
to
\Pol(\mathfrak{A})
. Let
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\Pol(\mathfrak{A})
be the image of this clone homomorphism.
Then
by Proposition
42.8

there are algebras
{\bf A}
and
{\bf B}
with the same signature such that
\Clo({\bf A})={\mathcal{C}}
,

\Clo({\bf B})=\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
,
and
{\bf A}\in\HSPfin({\bf B})
.
This in turn means that
\mathfrak{A}
has a primitive positive interpretation in
\mathfrak{B}
by Theorem
38.4
.
∎

Corollary 42.13
.

Two finite structures
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
are primitively positively bi-interpretable if and only if
\Pol(\mathfrak{A})
and
\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
are isomorphic as abstract clones.

Proof.

Combine Proposition
38.5
and the second part of Proposition
42.8
.
∎

42.3
Hardness from Factors

An algebra is called
idempotent
if all of its operations are idempotent.
For idempotent algebras
{\bf A}

there is another
characterisation for the existence of a clone homomorphism to
\Proj

by Bulatov and Jeavons
[
37
, Proposition 4.14]
(Corollary
42.15
below). We present a slightly strengthened version of their result by Zhuk
[
99
, Lemma 4.2]
.

Theorem 42.14
.

Let
{\bf B}
be an idempotent algebra
and suppose that
{\bf A}\in\HSPfin({\bf B})
has at least two elements. Then
\HS({\bf B})
contains
a subalgebra
{\bf A}^{\prime}
of
{\bf A}
with at least two elements.

Proof.

Suppose that
{\bf C}\in S({\bf B}^{d})
for some
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
has a congruence
K

such that
{\bf A}:={\bf C}/K

has at least two elements. We show the statement by induction on
d
. For
d=1
there is nothing to be shown because we can choose
{\bf A}^{\prime}:={\bf A}
.
If for any two equivalence classes
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
of
K
the intersection
\pi_{1}(E_{1})\cap\pi_{1}(E_{2})
is empty, then let

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle:=\pi_{1}(C)

\displaystyle K^{\prime}

\displaystyle:=\{(\pi_{1}(a),\pi_{1}(b))\mid(a,b)\in K\}.

Then
C^{\prime}
is the universe of a subalgebra
{\bf C}^{\prime}
of
{\bf C}
,

K^{\prime}
is a congruence of
{\bf C}^{\prime}
, and
{\bf C}^{\prime}/K^{\prime}
is isomorphic to
{\bf C}/K={\bf A}
. Again, we have
{\bf A}\in\HS(\mathfrak{B})
.

Now suppose that
K
has two equivalence classes
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
such that
\pi_{1}(E_{1})\cap\pi_{1}(E_{2})\neq\emptyset
. Let
a\in\pi_{1}(E_{1})\cap\pi_{1}(E_{2})
and define

\displaystyle C^{\prime}

\displaystyle:=\pi_{\{2,\dots,n\}}(S\cap(\{a\}\times A^{d-1})

\displaystyle K^{\prime}

\displaystyle:=\{((b_{2},\dots,b_{d}),(c_{2},\dots,c_{d}))\mid((a,b_{2},\dots,b_{d}),(a,c_{2},\dots,c_{d}))\in K\}.

Since
{\bf B}
and
{\bf C}
are idempotent,
the set
C^{\prime}
is the universe of a subalgebra
{\bf C}^{\prime}
of
{\bf C}
, and
K^{\prime}
is a congruence of
{\bf C}^{\prime}
.
The algebra
{\bf C}^{\prime}/K^{\prime}
has at least two elements and is isomorphic to a subalgebra of
{\bf C}/K={\bf A}
.
Thus, the statement follows from the inductive assumption.
∎

Corollary 42.15
.

Let
{\bf B}
be an idempotent algebra.
Then
\HSPfin({\bf B})
contains
an algebra with at least two elements all of whose operations are projections if and only if
\HS(\mathfrak{B})
does.

Proof.

If all operations
{\bf A}
of an algebra are projections, then the same applies to all subalgebras of
{\bf A}
. Therefore the statement follows from Theorem
42.14
.
∎

Since the size of the algebras in
\HS({\bf B})
is bounded by the size of
\bf B
, this leads to an algorithm
that decides whether a given finite structure
\mathfrak{B}
satisfies
the equivalent conditions in Theorem
43.15
. We summarise various equivalent conditions for finite idempotent algebras that were treated in this chapter.

Corollary 42.16
.

Let
{\bf B}
be a finite idempotent algebra. Then the following are equivalent.

1.

There is no homomorphism from
\Clo({\bf B})
to
\Proj
.

2.

{\bf B}
satisfies some non-trivial finite set of identities.

3.

\HSP({\bf B})
does not contain an at least 2-element algebra all of whose operations are projections.

4.

\HS({\bf B})
does not contain an at least 2-element algebra all of whose operations are projections.

Proof.

The equivalence of (
1
)
and (
2
) follows from Corollary
42.10
.
The equivalence of (
1
) and (
3
) follows from
Proposition
42.8
.
The equivalence of
(
3
) and (
41
)
follows from Theorem
38.6
combined with Theorem
42.14
.
∎

Exercices.

154.

Let
{\bf B}
be an idempotent algebra such that
\HSPfin({\bf B})
contains an affine algebra

with at least two elements.
Then
\HS({\bf B})
contains an affine algebra

with at least two elements.

43
Minions

(Abstract) minions generalise (abstract) clones,
and function minions generalise operation clones.
The name has been introduced in 2018
when it became clear that function minions over finite domains capture the complexity of so-called
promise CSPs
, which generalise CSPs
[
8
]
.
This text does not cover promise CSPs; however, we still introduce minions, because minion homomorphisms play an important role when studying clones as well (see, e.g.,
[
16
,
28
,
30
]
). In particular, minion homomorphisms can be used to characterise
the operator
\HI
from the second formulation of the tractability theorem, Theorem
20.1
.

43.1
Minors and Minions

Let
A,B
be sets and
k,l\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\geq 1}
. Let
f\colon A^{k}\to B
be a function and let
\alpha\colon[k]\to[m]
. Then
f_{\alpha}
denotes the function
g\colon A^{m}\to B
given by
g(x_{1},\dots,x_{m}):=f(x_{\alpha(1)},\dots,x_{\alpha(k)})
.
A
minor
of
f
is a function of the form
f_{\alpha}
, for some
\alpha\colon[k]\to[m]
.
Note that

•

if
\alpha\colon[k]\to[m]
and
\beta\colon[m]\to[n]
,
then

(f_{\alpha})_{\beta}=f_{\beta\circ\alpha},

so the minor relation is transitive.

•

if
\alpha\colon[k]\to[m]

then
(\pi^{k}_{i})_{\alpha}=\pi^{m}_{\alpha(i)}
.

•

if
f
and
g
are idempotent operations on
A
, then
for all
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k}\colon[m]\to[n]
there exists
\beta\colon[km]\to[n]
such that

\displaystyle f(g_{\alpha_{1}},\dots,g_{\alpha_{k}})

\displaystyle=(f*g)_{\beta}

(recall Definition
42.1
).

Definition 43.1
.

A
function minion
is a subset
{\mathscr{M}}
of
\bigcup_{k\geq 1}B^{A^{k}}
, where
A
and
B
be sets, which is closed under taking minors.

Note that every operation clone is a function minion where
A=B
and where we additionally require the presence of the projections and closure under composition.
A
minion
is the abstract version of a function minion, analogously as clones can be viewed as the abstract version of operation clones.

Definition 43.2
.

An
(abstract) minion
is a multi-sorted algebra
{{\bf M}}
with sorts
M^{(1)},M^{(2)},\dots
and for each
\alpha\colon[n]\to[m]
the operation
{}_{\alpha}\colon M^{(n)}\to M^{(m)}
such that
for every
\sigma\colon[n]\to[m]
and
\rho\colon[m]\to[k]
and
f\in M^{(n)}

(f_{\sigma})_{\rho}=f_{\rho\circ\sigma}.

Clearly, every function minion gives rise to a minion in the obvious way.
This statement has a converse, which is analogous to Cayley’s theorem for groups (see Proposition
42.3
for the corresponding statement for clones);
see Exercise
160
.

Definition 43.3
.

Let
{\bf M}
and
{\bf N}
be minions.

•

A
minion homomorphism
from
{\bf M}
to
{\bf N}
is a map
\xi\colon M\to N
such that for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
f\in M^{(n)}
we have
\xi(f)\in D^{(n)}
and for every
m,n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\alpha\colon[n]\to[m]
and
f\in M^{(n)}
we have
\xi(f_{\alpha})=\xi(f)_{\alpha}
.

•

A
minion isomorphism
is a bijective minion homomorphism
\xi\colon{\bf M}\to{\bf N}
such that
\xi^{-1}
is a minion homomorphism as well. In this case,
{\bf M}
and
{\bf N}
are called
isomorphic
.

Similarly as for clones in Lemma
42.9
, we may apply the compactness theorem of first-order logic to characterise the existence of minion homomorphisms to function minions on finite sets.

Lemma 43.4
.

Let
{\bf M}
be a minion and let
{\bf F}
be a minion with finitely many elements of each sort.
If there is no minion homomorphism from
{\bf M}
to
{\bf F}
, then there exists a primitive positive sentence over the signature of (abstract) minions that holds in
{\bf M}
but not in
{\bf F}
.

Exercises.

155.

Let
{\bf C}
and
{\bf D}
be clones.
Show that
\xi\colon C\to D
is a minion homomorphism if and only if

•

\xi
preserves arities, i.e.,

\xi(C^{(i)})\subseteq D^{(i)}
for all
i\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and

•

\xi
preserves composition with projections, that is, for all
n,k\geq 1

and
f\in C^{(k)}

\xi(f\big((\pi^{n}_{i_{1}})^{{\bf C}},\dots,(\pi^{n}_{i_{k}})^{{\bf C}}\big))=\xi(f)\big((\pi^{n}_{i_{1}})^{{\bf D}}),\dots,(\pi^{n}_{i_{k}})^{{\bf D}}\big).

Let
\alpha\colon[k]\to[m]
. Write down a primitive positive formula

\phi(x,y)

in the language of clones such that for every operation clone
{\mathscr{C}}
and

all
f,g\in{\mathscr{C}}
we have
{\mathscr{C}}\models\phi(f,g)
if and only if
g=f_{\alpha}
.

Let
{\bf M}
be a minion. Show that for every injective
\alpha\colon[n]\to[m]

the operation
f\mapsto f_{\alpha}
from
M^{(n)}
to
M^{(m)}
is injective, and for every

surjective
\alpha\colon[n]\to[m]
the operation
f\mapsto f_{\alpha}
from
M^{(n)}
to
M^{(m)}

is surjective.

Let
Set
denote the category
of all set and mappings between them, and let

denote the category of finite ordinals and mappings between them.

•

Explain how minions can be viewed as
functors from
{\bf FinOrd}
to
{\bf Set}
. Show that natural transformations between such functors are minion homomorphisms.

•

as
Set
-endofunctors
F
which are
finitary
:
that is, if
X
is a set, then
F(X)
is the union of the sets
\{F(i)(u)\mid u\in U\}
, where
U
is a finite subset of
X
and
i\colon U\to X
is the inclusion map. Show that natural transformations between
Set
-endofunctors are minion homomorphisms.

43.2
Reflections

In Section
38.2
we have
seen that the
\HSPfin
operator is the algebraic counterpart to full
primitive positive interpretations. This section treats a relatively new universal-algebraic operator, for forming
reflections
(introduced in
[
16
]
), which can be used to characterise the structure-building operator
\HI
.
Recall from Section
19.2

that
\HI
is the operator that is most relevant for constraint satisfaction.

Definition 43.5
.

Let
{\bf B}
be a
\tau
-algebra, let
A
be a set,
and let
h\colon B\to A
and
g\colon A\to B
be two maps. Then the
reflection
of
{\bf B}
with respect to
(h,g)
is the
\tau
-algebra
{\bf A}
with domain
A
where for all
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in A

and
f\in\tau
of arity
n
we define

f^{{\bf A}}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n}):=h(f^{{\bf B}}(g(a_{1}),\dots,g(a_{n})))\,.

The class of reflections of a class of
\tau
-algebras
{\mathcal{C}}
is denoted by

\operatorname{Refl}({\mathcal{C}})
.

As for the other operators on algebras, we write

\operatorname{Refl}({\bf B})
instead of
\operatorname{Refl}(\{{\bf B}\})
.
The following is an analog to the
\HSP
-lemma (Lemma

38.3
).

Lemma 43.6

(from
[
16
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a class of
\tau
-algebras.

•

The smallest class of

\tau
-algebras that contains
\mathcal{C}
and is closed under
\operatorname{Refl}
,
\Hom
,
\operatorname{S}
, and
\operatorname{P}

equals
\RP({\mathcal{C}})
.

•

The smallest class of

\tau
-algebras that contains
\mathcal{C}
and is closed under
\operatorname{Refl}
,
\Hom
,
\operatorname{S}
, and
\Pfin

equals
\RPfin({\mathcal{C}})
.

Proof.

For the first statement, it suffices to prove that

\RP({\mathcal{C}})

is closed under
\operatorname{Refl}
,
\Hom
,
\operatorname{S}
,
\operatorname{P}
,
and for the second that
\RPfin({\mathcal{C}})

is closed under
\operatorname{Refl}
,
\Hom
,
\operatorname{S}
,

\operatorname{P}^{\fin}
.
For the operator
\operatorname{Refl}
this follows
from the simple fact that
\operatorname{Refl}\operatorname{Refl}({\mathcal{K}})=\operatorname{Refl}({\mathcal{K}})
for any class

\mathcal{K}
.

To prove that
\RP({\mathcal{C}})
and

\RPfin({\mathcal{C}})
are closed under
\Hom
,
we show that
\Hom({\mathcal{K}})\subseteq\operatorname{Refl}({\mathcal{K}})
for any class
{\mathcal{K}}
.
Let
{\bf B}\in{\mathcal{K}}
and
h\colon B\to A

be a surjective homomorphism to an algebra
{\bf A}
.
Pick any function
g
such that
h\circ g
is the identity on
A
. Then
h
and
g
witness that
{\bf A}
is a reflection of
{\bf B}
since

\displaystyle h(f^{{\bf B}}(g(x_{1}),\dots,g(x_{n}))

\displaystyle=f^{{\bf A}}(h\circ g(x_{1}),\dots,h\circ g(x_{n}))

(since
h
is a homomorphism)

\displaystyle=f^{{\bf A}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})

(by the choice of
g
).

To prove that
\RP({\mathcal{C}})
and
\RPfin({\mathcal{C}})
are closed under
\operatorname{S}
,
we show that
\operatorname{S}({\mathcal{K}})\subseteq\operatorname{Refl}({\mathcal{K}})
for any class
{\mathcal{K}}
.
Let
{\bf B}\in{\mathcal{K}}
and suppose that
{\bf A}
is a subalgebra of
{\bf B}
. Let
g\colon A\to B
be the identity on
A
, and
h\colon B\to A
be any
extension of
g
to
B
. Then
h
and
g
show that
{\bf A}
is a reflection of
{\bf B}
since

h(f^{{\bf B}}(g(x_{1}),\dots,g(x_{n}))=f^{{\bf B}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=f^{{\bf A}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\,.

Let
I
be an arbitrary set,
({\bf B}_{i})_{i\in I}
be algebras from
\operatorname{P}({\mathcal{C}})
, and suppose that
{\bf A}_{i}
is a reflection of
{\bf B}_{i}
for every
i\in I
, witnessed by functions
h_{i}\colon B_{i}\to A_{i}
and
g_{i}\colon A_{i}\to B_{i}
. Then the map
h\colon\prod_{i\in I}B_{i}\to\prod_{i\in I}A_{i}
that sends

(b_{i})_{i\in I}
to
(h_{i}(b_{i}))_{i\in I}
and the map

g\colon\prod_{i\in I}A_{i}\to\prod_{i\in I}B_{i}
that sends

(a_{i})_{i\in I}
to
(g_{i}(a_{i}))_{i\in I}
witness
that
\prod_{i\in I}{\bf A}_{i}
is a reflection of
\prod_{i\in I}{\bf B}_{i}
. This shows that
\operatorname{P}(\RP({\mathcal{C}}))\subseteq\RP({\mathcal{C}})

and likewise that
\operatorname{P}^{\fin}(\RPfin({\mathcal{C}}))\subseteq\RPfin({\mathcal{C}})
.
∎

Theorem 43.7
.

Let
\mathfrak{B},\mathfrak{C}
be finite relational structures and let
{\bf C}
be a polymorphism algebra of
\mathfrak{C}
. Then

1.

\mathfrak{B}\in\Hom(\mathfrak{C}^{\prime})
for some
structure
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}
which is primitively positively definable in
\mathfrak{C}

if and only if there is an algebra
{\bf B}\in\operatorname{Refl}({\bf C})
such that
\Clo({\bf B})\subseteq\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
.

2.

\mathfrak{B}\in\HI(\mathfrak{C})
if and only if there is an algebra
{\bf B}\in\RPfin({\bf C})
such that
\Clo({\bf B})\subseteq\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
.

Proof.

To show
(1)
, first suppose that
\mathfrak{B}\in\Hom(\mathfrak{C}^{\prime})
for some
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}
which is pp definable in
\mathfrak{C}
; let
h\colon\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{B}
and
g\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}
be homomorphisms witnessing homomorphic equivalence of
\mathfrak{B}
and
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}
.
Let
{\bf C}^{\prime}
be an expansion of
{\bf C}
which is a polymorphism algebra
of
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}
.
Let
{\bf B}^{\prime}
be the reflection of
{\bf C}^{\prime}
with respect to
(h,g)
. Every operation of
\Clo({\bf B}^{\prime})
is obtained as a composition of homomorphisms, so preserves all the relations of
\mathfrak{B}
, so
\Clo({\bf B}^{\prime})\subseteq\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
. Let
{\bf B}
be the reduct of
{\bf B}^{\prime}
where we only keep the operations for the signature of
{\bf C}
, and note that
{\bf B}\in\operatorname{Refl}({\bf C})
is such that
\Clo({\bf B})\subseteq\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
.

Conversely, suppose that
the reflection
{\bf B}
of
{\bf C}
at
h\colon C\to B
and
g\colon B\to C
is such that
\Clo({\bf B})\subseteq\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
. Let
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}
be the structure
with domain
C
and the same signature as
\mathfrak{B}
which contains for every
k
-ary relation symbol
R
of
\mathfrak{B}
the relation

\displaystyle R^{\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}}:=\{

\displaystyle(f(g(b^{1}_{1}),\dots,g(b^{\ell}_{1})),\dots,f(g(b^{1}_{k}),\dots,g(b^{\ell}_{k})))

\displaystyle\mid f\in\Pol(\mathfrak{C}),(b^{1}_{1},\dots,b^{1}_{k}),\dots(b^{\ell}_{1},\dots,b^{\ell}_{k})\in R^{\mathfrak{B}}\}.

These relations are preserved by
\Pol(\mathfrak{C})
, so they are pp definable in
\mathfrak{C}
by Theorem
21.6
,
and hence
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}\in\Red(\mathfrak{C})
. Clearly,
g
is a homomorphism from
\mathfrak{B}
to
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}
. We claim that
h
is a homomorphism from
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}
to
\mathfrak{B}
. Indeed, if
b_{1},\dots,b_{k}\in B
are such that
(f(g(b_{1}),\dots,g(b_{k})))\in R^{\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}}
,
then
h(f(g(b_{1}),\dots,g(b_{k})))\in R^{\mathfrak{B}}
because
the operation
(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})\mapsto h(f(g(x_{1}),\dots,g(x_{k})))
is an operation of
{\bf B}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Refl}({\bf C})

and hence a polymorphism of
\mathfrak{B}

since
\Clo({\bf B}^{\prime})\subseteq\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
.
Thus,
\mathfrak{B}\in\Hom(\mathfrak{C}^{\prime})
.

Item
(2)
is a combination of
item
(1)
with Theorem
38.4
:
First suppose that

\mathfrak{B}\in\HI(\mathfrak{C})
.
Then there exists a structure
\mathfrak{D}\in I(\mathfrak{C})

such that
\mathfrak{B}\in\Hom(\mathfrak{D})
.
By Theorem
38.4
there is an algebra
{\bf D}\in\HSPfin({\bf C})
such that
\Clo({\bf D})\subseteq\Pol(\mathfrak{D})
, and by item
(1)
there is an
algebra
{\bf B}\in\operatorname{Refl}({\bf D})
such that
\Clo({\bf B})\subseteq\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
. This proves the statement since

\displaystyle{\bf B}\in\operatorname{Refl}({\bf D})

\displaystyle\subseteq\operatorname{Refl}\HSPfin({\bf C})

\displaystyle=\RPfin({\bf C})

(by Lemma
43.6
).

Conversely, suppose that there exists

{\bf B}\in\RPfin({\bf C})
such that
\Clo({\bf B})\subseteq\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
. Then there
exists
{\bf D}\in\Pfin({\bf C})
such that
{\bf B}\in\operatorname{Refl}({\bf D})
. Let
\mathfrak{D}
be the structure with the same domain as
{\bf D}
which contains all the relations preserved by all operations
of
\Clo({\bf D})
. Then
\mathfrak{D}\in\operatorname{I}(\mathfrak{C})
.
Moreover, by item (1) there exists
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}
which is pp definable in
\mathfrak{D}
such that

\mathfrak{B}\in\Hom(\mathfrak{D}^{\prime})
. Clearly,
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{I}(\mathfrak{C})
and hence
\mathfrak{B}\in\HI(\mathfrak{C})
.
∎

We now characterise in many different ways
the hardness condition from the second formulation of the tractability theorem (Theorem
20.1
); quite remarkably, we do not need to assume that the involved polymorphism algebra is idempotent.

Corollary 43.8
.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a structure with a finite domain and let
{\bf B}
be a polymorphism algebra of
\mathfrak{B}
. Then the following are equivalent.

1.

\HI(\mathfrak{B})
contains
K_{3}
;

2.

\HI(\mathfrak{B})
contains all finite structures;

3.

\HI(\mathfrak{B})
contains
(\{0,1\};\NAE)
;

4.

\operatorname{Refl}\Pfin({\bf B})
contains an algebra of size at least 2 all of whose operations are projections.

5.

\operatorname{Refl}\Pfin({\bf B})
contains for every finite set
A
an algebra on
A
all of whose operations are projections.

If these condition apply then
\mathfrak{B}
has a finite-signature reduct with an NP-hard
\Csp
.

Proof.

The implication from
\ref{eq:hi-k3}.
to

\ref{eq:hi-all}.
follows from the
fact that
\operatorname{I}(K_{3})
contains all finite structures (Theorem
19.3
),
and that
\operatorname{I}\HomE\operatorname{I}(\mathfrak{B})\subseteq\HI(\mathfrak{B})

by Theorem
19.12
.
The implication from
\ref{eq:hi-all}.
to
\ref{eq:hi-oit}.
is trivial.
The equivalence of
\ref{eq:hi-oit}.

and
\ref{eq:rp1}.
follows
from the fact that all polymorphisms
of
(\{0,1\};\NAE)
are essentially unary (Exercise
123
)
and Theorem
43.7
.
We leave the proof of the equivalence of

\ref{eq:rp2}.
to the reader.
The final statement follows from Corollary
19.2
.
∎

Exercises.

159.

Prove the equivalence of
5
. with the other items of Corollary
43.8
.

43.3
Birkhoff’s Theorem for Height-One Identities

A
height-one identity

is an identity
s\approx t
where the involved terms
s
and
t
have
height one
, i.e., each term involves exactly one function symbol.
Three examples of properties that
can be expressed by finite sets of height-one identities are listed below.

\displaystyle f(x,y)

\displaystyle\approx f(y,x)

(
f
is symmetric)

\displaystyle f(x,x,y)

\displaystyle\approx f(x,y,x)\approx f(y,x,x)\approx f(x,x,x)

(
f
is quasi majority)

\displaystyle f(x,x,y)

\displaystyle\approx f(y,x,x)\approx f(y,y,y)

(
f
is quasi Maltsev)

A non-example is furnished by the Maltsev identities

f(x,x,y)\approx f(y,x,x)\approx y
because the term
y
involves no function symbol. Identities where each term involves
at most
one function symbol are called
linear
; so the Maltsev identities are an example of a set of linear identities.
An example of a non-linear identity is the associativity law

f(x,f(y,z))\approx f(f(x,y),z).

If
{\bf A}
is a
\tau
-algebra, then
we write
\Minion({\bf A})
for the smallest function minion
that contains
\{f^{{\bf A}}\mid f\in\tau\}
.
If
{\bf A}
and
{\bf B}
are
\tau
-algebras
then
there exists a minion homomorphism
\xi\colon\Minion({\bf B})\to\Minion({\bf A})

that maps
f^{{\bf B}}
to
f^{{\bf A}}

if and only if for all

f,g\in\tau
of arity
k
and
l
and all
m
-ary projections
p_{1},\dots,p_{k},q_{1},\dots,q_{l}
we have that

f^{\bf A}(p_{1},\dots,p_{k})=g^{\bf A}(q_{1},\dots,q_{l})
whenever
f^{\bf B}(p_{1},\dots,p_{k})=g^{\bf B}(q_{1},\dots,q_{l})
.
If this map exists it
must be surjective and we call it the
natural minor-preserving map from

\Minion({\bf B})
to
\Minion({\bf A})
.
The following theorem is a variant of Birkhoff’s theorem (Theorem
38.6
)
for height-one identities.

Theorem 43.9

(cf. Proposition 5.3 of
[
16
]
)
.

Let
{\bf A}
and
{\bf B}
be
\tau
-algebras
such that
\Minion({\bf A})
and
\Minion({\bf B})
are
operation clones. Then the following are equivalent.

1.

The natural minor-preserving map from

\Minion({\bf B})
to
\Minion({\bf A})
exists.

2.

All height-one identities that hold in
{\bf B}
also hold in
{\bf A}
.

3.

{\bf A}\in\RP({\bf B})
.

Moreover, if
A
and
B
are finite then we can add the following to the list:

4.

{\bf A}\in\RPfin({\bf B})
.

Proof.

The equivalence of
1.
and
2.
is straightforward from the definitions, as in the proof of Theorem
38.6
.

The proof that
2.
implies
3.
is similar to the proof of Theorem
38.6
.
For every
a\in A
, let
\pi^{A}_{a}\in B^{B^{A}}

be the function that maps every tuple in
B^{A}
to its
a
-th entry. Let
{\bf S}
be the
subalgebra of
{\bf B}^{B^{A}}
generated by

\{\pi^{A}_{a}\mid a\in A\}
.
Define
h\colon S\to A
as

h(f^{{\bf B}}(\pi^{A}_{a_{1}},\dots,\pi^{A}_{a_{n}})):=f^{{\bf A}}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n}).

Similarly as in the proof of Theorem
38.6
one can show that

h
is well defined using that all height-one identities that hold in
{\bf B}
also hold in
{\bf A}
.
Note that
h
is defined on all of
S

because
\Minion({\bf B})
is an operation clone.

Let
g\colon A\to S
be the mapping which sends every
a\in A
to
\pi^{{\bf A}}_{a}
. Then
h
and
g

show that
{\bf A}\in\operatorname{Refl}({\bf S})\subseteq\operatorname{Refl}\operatorname{S}\operatorname{P}({\bf B})=\RP({\bf B})
: for all
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in A

\displaystyle f^{\bf A}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})=h(f^{{\bf B}}(g(a_{1}),\dots,g(a_{n}))).

If
A
and
B
are finite, then
B^{A}
is finite
and hence
{\bf S}\in\operatorname{S}\Pfin({\bf B})
, so the proof implies that
{\bf A}\in\RPfin({\bf B})
.

3.
implies
2
.
If
{\bf A}\in\operatorname{P}({\bf B})
then the statement follows from Theorem
38.6
. Now
suppose that
{\bf A}
is a reflection of
{\bf B}
via the maps
h\colon B\to A
and

g\colon A\to B
.
Let
\phi
be the identity
\forall x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\colon f_{1}(x_{i_{1}},\dots,x_{i_{k}})=f_{2}(x_{j_{1}},\dots,x_{j_{l}})
for
f_{1},f_{2}\in\tau

and suppose that
{\bf B}\models\phi
.
For all
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in A
we have

\displaystyle f^{{\bf A}}_{1}(a_{i_{1}},\dots,a_{i_{k}})

\displaystyle=h(f^{{\bf B}}_{1}(g(a_{i_{1}}),\dots,g(a_{i_{k}})))

\displaystyle=h(f^{{\bf B}}_{2}(g(a_{j_{1}}),\dots,g(a_{j_{l}})))=f^{{\bf A}}_{2}(a_{j_{1}},\dots,a_{j_{l}}).

Since
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}
were chosen arbitrarily, we have that
{\bf A}\models\phi
.
∎

Exercises.

160.

Prove a minion version of Cayley’s theorem: show that every minion

is isomorphic to a function minion.

161.

Let
\Sigma
be a finite set of height-one identities.

Assume that there exists an algorithm with the following properties:

•

it takes as input two finite
\tau
-structures
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
;

•

if the algorithm returns ‘no’ then
\mathfrak{A}\not\to\mathfrak{B}
;

•

it runs in polynomial time in the size of
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
;

•

if the polymorphisms of
\mathfrak{B}
satisfy
\Sigma
, and the algorithm returns ‘yes’, then
\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
.

Show that:

(a)

if
\Sigma
expresses the existence of a majority operation, then the path consistency procedure
\PC_{H}
provides an example for such an algorithm
A
(viewing the graph
H
as part of the input of
\PC_{H}
);

(b)

if there is such an algorithm
A
for
\Sigma
, then there exists a polynomial-time algorithm that decides for
a given finite
\tau
-structure
\mathfrak{B}
whether
\mathfrak{B}
has polymorphisms that satisfy
\Sigma
.

43.4
Minion Homomorphisms and Primitive Positive Constructions

We have characterised primitive positive constructions in terms of polymorphism algebras and the reflection operator, and then we have characterised varieties that are additionally closed under reflection in terms of minion homomorphisms. In this section we present straightforward combinations of these links that are elegant and convenient for later use.
The following is analogous to Corollary
42.12
for primitive positive constructions and clone homomorphisms.

Corollary 43.10
.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
and
\mathfrak{A}
be finite structures. Then

\mathfrak{A}\in\HI(\mathfrak{B})

if and only if there exists a minion homomorphism from
\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
to
\Pol(\mathfrak{A})
.

Proof.

Combine Theorem
43.7
with
Theorem
43.9
.
∎

Corollary 43.11
.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a finite structure. Then there exists a minion homomorphism from
\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
to
\Proj
if and only if

K_{3}\in\HI(\mathfrak{B})
.

Proof.

Let
{\bf B}
be an algebra such that
\Minion({\bf B})=\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
. Corollary
43.8

states that
K_{3}\in\HI(\mathfrak{B})
if and only if

\operatorname{Refl}\Pfin({\bf B})
contains an algebra of size at least 2 all of whose operations are projections, and whose clone is therefore
\Proj
.
Theorem
43.9
implies that this is equivalent to the existence of a minor-preserving map to
\Proj
.
∎

(Abstract) Clone

Corollary
42.4

A dictionary between corresponding notions.

Proposition 43.12
.

For every operation clone
\mathscr{C}
on a finite set there exists an idempotent operation clone
\mathscr{D}

on a finite set such that there exists a minion homomorphism from
\mathscr{C}
to
\mathscr{D}
and from
\mathscr{D}
to
\mathscr{C}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a structure such that
\Pol(\mathfrak{B})={\mathscr{C}}
(such a
\mathfrak{B}
exists by Proposition
21.4
). Let
\mathfrak{C}
be the core of
\mathfrak{B}
(which exists by the generalisation of Proposition
2.7
to relational structures). Let
\mathfrak{D}
be the expansion of
\mathfrak{C}
by all unary singleton relations.
We have that

\mathfrak{D}\in\HI(\mathfrak{B})
by Proposition
19.11
.
It follows from Corollary
43.10
that there exists a minion homomorphism from
{\mathscr{C}}=\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
to the idempotent clone
{\mathscr{D}}:=\Pol(\mathfrak{D})
. Conversely, we have that

{\mathscr{D}}\subseteq\Pol(\mathfrak{C})
,
and
\Pol(\mathfrak{C})
has a minion homomorphism to

\Pol(\mathfrak{B})={\mathscr{C}}
since
\mathfrak{B}\in\HomE(\mathfrak{C})
and again by Corollary
43.10
.
∎

\mathfrak{B}

.
