43.5
Taylor Terms

The following
goes back to Walter Taylor
[
96
]
.
We slightly deviate from the historic definition in that we do not require idempotence – this allows us to give stronger formulations of several results in the following.

Definition 43.13

(Taylor operations)
.

A function
f\colon B^{n}\to B
, for
n\geq 2
,
is called a
Taylor operation

if for every
i\in[n]
there are
\alpha,\beta\colon[n]\to[2]
such that
f_{\alpha}=f_{\beta}
and
\alpha(i)\neq\beta(i)
.

Examples for Taylor operations are binary commutative operations,
majority operations, and Maltsev operations. Since we do not insist on idempotence, also quasi majority operations (Exercise
63
) are examples of Taylor operations.

A
Taylor term
of a
\tau
-algebra
{\bf B}
is a
\tau
-term
t(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
, for
n\geq 2
, such that
t^{{\bf B}}
is a Taylor operation.
Note that
t
is a Taylor term
if and only if
it satisfies a set of
n
height-one identities that can be written as

t\begin{pmatrix}x&?&?&\cdots&?\\
?&x&?&&\vdots\\
\vdots&?&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\vdots&&\ddots&x&?\\
?&\cdots&\cdots&?&x\end{pmatrix}\approx t\begin{pmatrix}y&?&?&\cdots&?\\
?&y&?&&\vdots\\
\vdots&?&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\vdots&&\ddots&y&?\\
?&\cdots&\cdots&?&y\\
\end{pmatrix}

where
t
is applied row-wise and ? stands for either
x
or
y
.

Walter Taylor did not just introduce Taylor operations, but he also found a
beautiful statement about their existence (Theorem
43.15
). In the proof of this statement, we need the following important observation about the star product in idempotent algebras.

Lemma 43.14
.

For
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let
(A;f_{1},\dots,f_{n})
be an idempotent algebra.
Then there exists
g\in\Clo({\bf A})
such that
for every
f\in\{f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\}
there exists

\alpha
such that
g_{\alpha}=f
.

Proof.

The statement is clear if
n\leq 1
.
First consider the case that
n=2
.
Let
m
be the arity of
f_{1}
and let
l
be the arity of
f_{2}
.
Note that

\displaystyle\Clo({\bf A})\models

\displaystyle\;\big(f_{1}=\comp^{ml}_{m}(f_{1}*f_{2},\underbrace{\pi^{m}_{1},\dots,\pi^{m}_{1}}_{l\text{ times}},\underbrace{\pi^{m}_{2},\dots,\pi^{m}_{2}}_{l\text{ times}},\dots,\underbrace{\pi^{m}_{m},\dots,\pi^{m}_{m}}_{l\text{ times}})\big)

(23)

\displaystyle\text{ and }\Clo({\bf A})\models

\displaystyle\;\big(f_{2}=\comp^{ml}_{l}(f_{1}*f_{2},\underbrace{\pi^{l}_{1},\dots,\pi^{l}_{l}}_{m\text{ times}},\underbrace{\pi^{l}_{1},\dots,\pi^{l}_{l}}_{m\text{ times}},\dots,\underbrace{\pi^{l}_{1},\dots,\pi^{l}_{l}}_{m\text{ times}})\big)

(24)

since
{\bf A}
is idempotent. The general case can be shown easily by induction on
n
.
∎

Theorem 43.15
.

Let
{\bf B}
be an idempotent algebra. Then
the following are equivalent.

(1)

\bf B
has a Taylor term
t
.

(2)

there is no minion homomorphism
from
\Clo({\bf B})
to
{\bf Proj}
.

Proof.

To show that (1) implies (2),
suppose for contradiction that there is a minion homomorphism

\xi
from
\Clo({\bf B})
to
{\bf Proj}
.
By definition of
{\bf Proj}
we have
\xi(t^{{\bf B}})=\pi^{n}_{l}
for some
l\leq n
.
By assumption, there are
\alpha,\beta\colon[n]\to[2]
such that
(t^{{\bf B}})_{\alpha}=(t^{{\bf B}})_{\beta}
and
\alpha(l)\neq\beta(l)
.
Since
\xi(t^{{\bf B}})=\pi^{n}_{l}
and
\xi
is a minion homomorphism, we therefore obtain
that
\pi^{2}_{1}=\pi^{2}_{2}
, which does not hold in
{\bf Proj}
, a contradiction.

To show the converse implication,
suppose that
{\bf B}
does not have a Taylor term.
We have to show that
\Clo({\bf B})
has a minion homomorphism to
\Proj
.
By Lemma
43.4
, it suffices to show that every primitive positive sentence
\phi
in the language of minions that holds in
\Clo({\bf B})
also holds in
\Proj
. If
g_{1},\dots,g_{m}
are the existentially quantified variables in
\phi
, then by Lemma
43.14
there exists
g\in\Clo({\bf B})^{(n)}
, for some
n
, such that every
g_{i}
,
i\in[m]
, is a minor
g_{\alpha_{i}}
of
g
.
Hence, it suffices to define a minion homomorphism from
\Minion(B;g)
to
\Proj
.
By assumption,
g
is not a Taylor term, so there exists an argument
i

such that for all
\alpha,\beta\colon[n]\to[2]

we have
\alpha(i)=\beta(i)
or
g_{\alpha}\neq g_{\beta}
. For
\alpha\colon[n]\to[k]
, define
\xi(g_{\alpha}):=\pi^{k}_{\alpha(i)}
. This map is well-defined because if
g_{\alpha}=g_{\beta}
for
\alpha,\beta\colon[n]\to[k]
,
then
(g_{\alpha})_{\gamma}=(g_{\beta})_{\gamma}
for all
\gamma\colon[k]\to[2]
, and hence
\gamma\circ\alpha(i)=\gamma\circ\beta(i)
for all
\gamma\colon[k]\to[2]
,
which implies that
\alpha(i)=\beta(i)
. Moreover,
\xi
is a minion homomorphism because
\xi(g_{\alpha})=\pi^{k}_{\alpha(i)}=(\pi^{n}_{i})_{\alpha}=\xi(g)_{\alpha}
.
∎

Remark 43.16
.

The original statement of Taylor is
Theorem
43.15
with
clone homomorphism
instead of minion homomorphism;
the version with minion homomorphisms in Theorem
43.15
is the statement we really care about in this course and leads to an easier proof.

The following lemma should be clear from the results that we have already seen.

Lemma 43.17
.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
and
\mathfrak{C}
be homomorphically
equivalent structures.
Then
\mathfrak{B}
has a Taylor polymorphism
if and only if

\mathfrak{C}
has a Taylor polymorphism.

Proof.

Let
h
be a homomorphism from
\mathfrak{B}

to
\mathfrak{C}
, and
g
be a homomorphism from

\mathfrak{C}
to
\mathfrak{B}
.
Suppose that
f
is a Taylor polymorphism for
\mathfrak{B}
of arity
n
.
Then
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\mapsto h(f(g(x_{1}),\dots,g(x_{n})))
is a Taylor polymorphism of
\mathfrak{C}
. ∎

Corollary 43.18
.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a finite structure. Then the following are equivalent.

1.

K_{3}\notin\HI(\mathfrak{B})
.

2.

\mathfrak{B}
has a Taylor polymorphism.

3.

\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
has no minion homomorphism to
\Proj
.

If these condition don’t apply then
\mathfrak{B}
has a finite-signature reduct with an NP-hard
\Csp
.

Proof.

The equivalence of 1. and 3. is Corollary
43.11
.
Now suppose that
K_{3}\notin\HI(\mathfrak{B})
. If
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}
is the core of
\mathfrak{B}
, and
\mathfrak{C}
is the expansion of
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}
by all unary singleton relations,
then
\CC(\HH(\mathfrak{B}))\subseteq\HI(\mathfrak{B})
implies that

K_{3}
is not pp constructible in
\mathfrak{C}
.
Hence, the idempotent clone
\Pol(\mathfrak{C})
does not have a minion homomorphism to
\Proj
.
Theorem
43.15
shows that
\mathfrak{C}
and thus also
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}
must
have a Taylor polymorphism.
Lemma
43.17
implies that
\mathfrak{B}
has a Taylor polymorphism.

Note that the existence of Taylor polymorphisms is preserved by minion homomorphisms, and since
\Proj
does not have a Taylor operation we have that
2.
implies
3
.

The final statement follows from Corollary
19.2
.
∎

Theorem 43.19

(Tractability Theorem, Version 3)
.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a relational structure with finite domain and finite signature.
If
\mathfrak{B}
has a Taylor polymorphism, then
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
is in P. Otherwise,

\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
is NP-complete.

Proof.

An immediate consequence of Corollary
43.18
and Theorem
20.1
.
∎

A clone is said to be
Taylor
if it has a Taylor operation, and an algebra is called
Taylor

if it has a Taylor term operation.

Remark 43.20
.

We will from now on often use the formulation ‘let
{\bf A}
be a finite Taylor algebra’ instead of ‘let
{\bf A}
be a finite algebra such that
\Clo({\bf A})
does not have a minion homomorphism to
\Proj
’, even if we can avoid in the proofs the use of Taylor operations alltogether. The reason is that the assumption is shorter to state, and equivalent by the results of this section (see Exercise
162
).

Exercises.

162.

Show that the assumption in Theorem
43.15
that
{\bf B}
is idempotent

can be replaced
by the assumption that its domain is finite.

163.

Show that if
{\bf A}
is a finite idempotent algebra such that
\HS({\bf A})

does not

contain an affine algebra with at least two elements, then
{\bf A}
has a Taylor term.

Hint.
Use Exercise
154
.

43.6
Arc-consistency Revisited

In this section we revisit the arc-consistency procedure from Section
5
, generalised to arbitrary relational structures with finite domain and finite signature, in the light of minions and primitive positive constructions.

Definition 43.21
.

The minion
{\bf M}_{\text{AC}}
is defined as follows.
For
n\geq 1
, the set
M_{\text{AC}}^{(n)}
consists of the set of all non-empty subsets of
\{1,\dots,n\}
.
For
\alpha\colon[n]\to[m]
and
f\in M_{\text{AC}}^{(n)}
, we define

f_{\alpha}
to be
\big\{\alpha(a)\mid a\in f\}
.

Note that
{\bf M}_{\text{AC}}
is isomorphic to

\Pol(\{0,1\};\{0\},\{1\},\{0,1\}^{3}\setminus\{1,1,0\})
(see Exercise
125
).

Definition 43.22

(
\AC_{\mathfrak{B}}
)
.

For a structure
\mathfrak{B}
with finite domain and finite relational signature
\tau
,
we write
\AC_{\mathfrak{B}}
for the generalisation
of the arc-consistency procedure
\AC_{H}
from digraphs
H
to general relational structures
\mathfrak{B}
(Exercise
86
).

We also generalise the concept of
arc-consistency
for digraphs form Section
5
.

Definition 43.23

(Arc-consistency)
.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a structure with the relational signature
\tau
.
A finite
\tau
-structure
\mathfrak{A}
together with a set
L(a)\subseteq B
for every
a\in A
is called
arc-consistent (with respect to
\mathfrak{B}
)
if for every

R\in\tau
of arity
k
,
(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})\in R^{\mathfrak{A}}
,
i\in[k]
,
b_{i}\in L(a_{i})
, and
j\in[k]\setminus\{i\}
there exists

b_{j}\in L(a_{j})
such that
(b_{1},\dots,b_{k})\in R^{\mathfrak{B}}
.

Note that for any two finite
\tau
-structures
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
, if
L(a)\subseteq B
is the list computed for
a\in A
by
\AC_{\mathfrak{B}}
at the final stage of the algorithm,
then
\mathfrak{A}
together with these sets
L(a)
is arc-consistent.

Theorem 43.24
.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a finite structure. Then the following are equivalent.

1.

\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
is solved by
\AC_{\mathfrak{B}}
.

2.

\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
has totally symmetric polymorphisms of all arities.

3.

{\bf M}_{\AC}
has a minion homomorphism to
\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
.

4.

\mathfrak{B}
has a primitive positive construction in
(\{0,1\};\{0\},\{1\},\{0,1\}^{3}\setminus\{(1,1,0)\}
.

Proof.

1\Leftrightarrow 2
was already shown in Theorem
7.1
.

2\Rightarrow 3
: let
s_{k}\in\Pol(\mathfrak{B})

be a totally symmetric operation of arity
k
.
Then the map which sends for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
k\leq n
the element

\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\}\in M^{(n)}_{\text{AC}}
to the operation

(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\mapsto s_{k}(x_{i_{1}},\dots,x_{i_{k}})

is a minion homomorphism
\xi\colon{\bf M}_{\text{AC}}\to\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
:
if
\alpha\colon[n]\to[m]
for some
n,m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, then

\displaystyle\xi(\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\}_{\alpha})

\displaystyle=\xi(\{\alpha(i_{1}),\dots,\alpha(i_{k})\})

\displaystyle=((x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\mapsto s_{k}(x_{\alpha(i_{1})},\dots,x_{\alpha(i_{k})})

\displaystyle=((x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\mapsto s_{k}(x_{i_{1}},\dots,x_{i_{k}})_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\xi(\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\})_{\alpha}.

3\Rightarrow 2
: let
\xi
be the minion homomorphism from
{\bf M}_{\text{AC}}
to
\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
. Then the operation
s_{n}:=\xi(\{1,\dots,n\})\in\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
is totally symmetric and of arity
n
. Indeed, suppose that
a_{1},\dots,a_{n},b_{1},\dots,b_{n}\in B

are such that
\{a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\}=\{b_{1},\dots,b_{n}\}=\{c_{1},\dots,c_{m}\}

for some
m\leq n

such that
c_{1},\dots,c_{m}
are pairwise distinct.
Then there are
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\colon[n]\to[m]
such that
\alpha_{1}(i)=\alpha_{2}(i)\in\{1,\dots,m\}
for every
i\in[n]
.
Note that
s_{n}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})=(s_{n})_{\alpha_{1}}(c_{1},\dots,c_{m})=(s_{n})_{\alpha_{2}}(b_{1},\dots,b_{n})
.

3\Leftrightarrow 4
: Corollary
43.10
.
∎

Later, Theorem
43.24
will be the blueprint for characterisations of various other algorithms. To prove these characterisations, we need to generalise one important concept that was relevant in the proof of Theorem
43.24
(since it uses Theorem
7.1
): the concept of
P(H)
.
Obviously, this concept can be generalised to relational structure
\mathfrak{B}
instead of
H
.
Thus we get that
P(\mathfrak{B})
has a homomorphism to
\mathfrak{B}
if and only if
{\bf M}_{\AC}
has a homomorphism to
\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
(combining Theorem
43.24
with Theorem
7.1
).
Less obviously, this can also be generalised
from
{\bf M}_{\AC}
to arbitrary minions
{\bf M}
, and this is the content of the next section.

Exercises.

162.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
be finite structures with finite relational signature
\tau
and let
{\bf B}
be an algebra such that

\Clo({\bf B})=\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
.
Show that if

L(a)
is the list computed by
\AC_{\mathfrak{B}}
on input
\mathfrak{A}
for
a\in A
, then
L(a)\leq{\bf B}
.

43.7
The Free Structure

The definition of free structures first appeared in ‘the wonderland paper’
[
16
]
, and also plays a fundamental role for the theory of promise CSPs
[
8
]
;
we essentially follow the presentation there (Definition 4.1). However, unlike there, we use the following notational convention: we allow arbitrary finite sets
A
as index sets for tuples, rather than only sets of the form
[n]=\{1,\dots,n\}
.
Since
n
-tuples from a set
A
can be formalised as functions from
[n]
to
A
,
this is just a small stretch of the usual notation. We already used the notation
B^{A}
for the set of all functions from
A
to
B
, i.e., for a power of
B
where the factors are indexed by
A
.
Likewise,
B^{A}\to C
are functions from
B^{A}
to
C
where the arguments are indexed by
A
.
This notation extends

•

to minors, which are now specified by maps
\alpha\colon A\to B
between arbitrary finite sets
A
and
B
(rather than just maps of the form
\alpha\colon[k]\to[m]
),
which means in particular that we may use tuples
d\in C^{r}
to specify a minor
f_{d}\colon B^{C}\to B
of an operation
f\colon B^{r}\to B
, since
d
is a function from
[r]
to
C
, and

•

to
minions, where we are now allowed to write
M^{(A)}
for the elements of
M^{(|A|)}

(in other words, we now treat minions as functors from
FinSet
to
Set
, rather than functors from
FinOrd
to
Set
; see Exercise
158
).

Note that if
f\colon B^{r}\to B
,
\alpha\colon[r]\to[s]
,
d\in B^{s}
, then

\displaystyle f_{\alpha}(d)

\displaystyle=f(d\circ\alpha).

(25)

Definition 43.25

(the free structure)
.

Let
{\bf M}
be a minion and
\mathfrak{B}
be a structure with relational signature
\tau
. Then
\mathfrak{F}_{\bf M}(\mathfrak{B})
, the
free structure of
{\bf M}
generated by
\mathfrak{B}
, is the
\tau
-structure with the domain
M^{(B)}
. For
R\in\tau
of arity
k
, the relation

R^{\mathfrak{F}_{\bf M}(\mathfrak{B})}
is defined as follows.
Below we will use
\pi_{i}
, for
i\in[k]
, as a shortcut for the restriction of
\pi^{k}_{i}
to

R^{\mathfrak{B}}
, which is a function from
R^{\mathfrak{B}}
to
B
, and hence can be used to form minors of functions in
M^{(R^{\mathfrak{B}})}
.
The relation
R^{\mathfrak{F}_{\bf M}(\mathfrak{B})}

consists of the set of all
k
-tuples
(f_{1},\dots,f_{k})\in(M^{(B)})^{k}

such that there exists
g\in M^{(R^{\mathfrak{B}})}
satisfying
g_{\pi_{i}}=f_{i}
for every
i\in[k]
.

For readers who need some intuition for Definition
43.25
, but
are already comfortable with the material in Section
5.3
, we strongly recommend doing Exercise
164
.

Proposition 43.26
.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a structure with a finite domain and a relational signature
\tau
. Let
{\bf M}
be a minion.
Then the following are equivalent.

•

{\mathfrak{F}}_{{\bf M}}(\mathfrak{B})\to\mathfrak{B}
.

•

{\bf M}
has a homomorphism to
\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
.

Proof.

Let

B=\{b_{1},\dots,b_{n}\}
. First suppose that

h\colon{\mathfrak{F}}_{\bf M}(\mathfrak{B})\to\mathfrak{B}
is a homomorphism.
To define a minion homomorphism
\mu\colon{\bf M}\to\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
, let
f\in M^{(r)}
for some
r\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
For
c\in B^{r}
,
define

\mu(f)(c):=h(f_{c}).

To see that
\mu
is a minion homomorphism, suppose that

\beta\colon[r]\to[s]
is a function; we need to verify that
\mu(f_{\beta})=\mu(f)_{\beta}
.
Let
d\in B^{s}
.
Then

\displaystyle\mu(f_{\beta})(d)

\displaystyle=h((f_{\beta})_{d})

(Definition of
\mu
)

\displaystyle=h(f_{d\circ\beta})

(
{\bf M}
is a minion)

\displaystyle=\mu(f)(d\circ\beta)

(Definition of
\mu
)

\displaystyle=\mu(f)_{\beta}(d)

\displaystyle\eqref{eq:minor}.

We also have to verify that
\mu(f)\in\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
, i.e., that
\mu(f)
preserves
R^{\mathfrak{B}}
for every
R\in\tau
.
Let
t^{1},\dots,t^{r}\in R^{\mathfrak{B}}
; we need to show that
\mu(f)(t^{1},\dots,t^{r})\in R^{\mathfrak{B}}
.
Let
k
be the arity of
R
.
For every
i\in[k]
let
t_{i}:=(t^{1}_{i},\dots,t^{r}_{i})
.
Let
t
be the function from
[k]
to
R^{\mathfrak{B}}

that maps

i\in[k]
to
t_{i}
,
and let

g:=f_{t}
.
By the definition of
\mathfrak{F}_{{\bf M}}(\mathfrak{B})
,
we have
(f_{t_{1}},\dots,f_{t_{k}})\in R^{\mathfrak{F}_{{\bf M}}(\mathfrak{B})}
, since
g_{\pi_{i}}=(f_{t})_{\pi_{i}}=f_{\pi_{i}\circ t}=f_{t_{i}}
for every
i\in[k]
.
Then

\displaystyle\mu(f)(t^{1},\dots,t^{r})

\displaystyle=(\mu(f)(t_{1}),\dots,\mu(f)(t_{k}))

(by the definition of
t_{1},\dots,t_{k}
)

\displaystyle=(h(f_{t_{1}}),\dots,h(f_{t_{k}}))

(by the definition of
\mu(f)
)

\displaystyle\in R^{\mathfrak{B}}

(since
h
is a homomorphism).

For the converse direction,
suppose that
\mu\colon{\bf M}\to\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
is a (minion) homomorphism.
To define the homomorphism
h\colon{\mathfrak{F}}_{\bf M}(\mathfrak{B})\to\mathfrak{B}
,
let
f\in M^{(B)}
be an element of

{\mathfrak{F}}_{\bf M}(\mathfrak{B})
, and define
h(f):=\mu(f)(b_{1},\dots,b_{n})
.
To see that this is a homomorphism, suppose that

R\in\tau
has arity
k
, and suppose that
(f_{1},\dots,f_{k})\in R^{{\mathfrak{F}}_{\bf M}(\mathfrak{B})}
, i.e., there exists
g\in M^{(R^{\mathfrak{B}})}
such that
g_{\pi_{i}}=f_{i}
for every
i\in[k]
.
Then

\displaystyle(h(f_{1}),\dots,h(f_{k}))

\displaystyle=(\mu(f_{1})(b_{1},\dots,b_{n}),\dots,\mu(f_{k})(b_{1},\dots,b_{n}))

(by definition of
h
)

\displaystyle=(\mu(g_{\pi_{1}})(b_{1},\dots,b_{n}),\dots,\mu(g_{\pi_{k}})(b_{1},\dots,b_{n}))

(by assumption)

\displaystyle=\mu(g)(r_{1},\dots,r_{m})

(hint: read bottom-up)

\displaystyle\in R^{\mathfrak{B}}

since
\mu(g)\in\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
,

which shows that
h
is a homomorphism.
∎

Exercises.

163.

Verify that
{\bf M}_{\text{AC}}
(Definition
43.21
) is indeed a minion.

164.

Let
H
be a digraph. Prove that
\mathfrak{F}_{{\bf M}_{\AC}}(H)
equals
P(H)
.

44
Undirected Graphs

This section contains a proof of the dichotomy for finite undirected graphs of Hell and Nešetřil, Theorem
2.6
. We prove something stronger,
namely that
the tractability theorem (Theorem
20.1
) is true
for finite undirected graphs
\mathfrak{B}

[
33
]
.
More specifically, the following is true.

Theorem 44.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a finite undirected graph.
Then either

•

\mathfrak{B}
is
bipartite
(i.e., homomorphic to
K_{2}
) or has a loop,
or

•

\HI(\mathfrak{B})
contains all finite structures.

Note that in combination with
Corollary
19.2
,
this theorem implies the tractability theorem (Theorem
20.1
) for the special case of finite undirected graphs.
This theorem also has a remarkable
consequence in universal algebra,
whose significance goes beyond the study
of the complexity of CSPs, and
which provides a strengthening of
Taylor’s theorem (Theorem
43.15
), discovered by Siggers in 2010 (see Section
44.2
).

44.1
The Hell-Nešetřil Theorem

The graph
K_{4}-\{0,1\}
(a clique with four vertices where one edge is missing) is called a
diamond
.
A graph is called
diamond-free

if it does not contain a copy of a diamond as a (not necessarily induced) subgraph. For every
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the graph
(K_{3})^{\ell}
is an example of a diamond-free graph.

Lemma 44.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a finite undirected loopless graph
which is not bipartite.
Then
\mathfrak{B}
pp-constructs a diamond-free core containing a triangle.

Proof.

We may assume that

1.

\HI(\mathfrak{B})

does not contain
a non-bipartite loopless graph with fewer vertices than
\mathfrak{B}
, since otherwise
we could replace
\mathfrak{B}
by this graph. In particular,
\mathfrak{B}
must then be a core.

2.

\mathfrak{B}=(V;E)

contains a triangle: if the length of the shortest odd cycle in
\mathfrak{B}
is
k
, then
(B;E^{k-2})

is a graph and
contains
a triangle, so it can replace
\mathfrak{B}
.

Claim 1.
Every vertex
of
\mathfrak{B}
is contained in a triangle:
Otherwise, we can replace
\mathfrak{B}

by the subgraph of
\mathfrak{B}
induced
by set defined by
the primitive positive formula

\exists u,v\,\big(E(x,u)\wedge E(x,v)\wedge E(u,v)\big)

which still contains a triangle, contradicting our first assumption.

Claim 2.

\mathfrak{B}
does not contain
a copy of
K_{4}
. Otherwise, if
a
is an element from a copy of
K_{4}
, then
the subgraph of
\mathfrak{B}
induced by
the set defined by the primitive positive formula
E(a,x)

is a non-bipartite
graph
\mathfrak{A}
, which has strictly less vertices than
\mathfrak{B}
because
a\notin A
. Moreover, Theorem
19.12
implies that expansions of cores by constants can be pp-constructed, and hence that
\mathfrak{B}
pp-constructs

\mathfrak{A}
, contrary to our initial assumption.

Claim 3.
The graph
\mathfrak{B}
must also be diamond-free.
To see this, let
R
be the binary relation with the
primitive positive definition

R(x,y):\Leftrightarrow\exists u,v\,\big(E(x,u)\wedge E(x,v)\wedge E(u,v)\wedge E(u,y)\wedge E(v,y)\big)

and let
T
be the transitive closure of
R
.
The relation
T
is clearly symmetric, and
since every vertex of
\mathfrak{B}
is contained
in a triangle, it is also reflexive, and hence
an equivalence relation of
\mathfrak{B}
.
Since
B
is finite, for some
n

the formula

\exists u_{1},\dots,u_{n}\,\big(R(x,u_{1})\wedge R(u_{1},u_{2})\wedge\cdots\wedge R(u_{n},y)\big)
defines
T
, showing that

T
is
primitively positively definable in
\mathfrak{B}
.

Figure 16:
Diagram for the proof of Lemma
44.2
.

We claim that the graph
\mathfrak{B}/T
(see Example
18
) does not contain loops. It suffices to show that
T\cap E=\emptyset
. Otherwise, let
(a,b)\in T\cap E
. Choose

(a,b)
in such a way that
the shortest sequence
a=a_{0},a_{1},\dots,a_{n}=b
with
R(a_{0},a_{1})
,
R(a_{1},a_{2})
, …,
R(a_{n-1},a_{n})
in
\mathfrak{B}
is shortest possible;
see Figure
16
.
This chain cannot have the form
R(a_{0},a_{1})
because
\mathfrak{B}
does not contain
K_{4}
subgraphs.
Suppose first that
n=2k
is even.
Let the vertices
u_{1},v_{1},u_{k+1}
and
v_{k+1}
be as depicted in Figure
16
.
Let
S
be the set defined by

\displaystyle\exists x_{1},\dots,x_{k}

\displaystyle\,\big(E(u_{k+1},x_{1})\wedge E(v_{k+1},x_{1})\wedge R(x_{1},x_{2})\wedge\cdots\wedge R(x_{k-1},x_{k})\wedge E(x_{k},x)\big).

Note that
a_{0},u_{1},v_{1}\in S
form a triangle.
If
a_{n}\in S

then we obtain a contradiction to the minimal choice of
n
.
Hence, the subgraph induced by
the primitively positively definable set
S

is non-bipartite and strictly smaller than
\mathfrak{B}
,
in contradiction to the initial assumption.

If
n=2k+1
is odd, we can argue analogously
with the set
S
defined by the formula

\displaystyle\exists x_{1},\dots,x_{k}

\displaystyle\,\big(R(a_{k+1},x_{1})\wedge R(x_{1},x_{2})\wedge\cdots\wedge R(x_{k-1},x_{k})\wedge E(x_{k},x)\big)

and again obtain a contradiction. So we conclude that
\mathfrak{B}/T
does not contain loops. It also follows that

\mathfrak{B}/T
contains a triangle, because
\mathfrak{B}
contains a triangle.

Thus, the initial assumption on
\mathfrak{B}
then
implies that
T
must be the equality relation on
B
, which in turn implies
that
\mathfrak{B}
does not contain any diamonds.
∎

Lemma 44.3

(from
[
33
]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a diamond-free undirected graph and let
h\colon(K_{3})^{k}\to\mathfrak{B}

be a homomorphism. Then the image
of
h
is isomorphic to
(K_{3})^{m}
for some

m\leq k
.

Proof.

Let
I\subseteq\{1,\dots,k\}
be maximal
such that
\ker(h)\subseteq\ker(\pr_{I})
.
Note that
\pr_{I}
is defined even if
I=\emptyset
(Definition
24.1
).
Such a set exists, because
\ker(\pr_{\emptyset})
is the total relation.
We claim that
\ker(h)=\ker(\pr_{I})
;
this clearly implies the statement.

By the maximality of
I
, for every

j\in\{1,\dots,k\}\setminus I
there
are
x,y\in(K_{3})^{k}

such that
h(x)=h(y)
and
x_{j}\neq y_{j}
.
We have to show that for all
z_{1},\dots,z_{k},z_{j}^{\prime}\in\{0,1,2\}

h(z_{1},\dots,z_{j},\dots,z_{k})=h(z_{1},\dots,z_{j-1},z_{j}^{\prime},z_{j+1},\dots,z_{k}).

We may suppose that
z_{j}\neq x_{j}
and
z^{\prime}_{j}=x_{j}
.
To simplify notation, we assume that
j=k
.
As we have seen in Exercises
7
and
8
, any two vertices
in
(K_{3})^{k}
have a common neighbour.

•

Let
r
be a common neighbour of

x
and
(z,z_{k}):=(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})
.
Note that
r
and
(z,z_{k}^{\prime})
are adjacent, too.

•

For all
i\neq k
we choose an element
s_{i}

of
K_{3}
that is distinct from both
r_{i}

and
y_{i}
. Since
x_{k}
is distinct
from
r_{k}
and
y_{k}

we have that

(s,x_{k}):=(s_{1},\dots,s_{k-1},x_{k})

is a common neighbour of
r
and
y
.

•

The tuple

(r,z_{k}):=(r_{1},\dots,r_{k-1},z_{k})

is a common neighbour of both
x
and
(s,x_{k})
.

•

Finally, for
i\neq k
choose
t_{i}

to be distinct from
z_{i}
and
r_{i}
,
and choose
t_{k}
to be distinct
from
z_{k}
and from
z_{k}^{\prime}
.
Then
t:=(t_{1},\dots,t_{k-1},t_{k})

is a common neighbour of
(z,z_{k})
,
of
(z,z_{k}^{\prime})
, and
of
(r,z_{k})
.

The situation is illustrated in Figure
17
.
Since
\mathfrak{B}
is diamond-free,

h(x)=h(y)
implies that
h(r)=h(r,z_{k})

and for the same reason
h(z,z_{k})=h(z,z_{k}^{\prime})

which completes the proof.
∎

Figure 17:
Diagram for the proof of Lemma
44.3
.

Lemma 44.4

(from
[
33
]
)
.

If a finite diamond-free graph
\mathfrak{B}

contains a triangle, then for some

k\in{\mathbb{N}}

there is a primitive positive
interpretation of

(K_{3})^{k}
with constants in
\mathfrak{B}
.

Proof.

We construct a strictly increasing sequence
of subgraphs
G_{1}\subset G_{2}\subset\cdots
of
\mathfrak{B}
such that
G_{i}
is isomorphic to

(K_{3})^{k_{i}}
for some
k_{i}\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Let
G_{1}
be any triangle in
\mathfrak{B}
.
Suppose now that
G_{i}
has already been
constructed. If the domain of
G_{i}
is
primitively positively definable in
\mathfrak{B}

with constants, then we are done.
Otherwise, there exists an idempotent polymorphism
f
of
\mathfrak{B}
and
v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\in G_{i}

such that
f(v_{1},\dots,v_{k})\notin G_{i}
.
The restriction of
f
to
G_{i}
is a homomorphism from
(K_{3})^{k_{i}}
to
the diamond-free graph
\mathfrak{B}
.
Lemma
44.3
shows
that
G_{i+1}:=f((G_{i})^{k})
induces a copy
of
(K_{3})^{k_{i+1}}
for some
k_{i+1}\leq k
.
Since
f
is idempotent, we have
that
G_{i}\subseteq G_{i+1}
, and by the choice of
f
the containment is strict.
Since
\mathfrak{B}
is finite, for some
m
the set
G_{m}
must have a primitive positive definition in
\mathfrak{B}
with constants.
∎

Proof of Theorem
44.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a finite undirected graph that is not bipartite. Then

\mathfrak{B}
interprets primitively positively a graph that is homomorphically equivalent to
a diamond-free core
\mathfrak{C}
containing a triangle, by Lemma
44.2
. We may now apply Lemma
44.4
to
\mathfrak{C}
and obtain
that for some
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
there
is a primitive positive interpretation of

(K_{3})^{k}
with constants in
\mathfrak{C}
.
Since
\mathfrak{C}
is a core, and since
(K_{3})^{k}
is
homomorphically equivalent to
K_{3}
,
it follows
that there is a primitive positive interpretation
of a structure that is homomorphically equivalent to
K_{3}
in
\mathfrak{C}
.
The structure
K_{3}
interprets all finite structures primitive positively (Theorem
19.3
), so
Theorem
19.12
implies
that
\HH(\PP(\mathfrak{B}))
contains all finite structures.
∎

44.2
Siggers Terms of Arity 6

An operation
s\colon B^{6}\to B

is called
Siggers operation
(of arity six
8
8

8

We stress the arity here since there is also a notion of Siggers operations for arity four, which is a similar but stronger result, see Section
63.3
.
)
if for all
x,y,z\in B

s(x,y,x,z,y,z)=s(y,x,z,x,z,y).

As usual, if
{\bf A}
is an algebra and
t
is a term such that
t^{{\bf A}}
is a Siggers operation, we call
t
a
Siggers term
.

Theorem 44.5

(from
[
94
]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a finite structure. Then
either
\mathfrak{B}
primitively positively interprets all finite structures
up to homomorphic equivalence,
or
\mathfrak{B}

has a Siggers polymorphism.

Proof.

Pick
k\geq 1
and
a,b,c\in B^{k}
such
that
\{(a_{i},b_{i},c_{i})\mid i\leq k\}=B^{3}
.
Let
R
be the binary relation on
B^{k}

such that
(u,v)\in R
if and only if there exists a 6-ary
s\in\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
such that

u=s(a,b,a,c,b,c)
and
v=s(b,a,c,a,c,b)
.
We make the following series of observations.

•

The vertices
a,b,c\in B^{k}
induce in
(B^{k};R)
a copy of
K_{3}
: each of the six edges of
K_{3}
is witnessed by one of the six 6-ary projections from
\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
.

•

The relation
R
is symmetric:
Suppose that
(u,v)\in R
and let
s\in\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
be such that

u=s(a,b,a,c,b,c)
and
v=s(b,a,c,a,c,b)
.
Define
s^{\prime}\in\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
by
s^{\prime}(x_{1},\dots,x_{6}):=s(x_{2},x_{1},x_{4},x_{3},x_{6},x_{5})
; then

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=s(b,a,c,a,c,b)=s^{\prime}(a,b,a,c,b,c)

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=s(a,b,a,c,b,c)=s^{\prime}(b,a,c,a,c,b)

and hence
s^{\prime}
witnesses that
(v,u)\in R
.

•

If the graph
(B^{k};R)
contains a loop

(w,w)\in R
, then there exists a 6-ary

s\in\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
such that

s(a,b,a,c,b,c)=w=s(b,a,c,a,c,b)\,.

The operation
s
is Siggers:
for all
x,y,z\in B
there exists an
i\leq k

such that
(x,y,z)=(a_{i},b_{i},c_{i})
,
and the above implies that

s(a_{i},b_{i},a_{i},c_{i},b_{i},c_{i})=s(b_{i},a_{i},c_{i},a_{i},c_{i},b_{i})

and we are done in this case.

So we may assume in the following
that
(B^{k};R)
is a simple (i.e., undirected and loopless) graph that contains a copy of
K_{3}
.
The relation
R
(as a
2k
-ary relation over
B
) is preserved by
\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
, and hence

(B^{k};R)
has a primitive positive interpretation in
\mathfrak{B}
.
By Theorem
44.1
applied to the undirected graph
(B^{k};R)
,
there is a primitive positive interpretation in
(B^{k};R)
of all finite structures up to homomorphic equivalence, and hence also
in
\mathfrak{B}
, and this concludes the proof.
∎

Theorem 44.6

(Tractability Theorem, Version 4)
.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a relational structure with finite domain and finite signature.
If
\mathfrak{B}
has a Siggers polymorphism, then
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
is in P. Otherwise,

\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
is NP-complete.

Proof.

An immediate consequence of Theorem
44.5
and Theorem
20.1
.
∎

Exercises.

165.

A Taylor clone
\bf C
is called
minimal Taylor
if every proper subclone of
\bf C

is not Taylor.
Show that every Taylor clone on a finite set

contains a minimal Taylor clone.

45
Congruences

The study of congruences of algebras and varieties is one of the central topics in universal algebra. In Section
45.1
we present some basic facts about congruences that will be used later in the text. Section
47.1
about congruence permutability
and Section
47.2
about congruence distributivity
cover material that is central in universal algebra, but will not be used later in the text and can be skipped by the hasty reader.

45.1
The Congruence Lattice

Let
{\bf A}
be a
\tau
-Algebra. We write
\Con({\bf A})
for the set of all congruences of
{\bf A}
(Definition
36.3
).
Clearly,
\Con({\bf A})
is closed under arbitrary intersections.
On the other hand, the union of two congruences is in general not a congruence.

Lemma 45.1
.

Let
{\bf A}
be an algebra. Then

(\Con({\bf A}),\subseteq)
is a complete lattice (Example
34
).

Proof.

Let
(E_{i})_{i\in I}
be a sequence of congruences of
{\bf A}
. Define (recall the definition of the relational product, Definition
17.1
)

\bigvee_{i\in I}E_{i}=\bigcup\big\{E_{i_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ E_{i_{k}}\mid i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\in I,k\in{\mathbb{N}}\big\}

Note that this is the smallest (with respect to inclusion) equivalence relation that contains all the
E_{i}
.
Let
f\in\tau
be
n
-ary and

(a_{1},b_{1}),\dots,(a_{n},b_{n})\in E
.
Then there are
i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\in I
such that for all
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}

(a_{j},b_{j})\in E_{i_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ E_{i_{k}}.

Hence,

(f(a_{1},\dots,a_{n}),f(b_{1},\dots,b_{n}))\in E_{i_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ E_{i_{k}}
and
E\in\Con({\bf A})
.
∎

Every algebra has the following two congruences.

•

\Delta_{A}
: the
diagonal relation

\{(a,a)\mid a\in A\}
.

•

\nabla_{A}
: the
universal relation

A^{2}
.

Congruences that are different from

\nabla_{A}
and
\Delta_{A}
are called
proper
.

Definition 45.2
.

Algebras
{\bf A}
without proper congruences are called

simple
.

Example 46
.

Groups that are simple in the sense of group theory are simple in the more general sense of
Defintion
45.2
.

Example 47
.

Let
G
be a permutation group on the set
A
.
Let
{\bf A}
be the algebra with domain
A
and signature
G
, und define

g^{{\bf A}}:=g
for all
g\in G
.
Then
{\bf A}
is simple if and only if
G
is primitive
as a permutation group.

Definition 47.1
.

Let
{\bf A}
be an algebra and let
{\bf B}
be the expansion of
{\bf A}
by all constant operations.
A
polynomial over
{\bf A}
is a term in the signature of
{\bf B}
. A
polynomial operation of
{\bf A}
is a term operation of
{\bf B}
.

Definition 47.2
.

Two algebras
{\bf A}_{1},{\bf A}_{2}
with the same domain
A
are called
polynomially equivalent

if they have the same polynomial operations.

Note that polynomially equivalent algebras
have the same congruences.

Lemma 47.3
.

Let
{\bf B}
be an algebra and
X\subseteq B^{2}
.
Then the smallest congruence of
{\bf B}
that contains
X
, denoted by
\Cg_{{\bf B}}(X)
, equals the symmetric transitive closure of

\displaystyle T:=\{(p(a),p(b))\in B^{2}\mid(a,b)\in X,p\text{ a unary polynomial operation of }{\bf B}\}.

(26)

Proof.

Let
C
be the symmetric transitive closure of
T
. Clearly, if
(a,b)\in X
and
p
is a unary polynomial operation of
{\bf B}
, then
(p(a),p(b))\in\Cg_{{\bf B}}(X)
since congruences are preserved by term operations and are reflexive.
Since
\Cg_{{\bf B}}(X)
is transitive and reflexive we obtain that
C\subseteq\Cg_{{\bf B}}(X)
. To prove that
\Cg_{{\bf B}}(X)\subseteq C
it suffices to prove that
C
is a congruence of
{\bf B}
that contains
X
. Since
C
is preserved by all constant operations, we have reflexivity of
C
,
and we clearly have that
X\subseteq C
.
We are left with the verification that
C
is a congruence. If
(u_{1},v_{1}),\dots,(u_{k},v_{k})\in C

and
f
is an operation of
{\bf B}
of arity
k
,
then for each
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
there exists a path
P_{i}=(a_{i,1},\dots,a_{i,r})
of edges in
T
that starts in
u_{i}
and ends in
v_{i}
. By the reflexivity of
T
we may duplicate elements on these paths such that

•

all these paths
P_{i}
have the same length
r
;

•

for every every
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
and every
\ell\in\{1,\dots,r-1\}

the
\ell
-th edge of
P_{i}
is
of the form
(a,a)
, i.e.,

a_{i,\ell}=a_{i,\ell+1}
, for all but one path, say that
i({\ell})
-th path.

Hence, for every
\ell\in\{1,\dots,r-1\}

the polynomial

p_{l}:=f(a_{1,\ell},\dots,a_{i(\ell)-1,\ell},x_{i(\ell)},a_{i(\ell)+1,\ell},\dots,a_{k,\ell})

shows that
there is an (forward or backward) edge between the elements

f(a_{1,\ell},\dots,a_{k,\ell})
and
f(a_{1,\ell+1},\dots,a_{k,\ell+1})
.
By symmetry and transitivity we therefore obtain a path of edges in
T
between
u_{0}:=f(u_{1},\dots,u_{k})
and
v_{0}:=f(v_{1},\dots,v_{k})
, showing that
(u_{0},v_{0})\in S
are we are done.
∎

Exercises.

is simple.

{\bf A}
is an idempotent algebra
and
C
a congruence of
{\bf A}
,
then every congruence class of
C
is a subalgebra of
{\bf A}
.

Prove the remark after Definition
47.2
.

Let
{\bf A}
be an algebra. Show that an equivalence relation
R\subseteq A^{2}
is
a congruence of
{\bf A}

if and only if it is preserved by all
unary
polynomials of
{\bf A}
.
Which properties of an equivalence relation do you need in the proof?

Let
{\bf A}
be an algebra. Then a relation
R\subseteq A^{n}
is called

•

reflexive
if
(a,\dots,a)\in R
for every
a\in A
.

•

transitive
if for all
(a_{ij})_{i,j\in\{1,\dots,n\}}\in A^{n\times n}
whose rows and columns are from
R
, we have that

(a_{11},\dots,a_{nn})\in R
.

Show that a reflexive and transitive
R

\Clo({\bf A})
if and only if it is preserved by all unary polynomial operations of
{\bf A}
.

is

, i.e., the clone of polynomial operations of

equals the set of all operations on
A
.

(b)

The

d\colon A^{3}\to A
is a polynomial operation of

. The discriminator operation is defined as follows:

\displaystyle d(x,y,z):=\begin{cases}z&x=y\\

=_{A}

\bigcup_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}}(R^{-1}\circ R)^{i}

is a congruence of
{\bf A}
.

47.1
Congruence Permutability

Two congruences
C_{1},C_{2}\in\Con({\bf A})

permute
if

C_{1}\circ C_{2}=C_{2}\circ C_{1}.

An algebra
{\bf A}
is called
congruence permutable

if all pairs of congruences of
{\bf A}
permute.
In the following, it will be convenient to write
a_{0}C_{1}a_{1}C_{2}a_{2}\cdots a_{n}C_{n}a_{n}

as a shortcut for

a_{1}C_{1}a_{2}
and
a_{2}C_{2}a_{3}
and

\cdots a_{n-1}Ca_{n}
.

Lemma 47.4
.

Let
{\bf A}
be an algebra such that
\Clo({\bf A})
contains a Maltsev operation
p
.
Then
{\bf A}
is congruence permutable.

Proof.

Let
C,E\in\Con({\bf A})

and let
(a,b)\in C\circ E
. Then there exists
c\in A
with

(a,c)\in C
und
(c,b)\in E
.
Note that

b=p^{{\bf A}}(c,c,b)\;C\;p^{{\bf A}}(a,c,b)\;E\;p^{{\bf A}}(a,b,b)=a

and thus
(b,a)\in C\circ E
and
(a,b)\in E\circ C
.
∎

Note that most classical algebras, such as groups, rings, fields, etc., do have a Maltsev term operation, and hence are congruence permutable.
Lemma
47.4
has a converse; however, for the converse we have to work with varieties of algebras.
A variety is called
congruence permutable
if all algebras in the variety are congruence permutable.
We will show that an algebra
{\bf A}
has a Maltsev term if and only if the variety generated by
{\bf A}
is congruence permutable (Corollary
47.6
).

In our proof of this result, we will use
the concept of rectangularity, which we have already encountered
in Section
11.2
,
where we proved that digraphs with a Maltsev polymorphism are rectangular.

Theorem 47.5

(Maltsev)
.

Let
{\mathcal{K}}
be a class of

\tau
-algebras. Then the following are equivalent.

1.

\HSP({\mathcal{K}})
is congruence permutable.

2.

For every algebra
{\bf B}\in{\mathcal{K}}

every subalgebra
R\leq{\bf B}^{2}
is rectangular.

3.

There exists a
\tau
-term
which denotes a Maltsev operation
in every algebra in
{\mathcal{K}}
.

Proof.

3. implies 1.:
If every algebra in
{\mathcal{K}}
has a Maltsev term operation, then so does
\HSP({\mathcal{K}})
, and hence the statement follows from Lemma
47.4
.

1. implies 2.: Apply congruence permutability to the two projection congruences of
R\leq{\bf B}^{2}
: these are the congruences

\displaystyle C_{1}

\displaystyle:=\{((a,b),(c,d))\mid(a,b),(c,d)\in R,a=c\}

\displaystyle C_{2}

\displaystyle:=\{((a,b),(c,d))\mid(a,b),(c,d)\in R,b=d\}.

2. implies 3.: Let

{\bf B}\in\HSP({\mathcal{K}})
be the free algebra for

\HSP({\mathcal{K}})
over
\{x,y\}
, and
let
R
be the subuniverse of
{\bf B}^{2}
generated by

\{(x,x),(x,y),(y,y)\}
. Since
R
is rectangular, we have that
(x,y)\in R
.
Hence, there exists a term
t
such that
t^{{\bf B}^{2}}((x,x),(x,y),(y,y))=(y,x)
. Then
t
satisfies

t(x,x,y)\approx y
and
t(x,y,y)\approx x

in every algebra of
\HSP({\mathcal{K}})
,
(Lemma
38.11
), and hence
{\bf A}
has a Maltsev term.
∎

Corollary 47.6
.

Let
{\bf A}
be an algebra. Then the following are equivalent.

1.

Every algebra in
\HSP({\bf A})
is congruence permutable.

2.

For every algebra
{\bf B}\in\HSP({\bf A})
, every
R\leq{\bf B}^{2}
is rectangular.

3.

{\bf A}
has a Maltsev term.

47.2
Congruence Distributivity

A lattice
(P;\wedge,\vee,0,1)

is called
distributive
if it satisfies

\displaystyle(x\wedge y)\vee z

\displaystyle\approx(x\vee z)\wedge(y\vee z)

\displaystyle\text{ and }\quad(x\vee y)\wedge z

\displaystyle\approx(x\wedge z)\vee(y\wedge z).

An example of a distributive lattice is
the set of subsets of a set
S
, ordered by inclusion:
({\mathcal{P}}(S);\subseteq)
.
If the congruence lattice of
{\bf A}
is distributive, we call
{\bf A}

congruence distributive
.

Lemma 47.7
.

Every algebra with a majority term operation is congruence distributive.

Proof.

Let
C,D,E\in\Con(\mathfrak{A})
and
(a,b)\in C\wedge(D\vee E)
.
Then there are

c_{1},\dots,c_{n}
such that

aDc_{1}Ec_{2}Dc_{3}\dots c_{n}Eb.

Since
(b,a)\in C
, we have for all
c\in A
that

\displaystyle m^{\mathfrak{A}}(a,c,b)\,C\,m^{\mathfrak{A}}(a,c,a)=a.

Thus, for all
c_{1},c_{2}\in A
we obtain

\displaystyle m^{\mathfrak{A}}(a,c_{1},b)\,C\,a\,C\,m^{\mathfrak{A}}(a,c_{2},b).

(27)

Therefore,

\displaystyle a=m^{\mathfrak{A}}(a,a,b)

\displaystyle(C\wedge D)m^{\mathfrak{A}}(a,c_{1},b)

(by (
27
))

\displaystyle(C\wedge E)m^{\mathfrak{A}}(a,c_{2},b)

\displaystyle\cdots

\displaystyle(C\wedge D)m^{\mathfrak{A}}(a,c_{n},b)

\displaystyle(C\wedge E)m^{\mathfrak{A}}(a,b,b)=b.

We conclude that
(a,b)\in(C\wedge D)\vee(C\wedge E)
.
∎

As in the case of congruence permutability,
there is even an equational characterisation of congruence distributivity of varieties.

Theorem 47.8

(Jónsson)
.

\HSP({\mathcal{K}})

is congruence distributive if and only if there
exists
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and

\tau
-terms
p_{0},\dots,p_{n}
such that

{\mathcal{K}}

satisfies the following identities.

\displaystyle p_{i}(x,y,x)

\displaystyle\approx x

\displaystyle\text{ for }i\in\{1,\dots,n\}

\displaystyle p_{0}(x,y,z)

\displaystyle\approx x

\displaystyle p_{i}(x,x,y)

\displaystyle\approx p_{i+1}(x,x,y)

for
i
even

\displaystyle p_{i}(x,y,y)

\displaystyle\approx p_{i+1}(x,y,y)

for
i
odd

\displaystyle p_{n}(x,y,z)

\displaystyle\approx z

Proof.

“
\Rightarrow
”. Let
{\bf F}:=F_{\mathcal{K}}(\{x,y,z\})
.
Let
C_{xy}
be the congruence of

{\bf F}
obtained by identifying the variables
x
and
y
, i.e.,

C_{xy}:=\{(r,s)\in{\bf F}^{2}\mid{\bf F}\models r(x,x,z)\approx s(x,x,z)\}.

The congruences
C_{yz}
and
C_{xz}
are defined analogously. Then we have

C_{xz}\wedge\big(C_{xy}\vee C_{yz}\big)=\big(C_{xz}\wedge C_{xy}\big)\vee\big(C_{xz}\wedge C_{yz}\big)

hence
(x,z)\in\big(C_{xz}\wedge C_{xy}\big)\vee\big(C_{xz}\wedge C_{yz}\big)
in
{\bf F}
. Thus, there are
p_{1},\dots,p_{n-1}\in{\bf F}
such that each of
(x,p_{1}),(p_{1},p_{2}),\dots,(p_{n-1},z)
is in
C_{xz}\wedge C_{xy}

or in
C_{xz}\wedge C_{yz}
. By padding the sequence
p_{1},\dots,p_{n-1}
with repeated entries, we may even suppose that

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\big(C_{xz}\wedge C_{xy}\big)p_{1}

(28)

\displaystyle p_{1}

\displaystyle\big(C_{xz}\wedge C_{yz}\big)p_{2}

(29)

\displaystyle\quad\vdots

\displaystyle p_{n-1}

\displaystyle\big(C_{xz}\wedge C_{yz}\big)z

(30)

From (
28
) we obtain that

x=p_{1}(x,y,x)=p_{1}(x,x,y),

from (
29
) we obtain

\displaystyle p_{1}(x,y,x)

\displaystyle=p_{2}(x,y,x)

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad p_{1}(x,y,y)

\displaystyle=p_{2}(x,y,y),

and from (
30
) that

p_{n-1}(x,y,x)=p_{n-1}(x,y,y)=z
.
Similarly, all other identities from the statement can be derived from the sequence
(
29
)-(
30
).

“
\Leftarrow
”.
Let
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}\in\Con({\bf A})
for
{\bf A}\in\HSP({\mathcal{K}})
. It suffices to show that

\displaystyle C_{1}\wedge(C_{2}\vee C_{3})

\displaystyle\subseteq(C_{1}\wedge C_{2})\vee(C_{1}\wedge C_{3})

since the converse inclusion holds in every lattice.
Let
(a,b)\in C_{1}\wedge(C_{2}\vee C_{3})
.
That is, there are
c_{1},\dots,c_{t}
with

aC_{2}c_{1}C_{3}c_{2}C_{2}\cdots c_{t}C_{3}b.

For
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}

p_{i}(a,a,b)C_{2}p_{i}(a,c_{1},b)C_{3}p_{i}(a,c_{2},b)\cdots C_{3}p_{i}(a,b,b)

and since
p_{i}(a,c,b)C_{1}p_{i}(a,c,a)=a

p_{i}(a,a,b)(C_{1}\wedge C_{2})p_{i}(a,c_{1},b)(C_{1}\wedge C_{3})p_{i}(a,c_{2},b)\cdots(C_{1}\wedge C_{3})p_{i}(a,b,b).

Therefore,

p_{i}(a,a,b)\big((C_{1}\wedge C_{2})\vee(C_{1}\wedge C_{3})\big)p_{i}(a,b,b)

We conclude that
a\big((C_{1}\wedge C_{2})\vee(C_{1}\wedge C_{3})\big)b
.
∎

If the variety is generated by the polymorphism clone of a finite structure
\mathfrak{B}
with finite relational signature, this condition has drastic consequences
for
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
, similarly as in the previous section for congruence permutable varities.
Barto
[
6
]
proved that in this case
\mathfrak{B}
must also have a near unanimity polymorphism and hence can be solved in polynomial time by the methods that will be presented in Section
65
.

,

, and

.

Let
f\colon{\bf A}\to\prod_{i\in I}{\bf A}_{i}

is both
congruence permutable and congruence distributive.

\displaystyle x\wedge(y\vee z)\approx(x\wedge y)\vee(x\wedge z)

\displaystyle x\vee(y\wedge z)\approx(x\vee y)\wedge(x\vee z)

if and only if it satisfies one of those identities.

180.

Let
{\bf A}
be an algebra.
A term
t
is called a
Pixley term
if it satisfies

\displaystyle t(x,y,y)

\displaystyle\approx x

\displaystyle t(x,y,x)

\displaystyle\approx x

\displaystyle t(y,y,x)

\displaystyle\approx x.

t
is a Pixley term, then

t(x,t(x,y,z),z)
is a majority term.

181.

{\bf A}
generates a variety which is both congruence permutable and congruence distributive if and only if it has a Pixley term.

182.

is a simple algebra, and it has a Pixley term, then it is polynomially complete (see Exercise
171
).

48
Abelian Algebras

Modules (Example
32
) have many strong properties and are very well understood.
Affine algebras are ‘essentially’ modules and introduced in Section
48.1
.
The relevance of affine algebras in the context of constraint satisfaction is that core structures with more than one element and whose polymorphism algebra is idempotent and affine can
pp-construct
({\mathbb{Z}}_{p};+,1)
, for some prime
p
(Section
49.1
).
We have seen in Theorem
26.2

that the CSP of such structures cannot be solved by
k
-consistency, for any
k
.

Abelian
algebras are defined more abstractly (Section
49.2
).
It turns out that under fairly general conditions, abelian algebras must be affine; this is the content of the fundamental theorem of abelian algebras which will be presented in Section
48
(Theorem
51.2
) and generalised later in Section
59.1
. Section
51.1
presents other useful characterisations of abelian algebras in terms of congruences.

48.1
Affine Algebras

An algebra
{\bf A}
is called
affine
if
{\bf A}
is polynomially equivalent (Definition
47.2
) to a module (Example
32
).
Clearly, every module
{\bf M}
has a Maltsev term operation, namely
(x,y,z)\mapsto x-y+z
, so every affine algebra has a Maltsev polynomial operation. Something stronger holds.

Lemma 48.1
.

If
{\bf A}
is affine, then

(x,y,z)\mapsto x-y+z
is the
unique

Maltsev polynomial operation of
{\bf A}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
{\bf A}
is polynomially equivalent to module
{\bf M}
over the ring
{\bf R}
.
Let
m(x,y,z)=\alpha x+\beta y+\gamma z+d
, for
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in R
and
d\in M
, be a Maltsev polynomial operation of
{\bf A}
. Since
m(0,0,0)=0
we must have
d=0
. Moreover,
for all
x\in M
we have
x=m(x,0,0)=\alpha x

and analogously we obtain
x=\gamma x
. Finally,
m(x,x,0)=x+\beta x=0
, and therefore

\beta x=-x
.
We conclude that
m(x,y,z)=x-y+z
.
∎

Remark 48.2
.

The operation
(x,y,z)\mapsto x-y+z
is an affine Maltsev operation as defined in Section
26.1
.
An algebra whose
term operations are generated by
an affine Maltsev operation is called an
affine Maltsev algebra
(also see Exercise
145
).
Note that affine Maltsev algebras
are affine in the sense defined above.

Example 49
.

Every commutative group is affine: let
{\bf R}
be the ring of integers
{\mathbb{Z}}
, and define scalar multiplication
n\cdot x
as
\underbrace{x+\cdots+x}_{n\text{ times}}
.

Definition 49.1
.

Let
{\bf A}
be an algebra. An operation
m\colon A^{k}\to A
is
called
central in
{\bf A}
if

m
is a homomorphism from
{\bf A}^{k}\to{\bf A}
.

Remark 49.2
.

Note that
m
is central in
{\bf A}
if and only if
every operation of
{\bf A}
preserves the graph of
m
(see Exercise
94
). Hence, if
{\bf A}=\Pol(\mathfrak{A})
for some finite structure
\mathfrak{A}
, then
m
is central in
{\bf A}
if and only if its graph has a primitive positive definition in
\mathfrak{A}
.

Lemma 49.3
.

Let
{\bf A}
be affine. Then the operation
(x,y,z)\mapsto x-y+z
is central.

Proof.

Let
f
be a basic operation of
{\bf A}
of arity
n
.
Since
{\bf A}
is affine we can write
f
as
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}x_{i}+c
. Then

\displaystyle f(\bar{x})-f(\bar{y})+f(\bar{z})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}}\alpha_{i}x_{i}+c-\left(\sum_{i=1}\alpha_{i}y_{i}+c\right)+\sum_{i=1}\alpha_{i}z_{i}+c

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}}\alpha_{i}(x_{i}-y_{i}+z_{i})+c

\displaystyle=f(x_{1}-y_{1}+z_{1},\dots,x_{n}-y_{n}+z_{n}).\qed

Exercises.

183.

Let
{\bf A}
be an affine conservative algebra. Show that
|A|=2
.

49.1
Structures with an Idempotent Affine Polymorphism Clone

In this section we show that if a finite structure of size at least two has an idempotent affine polymorphism algebra, it can simulate systems of linear equations over a finite field.

Proposition 49.4
.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a finite structure with at least two elements and let

{\bf A}
be an affine idempotent algebra such that
\Clo({\bf A})=\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
. Then there exists a prime number
p
such that
\mathfrak{B}
pp-constructs

({\mathbb{Z}}_{p};+,1)
.

Proof.

Since the operation
m\colon(x,y,z)\mapsto x-y+z
is central in
{\bf A}
(Lemma
49.3
), and since
{\bf A}
is idempotent, the addition operation
+\colon A^{2}\to A
which is given by
m(x,0,y)
is central as well, and
hence primitively positively definable in
\mathfrak{B}
.
Every element
a\in A
of the abelian group
(A;+,-,0)
generates a cyclic group, and some
a\in A
must have prime order
p
; choose
a\in A
such that
p
is smallest possible.
An element of
(A;+,-,0)
satisfies the formula

\underbrace{x+\cdots+x}_{p\text{ times}}=0

if and only if it is
0
or has order
p
.
The set of all these elements forms a subgroup

{\bf A}_{p}\leq(A;+,-,0)
.
By elementary group theory (see, e.g., Theorem 5 in Chapter 5 of
[
50
]
) there is an
isomorphism
i
between
{\bf A}_{p}

and

({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{k}
, for some
k\geq 1
.
Let
b:=i^{-1}(1,0,\dots,0)
.
It suffices to show
that
(A_{p};+,b)
is homomorphically equivalent to
({\mathbb{Z}}_{p},+,1)
.
The homomorphism from
(A_{p};+,b)
to

({\mathbb{Z}}_{p},+,1)
is given by

\pi_{1}\circ i
, and the homomorphism from

({\mathbb{Z}}_{p},+,1)
to
(A_{p},+,b)
is given by

x\mapsto i(x,0,\dots,0)
.
∎

49.2
The Term Condition

We will see that affine algebras
satisfy a general ‘universal-algebraic’ condition, abelianness.

Definition 49.5
.

An algebra
{\bf A}
is
abelian
if
it
satisfies the term condition
, i.e., for
every term
t
of arity
k+1
,
all
a,b\in A
and tuples
c,d\in A^{k}

t(a,c)=t(a,d)\Rightarrow t(b,c)=t(b,d).

We also say that in Definition
49.5
the term condition is
applied to the first argument of
t
;
since we can permute arguments of
t
it is clear what is meant by applying the term condition to other arguments of
t
.

Example 50
.

Every algebra all of whose operations are essentially unary is abelian.

Example 51
.

A group
{\bf G}=(G;\circ,^{-1},e)
(Example
30
) is abelian if and only if multiplication is commutative, i.e.,
{\bf G}
satisfies
x\circ y\approx y\circ x
.
Let us consider the term operation

[z_{1},z_{2}]:=z_{1}^{-1}\circ z_{2}^{-1}\circ z_{1}\circ z_{2}

(the
commutator
from group theory)
and let
x,y\in G
. Then
[e,y]=e=[e,e]
. The term condition implies that we can exchange
e
in the first argument of the term by
x
, and obtain
[x,y]=[x,e]=e
. Thus,
[x,y]=x^{-1}y^{-1}xy=e

which implies that
xy=yx
.
The converse direction follows from Lemma
51.1
.

Lemma 51.1
.

Every affine algebra
{\bf A}
is abelian.

Proof.

To verify the term condition of
{\bf A}
, let
t
be a term operation. By assumption,
t
can be written as
t(x,y_{1},\dots,y_{n})=\alpha_{0}x+\sum_{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}}\alpha_{i}y_{i}+c
. Now, if
a,b\in A
and
u,v\in A^{n}
then

\displaystyle t(a,u)=t(a,v)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\sum_{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}}\alpha_{i}u_{i}=\sum_{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}}\alpha_{i}v_{i}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow t(b,u)=t(b,v).\qed

The following result was found by H. P. Gumm and, independently, J. D. H. Smith;
note that it is
not
restricted to algebras with a finite domain.

Theorem 51.2

(Fundamental theorem of abelian algebras; see
[
84
]
)
.

Let
{\bf A}
be an algebra.
Then the following are equivalent.

(1)

{\bf A}
is abelian and has a Maltsev term
m
.

(2)

{\bf A}
is affine.

(3)

There exists an
abelian group
(A;+,-,0)
such that the operation
(x,y,z)\mapsto x-y+z
is central in
{\bf A}
and in
\Clo({{\bf A}})
.

(4)

{\bf A}
has a central Maltsev term
m
.

Proof.

We prove these implications in cyclic order.

For the implication from (1) to (2), we need to construct a module
{\bf M}
that is polynomially equivalent to
{\bf A}
. Arbitrarily fix
0\in A
. Define
x+^{{\bf M}}y:=m(x,0,y)
and
-^{{\bf M}}x:=m(0,x,0)
. The ring
{\bf R}
has the domain

R=\{r\in A^{A}\mid r\text{ unary polynomial operation such that }r(0)=0\}

and the operations:

•

r_{1}\cdot^{{\bf R}}r_{2}
is defined as
x\mapsto r_{1}(r_{2}(x))
;

•

r_{1}+^{{\bf R}}r_{2}
is defined as

x\mapsto m(r_{1}(x),0,r_{2}(x))
;

•

0^{{\bf R}}
is the unary polynomial operation which is constant
0
;

•

1^{{\bf R}}
is the unary polynomial operation
x\mapsto x
.

For
r\in R
we define
f_{r}^{{\bf M}}(a):=r(a)
; in the following, we just write
ra
instead.
The algebra
{\bf M}
thus defined is indeed a module:

•

For every
x\in A=M
we have
x+0=m(x,0,0)=x=m(0,0,x)=0+x
, so
0
is the neutral element in
{\bf M}
.

•

For associativity, consider the term
t(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})
given by
((x_{1}+x_{2})+(x_{3}+x_{4}))
. Note that
t(0,0,b,c)=t(0,b,0,c)
for all
b,c\in A
.
Applying the term condition to the first argument of
t
, we obtain
t(a,0,b,c)=t(a,b,0,c)

for any
a\in A
. Hence,
a+(b+c)=(a+b)+c
.

•

For any
a,b\in A
we have
m(a,a,b)=m(b,a,a)
; the term condition applied to the middle argument yields

m(a,0,b)=m(b,0,a)
, showing that
a+b=b+a
.

•

To see that
-a=m(0,a,0)
is the inverse of
a
, consider the polynomial
t(x,u_{1},u_{2})=u_{1}+m(x,u_{2},0)
. For every
a\in A
we then have

t(a,a,a)=t(a,0,0)
. Applying the term condition to the first argument, we get

t(0,a,a)=t(0,0,0)
; showing that

a+(-a)=0
.

•

To show (
17
), let
r\in R
and consider the term

t(x,y):=r(x+y)-r(x)-r(y)
. Let
a,b\in A
. Note that

t(0,b)=0=t(0,0)
, and applying the term condition to the first argument yields

t(a,b)=t(a,0)=0
, which proves that scalar multiplication by
r
distributes over addition.

•

Let
r,s\in R
and
a\in A
.
Note that

(r+s)(a)=m(ra,0,sa)=ra+sa
, showing (
18
). Moreover,
r(s(a))=rs(a)
by definition, showing (
19
).

To show that
{\bf A}
and
{\bf M}
are polynomially equivalent, first observe that
every operation of
{\bf M}
has been defined by a polynomial over
{\bf A}
. Conversely, let
p
be an operation of arity
n
of
{\bf A}
.
We prove by induction on
n
that
p
is a polynomial operation of
{\bf M}
.
If
n=1
then consider the unary polynomial operation
r(x):=p(x)-p(0)
.
We have
r\in R
, and thus see that
p(x)=rx+p(0)

is indeed a polynomial operation of
{\bf M}
.
If
n>1
, let
t(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
be the polynomial

p(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n})-p(0,x_{2},\dots,x_{n})-p(x_{1},0,\dots,0)+p(0,0,\dots,0).

We have
t(0,a_{2},\dots,a_{n})=0=t(0,0,\dots,0)
for all
a_{2},\dots,a_{n}\in A
,
and by the term condition we get that

t(a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{n})=t(a_{1},0,\dots,0)=0
. So

p(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n})=p(0,x_{2},\dots,x_{n})+p(x_{1},0,\dots,0)-p(0,0,\dots,0);

the three polynomials on the right have less variables and by the induction hypothesis can be written as polynomials over
{\bf M}
, which shows that
p
can be written as a polynomial over
{\bf M}
as well.

(2)
implies
(3)
. The assumptions imply that there exists an abelian group
(A;+,-,0)
such that in particular the operation

m\colon(x,y,z)\mapsto x-y+z
is a
polynomial operation in
{\bf A}
.
We have to show that
m
is not only a polynomial operation, but
even a term operation of
{\bf A}
.
Let
t
be a term such that
t^{{\bf A}}(x,y,z,a_{1},\dots,a_{n})=x-y+z
for some constants
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in A
.
Since
{\bf A}
is affine,
t^{{\bf A}}
can be written in the form

(x,y,z,a_{1},\dots,a_{n})\mapsto x-y+z+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}a_{i}+\lambda_{0}

for
\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}\in A
.
Now consider the term

s(x,y,z):=t(x,t(y,y,y,y,\dots,y),z,y,\dots,y).

Note that

\displaystyle s^{{\bf A}}(x,y,z)

\displaystyle=x-(y+\sum\lambda_{i}y+\lambda_{0})+z+\sum\lambda_{i}y+\lambda_{0}

\displaystyle=x-y+z.

so indeed
m\in\Clo({\bf A})
.
Lemma
49.3
states that
m
is central.

(3)
implies
(4)
.
We first prove that for every
f\in\Clo({\bf A})
there exist
a\in A
and endomorphisms
e_{1},\dots,e_{n}

of
(A;+,-,0)

such that for all
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in A

f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}(x_{i})+a.

Define
a:=f(0,\dots,0)
and

e_{i}(x):=f(0,\dots,0,x,0,\dots,0)-a
for every
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
and all
x\in A
.
Then
e_{i}
is indeed an endomorphism of
(A;+,-,0)
, because by the assumption that
(x,y,z)\mapsto x-y+z
is central we have that

\displaystyle e_{i}(x-y)

\displaystyle=f(0,\dots,0,x-y,0,\dots,0)-a

\displaystyle=f(0,\dots,0,x,0,\dots,0)-f(0,\dots,0,y,0,\dots,y)+f(0,\dots,0)-a

\displaystyle=e_{i}(x)-e_{i}(y).

Moreover,

f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=f(x_{1},0,\dots,0)-f(0,\dots,0)+f(0,x_{2},\dots,x_{n})=e_{1}(x_{1})+f(0,x_{2},\dots,x_{n})

and by induction it follows that

f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=\sum e_{i}(x_{i})+a
.
In particular,
m(x,y,z)=e_{1}(x)+e_{2}(y)+e_{3}(z)+a
, for some endomorphisms
e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}
of
(A;+,-,0)
.
We now proceed as in the proof of Lemma
48.1
:
we have
m(0,0,0)=0
, and hence
a=0
. Moreover, for all
x\in A
we have

x=m(x,0,0)=e_{1}(x)
, and thus
e_{1}
is the identity endomorphism. Analogously we have that
e_{3}
must be the identity endomorphism. Finally,

m(x,x,0)=x+e_{2}(x)=0
, and therefore
e_{2}(x)=-x
. Hence,
m(x,y,z)=x-y+z
. So the assumptions imply that
m
is central.

(4)
implies
(1)
.
Suppose that
m
is central. We verify that
{\bf A}
satisfies the term condition. Let
t
be a term operation of
{\bf A}
and
let
x,y\in A
and
u,v\in A^{n}
be such that

t(x,u)=t(x,v)
. We have to show that

t(y,u)=t(y,v)
. And indeed,

\displaystyle t(y,u)

\displaystyle=m(t(y,u),t(x,u),t(x,v))

(since
m
is Maltsev)

\displaystyle=t(m(y,x,x),m(u_{1},u_{1},v_{1}),\dots,m(u_{n},u_{n},v_{n}))

(centrality)

\displaystyle=t(y,v).

Exercises.

184.

Show that if
(A;+,-,0)
is a group, and

{\bf A}=(A;m)
where
m
is the
Maltsev operation given by
(x,y,z)\mapsto x-y+z
, then

{\bf A}
is abelian
if and only if the group

(A;+,-,0)
is abelian.

185.

Show that subalgebras of abelian algebras are abelian.

186.

Show that a semilattice
(L;\wedge)
(Example
33
) is abelian if and only if
|L|=1
.

187.

Show that subalgebras of affine algebras are affine.

188.

A ring
{\bf R}
(Example
31
) is abelian in the sense of Definition
49.5

if and only if for all
x,y\in R
we have
x\cdot y=0
.

189.

Show that in the definition of the term condition (Definition
49.5
),

we could have equivalently phrased the condition for polynomial operations instead of term operations
t
. However, show that it is not sufficient to require the condition only for the operations of the algebra.

190.

Show that
({\mathbb{Q}};(x,y)\mapsto\frac{x+y}{2})
is idempotent abelian,

but has no Maltsev polynomial.

191.

Let
(A,+,-,0)
be a group. Show that
(x-y+z)\mapsto x-y+z

is central in
{\bf A}
if and only if

\{(x,y,u,v)\in A^{4}\mid x+y=u+v\}

is a subalgebra of
{\bf A}^{4}
.

192.

Let
(A,+,-,0)
be a group. Show that

(x,y,z)\mapsto x-y+z
is central in
{\bf A}
if and only if

for every
f\in\Clo({\bf A})
there exist
a\in A
and endomorphisms
e_{1},\dots,e_{n}

of
(A;+,-,0)

such that for all
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in A

f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}(x_{i})+a.

193.

Let
{\bf A}
be a group. Show that the structure
(A;\{(a,b,ab)\mid a,b\in A\})
has a Maltsev polymorphism if and only if
{\bf A}
is abelian.

194.

Generalising the previous exercise, let
{\bf A}
be an algebra. Show that
{\bf A}
has a Maltsev polymorphism (here we study polymorphisms of an algebra, not a relational structure!) if and only if
{\bf A}
is abelian.

51.1
The Congruence Condition

We close with a relational characterisation of abelianess (which for some authors is the official definition of abelianness).
Recall that
\Delta_{A}
denotes
\{(a,a)\mid a\in A\}
, and that
\Cg_{{\bf A}}(X)
denotes the smallest congruence of
{\bf A}
that contains
X
(see Lemma
47.3
).

Theorem 51.3
.

Let
{\bf A}
be an algebra and
\Delta:=\Delta_{A}
. Then the following are equivalent.

1.

{\bf A}
is abelian.

2.

\Delta
is a congruence class of
\Cg_{{\bf A}^{2}}(\Delta^{2})
.

3.

\Delta
is a congruence class of a congruence of
{\bf A}^{2}
.

Proof.

The implication
(2)\Rightarrow(3)
is trivial.
For the implication
(3)\Rightarrow(2)
,
suppose that
C
is a congruence of
{\bf A}^{2}
where
\Delta
is a congruence class. Since
C^{\prime}:=\Cg_{{\bf A}^{2}}(\Delta^{2})

contains
\Delta^{2}
we have that
\Delta
is contained in a congruence class of
C^{\prime}
. But since
C^{\prime}\subseteq C
,
this congruence class must be
\Delta
.

For the equivalence between
(1)
and
(2)
, recall from Lemma
47.3
that
\Cg_{{\bf A}^{2}}(\Delta^{2})

equals that symmetric transitive closure of

\{(p(u),p(v))\mid u,v\in\Delta,p\text{ a unary polynomial operation of }{\bf A}^{2}\}.

Note that every unary polynomial operation
p(x)
of
{\bf A}^{2}
, can be written as

f(x,{c_{1}\choose d_{1}},\dots,{c_{n}\choose d_{n}})

for some
c_{1},d_{1},\dots,c_{n},d_{n}\in A,n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
f\in\Clo({\bf A})
.
Hence,
\Delta
is a congruence class of
\Cg_{{\bf A}^{2}}(\Delta^{2})
if and only if for all
a,b\in A,c_{1},d_{1},\dots,c_{n},d_{n}\in A,n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and every term
t

t^{{\bf A}^{2}}({a\choose a},{c_{1}\choose d_{1}},\dots,{c_{n}\choose d_{n}})\in\Delta\text{ if and only if }t^{{\bf A}^{2}}({b\choose b},{c_{1}\choose d_{1}},\dots,{c_{n}\choose d_{n}})\in\Delta;

this is exactly the term condition for
{\bf A}
applied to the first argument of
t
.
∎

Example 52
.

Let
n\geq 1
. Let
{\bf A}
be
the algebra
({\mathbb{Z}}_{n};m)
, where
m\colon{\mathbb{Z}}_{n}^{3}\to{\mathbb{Z}}_{n}
is given by
(x,y,z)\mapsto x-y+z
. Then
{\bf A}^{2}
has the congruence
\theta
defined as follows:

\displaystyle((x_{1},x_{2}),(y_{1},y_{2}))\in\theta\;\Leftrightarrow\;(x_{1}-x_{2}=y_{1}-y_{2})

and clearly
\{(a,a)\mid a\in A\}
is a congruence class of
\theta
.

Proposition 52.1
.

Let
{\bf A}
be an algebra
with
R\leq{{\bf A}}^{3}
such that
for every
a\in A
and
i\in\{1,2,3\}
the binary relation defined by
\exists x_{i}(R(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\wedge x_{i}=a)
is the graph of an automorphism of
{\bf A}
.
Then
{\bf A}
is abelian.

Proof.

Note that the assumptions imply that
R
is the graph of a surjective binary operation
f\colon A^{2}\to A
.
Also note that
f
is central, i.e.,
f\colon{\bf A}^{2}\to{\bf A}
is a homomorphism, because
R\leq{\bf A}^{3}
.
Arbitrarily pick
a\in A
. Then
f^{-1}(a)
is the graph of an automorphism
\alpha
of
{\bf A}
.
Then
(x,y)\mapsto f(x,\alpha(y))
is central and its kernel
C
is a congruence of
{\bf A}^{2}
.
The congruence class
(a,a)/C
equals
\Delta_{A}
and the statement follows from Theorem
51.3
.
∎

53
Absorption

“The notion of absorption is, in a sense, complementary to abelianness”

(Barto and Kozik
[
12
]
)

Absorption theory is an important topic in universal algebra, developed by Marcin Kozik and Libor Barto, which has powerful applications for the study of homomorphism problems. It can be seen
as a tool to show the existence of certain solutions
in instances of a CSP. This section covers material that stems from
[
11
,
14
,
7
]
.

Definition 53.1

(Absorbing subalgebras)
.

Let
{\bf A}
be an algebra and
f\in\Clo({\bf A})
of arity
n
. A subalgebra
{\bf B}
of
{\bf A}

is called an
absorbing subalgebra of
{\bf A}
with respect to
f
, in symbols
{\bf B}\vartriangleleft_{f}{\bf A}
, if for all
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}

f(B\times B\times\underbrace{A}_{i}\times B\times\cdots\times B)\subseteq B,

i.e., if for all
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in A
we have

f(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})\in B
whenever all
but at most one out of
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}

are from
B
. If such an
f
exists
we say that
{\bf B}

absorbs

{\bf A}
,
and write
{\bf B}\vartriangleleft{\bf A}
.
A subalgebra
{\bf B}
of
{\bf A}
is called
n-absorbing

if
{\bf B}\vartriangleleft_{f}{\bf A}
for some
f\in\Clo({\bf A})
of arity
n
.

Since subalgebras are uniquely determined
by their domain, we also use the notation

B\vartriangleleft{\bf A}
if
B
is the domain of
an absorbing subalgebra
{\bf B}
of
{\bf A}
.
Note that if
{\bf A}
is idempotent, then
B\subseteq A
is
1
-absorbing if and only if
A=B
. Also note that if
B
is
n
-absorbing, then it is also
m
-absorbing for all
m\geq n
.
Hence,
2
-absorbing subalgebras will be particularly interesting; they are also called
binary absorbing
.
We say that
{\bf A}
is
absorption-free
if
{\bf A}
has no proper absorbing subuniverse.

Remark 53.2
.

Impatient readers who would like to see algorithmic applications of the concept of absorption can forward-consult Section
65.6
.

Example 54
.

The subuniverse
\{0\}
of the algebra
(\{0,1\};\wedge)
is absorbing with respect to the operation
\wedge
.
More generally, if
{\bf A}=(A;\wedge)
where
A
is finite and
\wedge
is a semilattice operation, then

\{\bigwedge A\}\vartriangleleft{\bf A}
.

Example 55
.

If
{\bf A}=(\{0,1\};\majority)
then both
\{0\}\vartriangleleft{\bf A}

and
\{1\}\vartriangleleft{\bf A}
.
More generally, in any algebra
{\bf A}
with a near unanimity term
t
(see Section
23.4
), every one-element subalgebra is absorbing with respect to
t^{{\bf A}}
.

Example 56
.

Let
p
be a prime, and let
{\bf A}=(\{0,\dots,p-1\};m)
be the algebra where
m(x,y,z):=x-y+z
and where
+
and
-
are the usual addition and subtraction modulo
p
. Then the only absorbing subuniverses are
\emptyset
and
A
, so
{\bf A}
is absorption-free.
Indeed, we already know that the only proper subuniverses of
{\bf A}
are of the form
\{a\}
, for
a\in A
(Exercise
146
).
However, if
t
it an
\{m\}
-term, then by induction on the term structure one can show that
t(a,\dots,a,x,a,\dots,a)=x
, which shows that
\{a\}
is not absorbing with respect to
t^{{\bf A}}
, and hence
\{a\}\leq{\bf A}
is not absorbing.

Following the presentation in
[
12
]
, we will prove in Proposition
56.2
below a converse to the statement from Example
55
.

Lemma 56.1
.

If
{\bf B},{\bf C}\vartriangleleft{\bf A}
then
{\bf A}
has a
term operation
f
such that

{\bf B},{\bf C}\vartriangleleft_{f}{\bf A}
.

Proof.

If
{\bf B}\vartriangleleft_{s}{\bf A}

and

{\bf C}\vartriangleleft_{t}{\bf B}

for some
s,t\in\Clo({\bf A})
, choose
f:=s*t
(Definition
42.1
).
∎

Proposition 56.2
.

Let
{\bf A}
be a finite algebra. If every one-element subset is the domain of an absorbing subalgebra
of
{\bf A}
, then
{\bf A}
has a near unanimity term.

Proof.

Since
{\bf A}
is finite we can use Lemma
56.1
to construct a single term operation

h
such that
{\bf B}\vartriangleleft_{h}{\bf A}
for every
one-element subalgebra
{\bf B}
. But then
h
must be a near unanimity operation.
∎

An operation
f\colon V^{n}\to V
is called
(fully) symmetric
if
f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\approx f(x_{\rho(1)},\dots,x_{\rho(n)})
for every permutation
\rho\in\Sym(\{1,\dots,n\})
.
The following example from
[
74
]
shows a structure
which has fully symmetric polymorphisms of all arities, but not totally symmetric polymorphisms of arity 3.

Example 57
.

Let
{\bf A}
be the algebra on the domain
A=\{-1,0,1\}
which has for every
k\geq 1
the
k
-ary operation
s_{k}
defined as follows:

s_{k}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }\frac{x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n}}{n}\in(-\frac{1}{3},\frac{1}{3})\\
1&\text{ if }\frac{x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n}}{n}\geq\frac{1}{3}\\
-1&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

Note that
s_{k}
is fully symmetric and idempotent. Note that for every
k\geq 1
,
the operation
s_{k}
preserves the following two relations

\displaystyle R_{+}

\displaystyle:=\{(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in A\mid x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3}\geq 1\}

\displaystyle R_{-}

\displaystyle:=\{(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in A\mid x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3}\leq-1\}

Suppose for contradiction that there exists
a totally symmetric operation
t\colon A^{3}\to A
which preserves
R_{+}
and
R_{-}
.
Then

t(1,1,-1)=t(1,-1,1)=t(-1,1,1)=t(1,-1,-1)=t(-1,1,-1)=t(-1,-1,1)=:c.

If we apply
t
to the three tuples
(1,1,-1),(1,-1,1),(-1,1,1)\in R_{+}
we obtain

(c,c,c)\in R_{+}
, hence
c=1
. The same argument applied to
R_{-}
instead of
R_{+}
shows that
c=-1
, a contradiction.

Note that
{\bf A}
has the proper subalgebras
\{-1\},\{0\},\{1\},\{-1,0\},\{0,1\}
. All of them are absorbing, witnessed by
s_{k}
for some large enough
k
.

57.1
Absorption Transfer

We start with some warm-up exercises concerning absorption. Throughout,
{\bf A}
denotes an algebra with a not necessarily finite domain.

Lemma 57.1
.

If
{\bf C}\vartriangleleft{\bf B}\vartriangleleft{\bf A}
then
{\bf C}\vartriangleleft{\bf A}
.

Proof.

If
{\bf B}\vartriangleleft_{t}{\bf A}
for some
t\in\Clo({\bf A})
and

{\bf C}\vartriangleleft_{s}{\bf B}
for some
s\in\Clo({\bf B})
, then
{\bf C}\vartriangleleft_{s*t}{\bf A}
.
∎

Corollary 57.2
.

If
{\bf B}\vartriangleleft{\bf A}
and
{\bf C}\vartriangleleft{\bf A}

then
(B\cap C)\vartriangleleft{\bf A}
.

Proof.

Note that
(B\cap C)\vartriangleleft{\bf C}
with respect
to the same term as
B\vartriangleleft{\bf A}
.
Now the statement follows from
Lemma
57.1
.
∎

Lemma 57.3
.

Let
\sim
be a congruence of
{\bf A}
and suppose that
B\vartriangleleft{\bf A}/{\sim}
. Then
\bigcup B\vartriangleleft{\bf A}
.

Proof.

If
B\vartriangleleft_{t^{{\bf A}/{\sim}}}({\bf A}/{\sim})
for some term
t(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
and
b_{1},\dots,b_{n}\in A
are such that all but one are from
\cup B
, then

t^{{\bf A}}(b_{1},\dots,b_{n})/_{\sim}=t^{{\bf A}/\sim}(b_{1}/_{\sim},\dots,b_{n}/_{\sim})\in B
. Hence,

\bigcup B\vartriangleleft_{t^{{\bf A}}}{\bf A}
.
∎

Example 58
.

Consider the Maltsev operation
m
on the set
\{0,1,2\}
from Example
27
. We claim that

(\{0,1,2\};m)
is absorption-free.
By symmetry, if
\{0,1\}
would be absorbing, then
\{1,2\}
would be as well, and by
Lemma
57.3
the subalgebra
\{1\}
would be absorbing.
The argument that
\{1\}
is not absorbing is as in Example
56
.
Again by symmetry, this shows that all proper
subalgebras are not absorbing.

Lemma 58.1
.

Let
{\bf R}
be a subalgebra of
{\bf A}\times{\bf B}
and let
S\vartriangleleft_{f}{\bf R}
.
Then
\pi_{1}(S)\vartriangleleft_{f}\pi_{1}(R)
.

Proof.

Suppose that
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in\pi_{1}(S)
,
c\in\pi_{1}(R)
, and
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.
Then there are
b_{1},\dots,b_{n}\in B
and
d\in B
such that
(a_{1},b_{1}),\dots,(a_{n},b_{n})\in S
and
(c,d)\in R
. Since
S\vartriangleleft_{f}{\bf R}

we have

f((a_{1},b_{1}),\dots,(a_{i-1},b_{i-1}),(c,d),(a_{i+1},b_{i+1}),\dots,(a_{n},b_{n}))\in S,

and hence
f(a_{1},\dots,a_{i-1},c,a_{i+1},\dots,a_{n})\in\pi_{1}(S)
, which proves that

\pi_{1}(S)\vartriangleleft_{f}\pi_{1}(R)
.
∎

Lemma 58.2
.

Let
{\bf A}
be an idempotent algebra and suppose that
{\bf A}^{2}
has a proper
n
-absorbing subalgebra. Then
{\bf A}
has a proper
n
-absorbing subalgebra as well.

Proof.

Suppose that
B
is a proper non-empty subset of
A^{2}
such that

B\vartriangleleft_{f}{\bf A}^{2}
for some
f\in\Clo({\bf A})^{(n)}
.
Note that
\pi_{1}(B)\vartriangleleft_{f}{\bf A}
by Lemma
58.1
, and that
\pi_{1}(B)
is non-empty. Hence,
if
\pi_{1}(B)\neq A
then we are done, so suppose that
\pi_{1}(B)=A
.
Since
B\neq A^{2}
, there exists an
a\in A
such that
B^{\prime}:=\pi_{2}\big(B\cap(\{a\}\times A)\big)\neq A
.
Since
\pi_{1}(B)=A
we have that
B^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
,
so it suffices to show that
B^{\prime}\vartriangleleft_{f}{\bf A}
. Let
b_{1},\dots,b_{n}\in B^{\prime}
,
i\leq n
, and
c\in A
. We have to show that

d:=f(b_{1},\dots,b_{i-1},c,b_{i+1},\dots,b_{n})\in B^{\prime}
. By the definition of
B^{\prime}
we have

(a,b_{1}),\dots,(a,b_{n})\in B
. Then

\displaystyle f((a,b_{1}),\dots,(a,b_{i-1}),(a,c),(a,b_{i+1}),\dots,(a,b_{n}))

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle(f(a,\dots,a),f(b_{1},\dots,b_{i-1},c,b_{i+1},\dots,b_{n}))=(a,d)\in B

since
B\vartriangleleft_{f}{\bf A}
, hence
d\in B^{\prime}
.
∎

Exercises.

195.

Let
{\bf A}
and
{\bf B}
be two algebras of the same signature, and let
R
be the domain of a subalgebra of
{\bf A}\times{\bf B}
. For
X\subseteq A
we define

\displaystyle X+R

\displaystyle:=\{b\in B\mid\exists a\in X\colon R(a,b)\}.

Prove that

•

if
X\leq{\bf A}
then
(X+R)\leq{\bf B}
;

•

if

R\leq{\bf A}\times{\bf B}
is subdirect and
X\vartriangleleft_{f}{\bf A}
, then
(X+R)\vartriangleleft_{f}{\bf B}
.

196.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a relational
\tau
-structure
and
\phi
a primitive positive
\tau
-formula.

Let
\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}
be a
\tau
-structure on the same domain such that
R^{\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}}\vartriangleleft R^{\mathfrak{A}}

for each
R\in\tau
.
If
\phi
defines
S
in
\mathfrak{A}
and defines
S^{\prime}
in
\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}
, then
S^{\prime}\vartriangleleft S
.

If
R^{\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}}\vartriangleleft_{f}R^{\mathfrak{A}}
for the same operation
f
for all
R\in\tau
,
then
R^{\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}}\vartriangleleft_{f}R^{\mathfrak{A}}
.

197.

Suppose that
{\bf A}\vartriangleleft{\bf B}\leq{\bf C}
. Do we then have
{\bf A}\vartriangleleft{\bf C}
?

58.1
Essential Relations

This section presents a relational characterisation of absorption that will be needed in Section
59.2
and in Section
63.2
.
The following
material is mostly from Barto and Kazda
[
9
]
.
Throughout,
{\bf A}
denotes an algebra with a not necessarily finite domain.

Definition 58.3
.

Let
B\leq{\bf A}
and
n\geq 1
.
Then
R\leq{\bf A}^{n}
is
B
-essential
if for every
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}

\displaystyle R\cap(B\times\dots\times B\times\underbrace{A}_{i}\times B\times\cdots\times B)

\displaystyle\neq\emptyset

\displaystyle\text{ and }\quad R\cap B^{n}

\displaystyle=\emptyset.

Note that if

B\leq{\bf A}
is a proper subuniverse and
{\bf A}
is idempotent, then
\{a\}
is
B
-essential for every

a\in A\setminus B
.

Lemma 58.4
.

Let
B\leq{\bf A}
. If there is no
B
-essential relation of arity
m
, then for every
n\geq m
there
is no
B
-essential relation of arity
n
.

Proof.

If
R\leq{\bf A}^{n}
is
B
-essential,
then
\pi_{1,\dots,n-1}(R\cap(A^{n-1}\times B))
is
B
-essential.
∎

Lemma 58.5
.

Let
{\bf A}
be an algebra with a term operation
t
of arity
m
such that

B\vartriangleleft_{t}{\bf A}
. Then there are no
B
-essential relations
R\leq{\bf A}^{m}
.

Proof.

Suppose for contradiction that
R\leq{\bf A}^{m}
is
B
-essential.
Then there are
a^{1},\dots,a^{m}\in A^{m}
such that

\{a^{i}_{1},\dots,a^{i}_{m}\}\setminus\{a^{i}_{i}\}\subseteq B
for every
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
. Therefore,
t(a^{1},\dots,a^{m})\in R\cap B^{m}
, because
B\vartriangleleft_{t}{\bf A}
, contrary to our assumptions.
∎

Proposition 58.6
.

Let
B\leq{\bf A}
and
R\leq{\bf A}^{n}
for
n\geq m-1
.
Suppose that
{\bf A}
has no
B
-essential relation of arity
m
and for every
I\in{\{1,\dots,n\}\choose m-1}
we have
\pi_{I}(R)\cap B^{m-1}\neq\emptyset
.
Then

R\cap B^{n}\neq\emptyset.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
n\geq m-1
. The base case
n=m-1
is immediate by the assumption applied for
I=\{1,\dots,n\}
. For the inductive step, suppose that
n\geq m
.
For every
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
define

R_{i}:=\pi_{[n]\setminus\{i\}}(R)\leq{\bf A}^{n-1}

and note that
\pi_{I}(R_{i})\cap B^{m-1}\neq\emptyset

for every
I\subseteq{\{1,\dots,n\}\setminus\{i\}\choose m-1}
.
Hence, by the inductive assumption
we have that

R_{i}\cap B^{n-1}\neq\emptyset.

Since
R
is not essential by Lemma
58.4

we therefore must have

R\cap B^{n}\neq\emptyset
.
∎

Corollary 58.7
.

Let
t\in\Clo({\bf A})^{(m)}
be such that

B\vartriangleleft_{t}{\bf A}
and let
R\leq{\bf A}^{n}
for
n\geq m-1
.
Suppose that for every
I\in{\{1,\dots,n\}\choose m-1}
we have
\pi_{I}(R)\cap B^{m-1}\neq\emptyset
. Then

R\cap B^{n}\neq\emptyset.

Proof.

Combine
Lemma
58.6
with
Lemma
58.5
.
∎

Lemma
58.5
has a converse (Proposition 16 in
[
9
]
); also see
[
98
,
99
]
.

Theorem 58.8
.

Let
m\geq 1
. A subalgebra

{\bf B}\leq{\bf A}

m
-absorbs
{\bf A}
if and only if there are no
B
-essential relations
R\leq{{\bf A}}^{m}
.

Proof.

The forward implication is Lemma
58.5
. For the converse,
suppose that
{\bf A}
has no
B
-essential relations of arity
m
. Let
{\bf F}\leq{\bf A}^{A^{m}}
be the free algebra generated by
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
in
\HSP({\bf A})

(see Section
38.3
).
For
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
, let
X_{i}:=B^{i-1}\times(A\setminus B)\times B^{m-i}
, and let
X:=X_{1}\cup\cdots\cup X_{m}
,
which will be used as index set of the relation
R
defined as follows:

R:=\pi_{X}(F)\leq{\bf A}^{X_{1}}\times\cdots\times{\bf A}^{X_{m}}.

Let
I\subseteq{X\choose m-1}
.
We claim that
\pi_{I}(R)\cap B^{m-1}\neq\emptyset
:
indeed,
by the pigeon-hole principle there exists
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
such that

I\cap X_{i}=\emptyset
.
Since
{\bf F}
contains
\pi^{m}_{i}

we have

R\cap(B^{X_{1}}\times\cdots\times B^{X_{i-1}}\times A^{X_{i}}\times B^{X_{i+1}}\times\cdots\times B^{X_{m}})\neq\emptyset

which shows the claim.

Therefore, Proposition
58.6
implies that

R\cap B^{n}\neq\emptyset
. By definition, any element of
R\cap B^{X}
can be extended to an element
t\in\mathfrak{F}
, and any such
t
is a term operation of
{\bf A}
of arity
m
which absorbs
B
.
∎

58.2
The Absorption Theorem

The presentation of this section is based on lecture notes of Libor Barto. The goal of this section is to show that finite idempotent
Taylor algebras (see Remark
43.20
)
must have some form of absorption; this idea will be formalised in the absorption theorem, Theorem
59.2
below, which is from
[
11
]
.

Definition 58.9
.

Let
{\bf A}
be an algebra. A subset

B\subseteq A
is called
projective in
{\bf A}
(in some papers called
cube term blocker

[
82
]
) if for every

f\in\Clo({\bf A})
of arity
n
there exists
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}

such that

\displaystyle f(A,A,\dots,A,\underbrace{B}_{\text{position $i$}},A,\dots,A)\subseteq B;

as usual, the term on the left stands for
\{f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\mid x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in A,x_{i}\in B\}
.

Note that subsets of
A
that are projective in
{\bf A}
are subuniverses of
{\bf A}
.
Recall the definition of minion homomorphisms from Section
43.1
.
Our starting point is the following theorem.

Theorem 58.10
.

Let
{\bf A}
be an algebra such that there is no minion homomorphism from
\Clo({\bf A})
to
\Proj
and let
B\subseteq A
be projective in
{\bf A}
. Then
B
2-absorbs
{\bf A}
.

Proof.

Since
B
is projective in
{\bf A}
,
for every
f\in\Clo({\bf A})

of arity
n
there exists
i_{f}\in[n]
such that

\displaystyle f(A,\dots,A,\underbrace{B}_{i_{f}},A,\dots,A)\subseteq B.

(31)

If
i_{f}
is
unique
for every
f\in\Clo({\bf A})
,
then
\Clo({\bf A})\to\Proj

given by
f\mapsto\pi^{n}_{i_{f}}
is a minion homomorphism:
indeed, let
\alpha\colon[n]\to[k]
and
f\in\Clo({\bf A})

and suppose that there exists a unique
i\in[n]
such that (
31
) holds.
Then
f_{\alpha}
is an operation of arity
k
such that

f_{\alpha}(A,\dots,A,\underbrace{B}_{\alpha(i)},A,\dots,A)\subseteq f(A,\dots,A,\underbrace{B}_{i},A,\dots,A)\subseteq B

and by assumption
\alpha(i)
is the only
index
j\in[k]
such that
f_{\alpha}(A,\dots,A,\underbrace{B}_{j},A,\dots,A)\subseteq B
. Hence,

\xi(f_{\alpha})=\pi^{k}_{\alpha(i)}=(\pi^{n}_{i})_{\alpha}=\xi(f)_{\alpha}

and
\xi
is a minion homomorphism.

So there exists
f\in\Clo({\bf A})
and
i\neq j
such that

\displaystyle f(A,\dots,A,\underbrace{B}_{i},A,\dots,A)

\displaystyle\subseteq B

\displaystyle\text{ and }\quad f(A,\dots,A,\underbrace{B}_{j},A,\dots,A)

\displaystyle\subseteq B.

Define
r(x,y):=f(x,\dots,x,\underbrace{y}_{j},x,\dots,x)

and observe that

B\vartriangleleft_{r}{\bf A}
.
∎

If
{\bf A}
does not have proper projective subuniverses,
then in exchange it must have
a term operation satisfying the following strong condition.

Definition 58.11
.

An operation
t\colon A^{n}\to A
is called
transitive
if for every
a\in A
and
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
we have

t(A,\dots,A,\underbrace{\{a\}}_{i},A,\dots,A)=A.

Clearly, if
|A|>1
, then a transitive operation must have arity at least two.
