The following theorem for the first time depends on
{\bf A}
having a finite domain.

Theorem 58.12
.

Let
{\bf A}
be a finite idempotent algebra without proper projective subuniverses.
Then
\Clo({\bf A})
contains a transitive operation.

Proof.

By assumption, for every proper subset
B
of
A
there exists
t_{B}\in\Clo({\bf A})
of arity
n
such that for every
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}

t_{B}(A,\dots,A,\underbrace{B}_{i},A,\dots,A)\not\subseteq B.

Using the star product and the idempotence of
{\bf A}
, Lemma
43.14
implies that we may suppose that there exists a single term
t
that works for all proper
B\subset A
.
Then

u:=\underbrace{t*\cdots*t}_{|A|\text{ times }}

is transitive, because for every
a\in A
and
j\in\{1,\dots,|A|\}

|\underbrace{t*\cdots*t}_{j\text{ times}}(A,\dots,A,\underbrace{\{a\}}_{i},A,\dots,A)|\geq j

and hence

u(A,\dots,A,\underbrace{\{a\}}_{i},A,\dots,A)=A
.
∎

Corollary 58.13
.

Let
{\bf A}
be a finite idempotent
Taylor algebra.
Then
{\bf A}
has a proper 2-absorbing subuniverse, or
\Clo({\bf A})
contains a transitive operation.

Proof.

Since
{\bf A}
is Taylor, there is no minion homomorphism from
\Clo({\bf A})
to
\Proj

(Theorem
43.15
; Remark
43.20
).
If
{\bf A}
has a proper projective subuniverse
B
, then
B
2-absorbs
{\bf A}

by Theorem
58.10
and we are done.
Otherwise, all proper subuniverses of
{\bf A}
are not projective, and
\Clo({\bf A})
contains a
transitive operation by Theorem
58.12
.
∎

We will now explore consequences of having a transitive term operation
for the existence of proper absorbing subuniverses.
Let
{\bf A}
and
{\bf B}
be algebras,
and let
R\subseteq A\times B
be a relation.

Figure 18:
Illustrations of
R\leq{\bf A}\times{\bf B}
with non-empty left center
C
(left side), and of
R\leq{\bf A}\times{\bf B}
which is linked (right side); none of the two examples is subdirect.

Definition 58.14

(left centre
9
9

9

There is no connection with the notion of centrality from Definition
49.1
.
)
.

The
left centre of
R

is the set

\{a\in A\mid(a,b)\in R\text{ for every }b\in B\}.

See Figure
18
, left side.

Proposition 58.15
.

Let
{\bf A}
and
{\bf B}
be idempotent algebras with the same signature and let

R\leq{\bf A}\times{\bf B}

with left centre
C
be such that for every
a\in A
there exists
b\in B
such that
(a,b)\in R
.
If there exists a term such that
t^{{\bf B}}
is transitive, then
C\vartriangleleft_{t^{{\bf A}}}{\bf A}
.

Proof.

If
C
is empty, then the statement is trivial, so suppose that
C
is non-empty.
Since
{\bf B}
is idempotent,
C\leq{\bf A}
. Let
i\in[n]
,
z_{1},\dots,z_{i-1},z_{i+1},\dots,z_{n}\in C
,
a\in A
, and

a^{\prime}:=t^{{\bf A}}(z_{1},\dots,z_{i-1},a,z_{i+1},\dots,z_{n}).

To show that
C\vartriangleleft_{t^{{\bf A}}}{\bf A}
we need to show that
a^{\prime}\in C
, i.e.,

(a^{\prime},b)\in R
for every

b\in B
. Arbitrarily choose
b\in B
.
By assumption, there exists
c\in B
such that
(a,c)\in R
.
By the transitivity of
t^{{\bf B}}
there are
d_{1},\dots,d_{i-1},d_{i+1},\dots,d_{n}\in B
such that

t^{{\bf B}}(d_{1},\dots,d_{i-1},c,d_{i+1},\dots,d_{n})=b
.
Note that
(z_{1},d_{1}),\dots,(z_{i-1},d_{i-1}),(z_{i+1},d_{i+1}),\dots,(z_{n},d_{n})\in R
since
z_{1},\dots,z_{i-1},z_{i+1},\dots,z_{n}
are from the left center of
R
.
Since
R\leq{\bf A}\times{\bf B}
,
we have that

(a^{\prime},b)=\big(t^{{\bf A}}(z_{1},\dots,z_{i-1},a,z_{i+1},\dots,z_{n}),t^{{\bf B}}(d_{1},\dots,d_{i-1},c,d_{i+1},\dots,d_{n})\big)\in R

and the proof is complete.
∎

Corollary 58.16
.

Let
{\bf A}
and
{\bf B}
be finite idempotent algebras with the same signature
such that
{\bf B}
is Taylor.
Let
R\leq{\bf A}\times{\bf B}
with left centre
C
be such that for every
a\in A
there exists
b\in B
such that
(a,b)\in R
. Then
{\bf B}
has a proper 2-absorbing subuniverse or
C\vartriangleleft{\bf A}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
{\bf B}
does not have a proper 2-absorbing subuniverse.
Then Corollary
58.13
implies that
{\bf B}
has a transitive term operation
t
. Hence, Proposition
58.15
implies that
C\vartriangleleft_{t}{\bf A}
.
∎

The relation
R
can be viewed as the edge relation of a bipartite graph
G_{R}
with color classes
A
and
B
(this perspective was already presented in Section
38.1
).

Definition 58.17

(Linked relations)
.

A relation
R\subseteq A\times B
is called
linked

if
G_{R}
is connected after removing
isolated vertices.

See Figure
18
on the right. Note that if
R
is a subdirect subalgebra of
{\bf A}\times{\bf B}
(Definition
38.2
)
then
G_{R}
has no isolated vertices. Also note that if
R
has a non-empty left centre, then it is linked (but not necessarily subdirect).
Recall the definition of
R^{-1}
from Exercise
96
.

Proposition 58.18
.

Let
{\bf A}
and
{\bf B}
be finite idempotent algebras
with the same signature
and let
R\leq{\bf A}\times{\bf B}
be with
empty left centre and such that
R^{-1}\circ R=B^{2}
.
Then there exists a subdirect

R^{\prime}\leq{\bf B}^{2}
whose left centre is a proper subuniverse of
{\bf B}
.

Before we go into the proof we consider an example.

Example 59
.

Suppose that
{\bf B}
has domain
B=\{1,2,3\}
and

{\bf B}^{2}
has the subuniverse
R:=\{(u,v)\in B^{2}\mid u\neq v\}
.
Clearly, the left centre of
R
is empty. Then

R^{\prime}:=\{(x,y)\mid\exists a(R(a,x)\wedge R(a,y)\wedge R(a,1))\}=\{(1,1),(1,2),(1,3),(2,1),(3,1)\}

is a subuniverse of
{\bf B}^{2}
(we use that
\{1\}
is a subuniverse), is subdirect,
and has the left centre
\{1\}
.

Figure 19:
An illustration for the definition of
S_{E}
in the proof Proposition
58.18
.

Proof of Proposition
58.18
.

For
D=\{d_{1},\dots,d_{k}\}\subseteq B

define

S_{D}:=\big\{(x,y)\in B^{2}\mid\exists a\big(R(a,x)\wedge R(a,y)\wedge R(a,d_{1})\wedge\cdots\wedge R(a,d_{k})\big)\big\}.

Then

•

S_{D}\leq{\bf B}^{2}
by the idempotence of
{\bf B}
,

•

S_{\emptyset}=R^{-1}\circ R=B^{2}
by assumption,
and

•

S_{B}=\emptyset
because the left centre of
R
is empty.

Let
D
be maximal such that
S_{D}=B^{2}
, and
let
E\subseteq B
and
b\in B
be a set such
that
E\setminus D=\{b\}
. See Figure
19
.
Let
C
be the left centre
of
S_{E}
.

•

C
contains
b
and hence is non-empty:
indeed, for any
y\in B
there exists

a\in A
witnessing that
(b,y)\in S_{D}=B^{2}
, i.e.,
R(a,b)
,
R(a,y)
, and
R(a,d)
for every
d\in D
.
Hence,
(b,y)\in S_{E}
and
b\in C
.

•

S_{E}\leq{\bf B}^{2}
is subdirect: since
C
is non-empty, for every
y\in B
there is
c\in C
such that
(y,c)\in S_{E}
and
(c,y)\in S_{E}
.

•

C
is a proper subset of
B
. Otherwise,
the centrality of
C
would imply that
S_{E}=B^{2}
, contrary to the choice of
D
and
E
.

Therefore,
R^{\prime}:=S_{E}
meets the requirements.
∎

Proposition 59.1
.

Let
{\bf A},{\bf B}
be finite idempotent algebras with the same signature and let
R\leq{\bf A}\times{\bf B}
be subdirect and linked such that
R\neq A\times B
.
Then at least one of the following cases applies.

•

R
has a non-empty left centre.

•

there exists a subdirect
R^{\prime}\leq{\bf B}^{2}
whose left centre
is a proper subuniverse of
{\bf B}
.

Proof.

Suppose that the left centre
C
of
R
is empty.
If
R^{-1}\circ R=B^{2}
then the statement follows from Proposition
58.18
.
Otherwise,

R^{-1}\circ R\leq{\bf B}^{2}
is subdirect, proper, and linked (Exercise
200
), so we may replace
{\bf A}
by
{\bf B}
and
R
by

R^{-1}\circ R
.
Since
R
is linked and subdirect, we have that

(R^{-1}\circ R)^{n}=B^{2}
for some
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Hence, if we repeat the argument, we eventually find
a proper, subdirect, and linked subuniverse
R
of
{\bf B}^{2}
such that
R^{-1}\circ R=B^{2}
.
If the left centre of
R
is non-empty it is a proper
subalgebra of
{\bf B}
and we are done.
Otherwise, the statement again follows from Proposition
58.18
.
∎

Theorem 59.2

(Absorption theorem
[
11
]
)
.

Let
{\bf A},{\bf B}
be finite idempotent algebras such that
{\bf B}
is Taylor.
Then
for every linked and subdirect
R\leq{\bf A}\times{\bf B}
one of the following is true:

1.

R=A\times B
;

2.

{\bf A}
has a proper absorbing subuniverse.

3.

{\bf B}
has a proper absorbing subuniverse.

Proof.

Suppose that

R\neq A\times B
because otherwise item 1 of the theorem holds and we are done.
Let
C
be the left centre of
R
.
Note that
C\neq A
because
R\neq A\times B
.

Suppose also that
{\bf B}
is absorption-free, because otherwise item 3 of the theorem holds.
Corollary
58.16
then implies that
C\vartriangleleft{\bf A}
.
If
C
is non-empty, then we have found a proper absorbing subuniverse of
{\bf A}
and item 2 of the theorem holds.
Otherwise, Proposition
59.1
implies that there
exists a subdirect
R^{\prime}\leq{\bf B}^{2}
whose left centre is a proper subuniverse of
{\bf B}
.
In this case,
{\bf B}
has a proper absorbing subuniverse by Corollary
58.16
, in contradiction to the assumption above.
∎

Exercises.

198.

Find a counterexample to the absorption theorem if we drop the assumption that
R
is linked.

199.

Let
{\bf A}
and
{\bf B}
be idempotent algebras and
R\leq{\bf A}\times{\bf B}
. Show that

the left center of
R
is a subalgebra of
{\bf A}
.

200.

Show that if
R\leq{\bf A}\times{\bf B}
is linked, then
R^{-1}\circ R\leq{\bf B}^{2}
is linked, too.

201.

Suppose that
{\bf A}
is a simple algebra. Then every subdirect

R\leq{\bf A}^{2}

is linked or the graph of an automorphism of
{\bf A}
.

Hint.
Consider
\bigcup_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}}(R\circ R^{-1})^{i}
.

202.

Let
{\bf A}
be a finite algebra
and
B\subseteq A
. Then

B
is a projective in
{\bf A}

if and only if
\Clo({\bf A})
preserves
for every
n

the relation
B[n]:=A^{n}\setminus(A\setminus B)^{n}.

203.

Let
{\bf A},{\bf B}
be a algebras and let
R\leq{\bf A}\times{\bf B}
be subdirect. Let
\theta_{A}
be the kernel of
\pi_{1}\colon R\to A
and let
\theta_{B}
be the kernel of
\pi_{2}\colon R\to B
.
Show that
R
is linked if and only if
\theta_{A}\vee\theta_{B}=1_{R}
.

204.

Let
R\leq{\bf A}^{2}
,
S\lhd R
, and
(a,a),(b,b)\in S
be such that
(a,b)\in R
.
Then there exists
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
(a,b)\in S^{k}
.

59.1
Abelianness Revisited

The fundamental theorem of abelian algebras (Theorem
51.2
) implies that every abelian algebra with a Maltsev term is affine.
In this section we considerably strengthen this theorem for finite idempotent algebras by replacing the assumption of having a Maltsev term by having a Taylor term (Corollary
59.8
).
This result follows from tame congruence theory (Hobby and McKenzie
[
63
]
; see the discussion in
[
14
]
);
the new proof based on absorption that we present here is from
[
14
]
; the presentation follows lecture notes of Libor Barto.

Definition 59.3
.

An algebra
{\bf A}
is called
hereditarily absorption-free (HAF)
if no subalgebra of
{\bf A}
has a proper absorbing subalgebra, i.e., whenever
{\bf C}
is a non-empty absorbing subalgebra of an subalgebra
{\bf B}
of
{\bf A}
, then
C=B
.

We will first prove that ‘HAF and Taylor implies Maltsev’ (Theorem
59.6
),
and then that ‘abelian implies HAF’ (Theorem
59.7
).
First we prove that HAF is closed under taking direct products.

Lemma 59.4
.

Let
{\bf A},{\bf B}
be idempotent hereditarily absorption-free algebras with the same signature. Then

{\bf A}\times{\bf B}
is hereditarily absorption-free.

Proof.

Suppose that
S\vartriangleleft R\leq{\bf A}\times{\bf B}
is non-empty. We have to show that

S=R
.
Let
(a,b)\in R
and consider the subalgebras
{\bf D}\leq{\bf C}\leq{\bf B}
given by

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle:=\{b^{\prime}\mid(a,b^{\prime})\in R\}\leq{\bf B}

(since
{\bf B}
is idempotent)

\displaystyle\text{ and }D

\displaystyle:=\{b^{\prime}\mid(a,b^{\prime})\in S\}\leq{\bf C}

\displaystyle\text{(since ${\bf B}$ is idempotent)}.

Claim 1.

D\neq\emptyset
.
Note that
\pi_{1}(S)\leq\pi_{1}(R)\leq{\bf A}
(for the notation, see the comments after Definition
24.1
).
We even have
\pi_{1}(S)\vartriangleleft\pi_{1}(R)
(Lemma
58.1
).
Since
{\bf A}
is HAF, we get
\pi_{1}(S)=\pi_{1}(R)
.
Since
a\in\pi_{1}(R)
, there must be
b^{\prime}\in B
such that
(a,b^{\prime})\in S
. Therefore,
b^{\prime}\in D\neq\emptyset
.

Claim 2.

D\vartriangleleft{\bf C}
.
By assumption, there exists a term operation
f\in\Clo({\bf R})^{(n)}
such that
S\vartriangleleft_{f}{\bf R}
.
Let
b_{1},\dots,b_{n}\in C
be such that all but one of them are from
D
. Then
f((a,b_{1}),\dots,(a,b_{n}))=(a,f(b_{1},\dots,b_{n}))\in S
since
{\bf A}
is idempotent and
S\vartriangleleft_{f}{\bf R}
. It follows that
f(b_{1},\dots,b_{n})\in D
.

By the assumption that
{\bf B}
is HAF, we must have
D=C
and hence

(a,b)\in S
. Since
(a,b)\in R
was chosen arbitrarily, this implies that
R=S
.
∎

Corollary 59.5
.

The class of idempotent HAF algebras of fixed signature
\tau
forms a pseudo-variety.

Proof.

By definition of HAF, the class is closed under subalgebras. Closure under finite products has been established in Lemma
59.4
. Closure under homomorphic images is by Lemma
57.3
.
∎

Theorem 59.6

(Theorem 1.4 in
[
14
]
)
.

Let
{\bf A}
be a finite idempotent
Taylor algebra.
If
{\bf A}
is hereditarily absorption-free, then
{\bf A}
has a Maltsev term.

Proof.

Let
{\bf F}\in\HSPfin({\bf A})
be the free algebra
over two generators
x,y
(see Section
38.3
). It follows from Corollary
59.5
that
{\bf F}
is HAF.
Let
{\bf R}
be the subalgebra of
{\bf F}^{2}
generated by
(x,y)
,
(x,x)
, and
(y,x)
.

Claim 1.

R\leq{\bf F}^{2}
is subdirect.
Every element of
F
can be written as
t(x,y)
for some term
t
, and
since
(x,y)\in R
and
(y,x)\in R
we have that

(t^{{\bf F}}(x,y),t^{{\bf F}}(y,x))\in R
. A similar statement holds for the second argument of
R
. This shows
that
{\bf R}
is
a subdirect subalgebra of
{\bf F}^{2}
.

Claim 2.

R
is linked.
Every element of

R
can be written as

s^{{\bf R}}((x,y),(x,x),(y,x))=(s^{{\bf F}}(x,x,y),s^{{\bf F}}(y,x,x))

for some term
s
.
Since
(x,x),(y,x)\in R
we
have that

(s^{{\bf F}}(x,x,y),s^{{\bf F}}(x,x,x))\in R

and since
(x,y),(x,x)\in R
we have

(s^{{\bf F}}(x,x,x),s^{{\bf F}}(y,x,x))\in R
.
Note that
s^{{\bf F}}(x,x,x)=x
by the idempotence of
{\bf A}
and
{\bf F}
, and thus between any two elements of
F
there is a path of length at most three in the bipartite graph
G_{R}
of
R
, which proves the claim.

Since
{\bf F}\in\SP({\bf A})
(Proposition
38.9
) and
\Clo({\bf A})
has no minion homomorphism to
\Proj
, neither has
{\bf F}
(Proposition
42.8
).
Since
{\bf F}
has no proper non-empty absorbing subalgebra,
Theorem
59.2

implies that
R=F\times F
.
Let
m
be a term such that

m^{{\bf F}}((x,y),(x,x),(y,x))=(y,y)
. Then
m^{{\bf A}}
is a Maltsev operation.
∎

The following is a special case of Lemma 4.1 in
[
14
]
.

Theorem 59.7
.

Let
{\bf A}
be a finite idempotent algebra. If
{\bf A}
is abelian then
{\bf A}
is hereditarily absorption-free.

Proof.

Since every subalgebra of an abelian algebra is abelian (Exercise
185
), it suffices to show that if
{\bf B}\vartriangleleft{\bf A}
, then
B=A
.
We will show that if
{\bf B}\vartriangleleft_{t}{\bf A}
for some
n
-ary term operation
t\in\Clo({\bf A})
, for
n\geq 2
, then
{\bf B}\vartriangleleft_{s}{\bf A}
for some
n-1
-ary
s
. This is enough, because if
{\bf B}\vartriangleleft_{s}{\bf A}
and
s
is unary, then
s
must be the identity by the idempotence of
{\bf A}
, hence
B=A
.
Define the term
t_{m}(\bar{x},y)
, where
\bar{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1})
, as follows

t_{m}(\bar{x},y):=\underbrace{t(\bar{x},t(\bar{x},\dots,t(\bar{x}}_{m\text{ times}},y))).

Note that
{\bf B}\vartriangleleft_{t_{m}}{\bf A}
for every
m\geq 1
.

Claim 1.
For
m=|A|!
we have

\displaystyle t_{m}(\bar{x},t_{m}(\bar{x},y))=t_{m}(\bar{x},y).

(32)

To see this, define
r_{\bar{x}}\colon A\to A
by
r_{\bar{x}}(y):=t(\bar{x},y)
. Then note that

t_{m}(\bar{x},y)=\underbrace{r_{\bar{x}}\circ\cdots\circ r_{\bar{x}}}_{m\text{ times}}(y)

and observe that (see Exercise
117
)

\underbrace{r_{\bar{x}}\circ\cdots\circ r_{\bar{x}}}_{2m\text{ times}}(y)=\underbrace{r_{\bar{x}}\circ\cdots\circ r_{\bar{x}}}_{m\text{ times}}(y).

Claim 2.

{\bf B}\vartriangleleft_{s}{\bf A}
for
s\colon A^{n-1}\to A
defined by

s(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1}):=t_{m}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1},x_{n-1}).

Let
a\in A
and
b_{1},\dots,b_{n-2}\in B
.
Clearly,
s(b_{1},\dots,b_{i},a,b_{i+1},\dots,b_{n-2})\in B
for all
i<n-1
, since
B\vartriangleleft_{t}{\bf A}
.
We have to verify that

s(b_{1},\dots,b_{n-2},a)\in B
.
From (
32
) we obtain that

t_{m}(b_{1},\dots,b_{n-2},b_{n-1},t_{m}(b_{1},\dots,b_{n-2},b_{n-1},a))=t_{m}(b_{1},\dots,b_{n-2},b_{n-1},a)

and since
{\bf A}
is abelian, we may apply the term condition to the term
t_{m}
at the
(n-1)
-st argument and obtain

t_{m}(b_{1},\dots,b_{n-2},a,t_{m}(b_{1},\dots,b_{n-2},b_{n-1},a))=t_{m}(b_{1},\dots,b_{n-2},a,a).

The right hand side of this equation equals
s(b_{1},\dots,b_{n-2},a)
, and the left hand side is contained in
B
since
B\vartriangleleft_{t_{m}}{\bf A}
.
∎

Corollary 59.8
.

Let
{\bf A}
be a finite idempotent abelian Taylor algebra.
Then
{\bf A}
is affine.

Proof.

If
{\bf A}
is abelian, then by Theorem
59.7

it is hereditarily absorption-free.
Theorem
59.6

implies that
{\bf A}
has a Maltsev term
m
.
Then Theorem
51.2
implies that

{\bf A}
is affine.
∎

Exercises.

205.

Let
{\bf A}:=(A,+,-,0)
be a group
for
A=\{a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\}
.
Show that
\Csp(A;+,a_{1},\dots,a_{n})

is in P if
{\bf A}
is abelian, and is
NP-hard otherwise.
10
10

10

This result is due to Goldmann and Russell
[
58
]
; it can be derived relatively easily from the results in this text. Hint: Combine Corollary
43.18
,
Proposition
52.1
, Corollary
59.8
, and
Theorem
26.1
.

59.2
Ternary Absorption

It will be convenient later to work with ternary absorbing (i.e., 3-absorbing) subalgebras instead of absorbing subalgebras with respect to terms of unbounded arity; this will in particular help in some applications of the absorption theorem in Section
63
.
The results in this section are from
[
99
]
and the presentation is based on
[
98
]
.

Definition 59.9
.

We say that
C\vartriangleleft{\bf A}
is
centrally absorbing
, written

{\bf C}\vartriangleleft_{Z}{\bf A}
, if

(a,a)\notin\langle(\{a\}\times C)\cup(C\times\{a\})\rangle_{{\bf A}^{2}}

for every
a\in A\setminus C
.

See Figure
20
.

Figure 20:
An illustration for the definition of central absorption (Definition
59.9
).

Example 60
.

The absorbing subuniverse
\{0\}
of

{\bf A}:=(\{0,1\};\majority)
(Example
54
) is centrally absorbing, because

(1,1)\notin\langle(0,1),(1,0)\rangle_{{\bf A}^{2}}=\{(0,1),(1,0)\}.

The absorbing subuniverse
\{0\}
of
{\bf B}:=(\{0,1\};\wedge)
(Example
55
) is centrally absorbing, because
(1,1)\notin\langle(0,1),(1,0)\rangle_{{\bf B}^{2}}=\{(0,0),(0,1),(1,0)\}
.

The next example shows an algebra with an absorbing subuniverse which is
not
centrally absorbing.

Example 61
.

Let
{\bf A}:=(\{0,1\};\wedge,\vee)
be the square of the 2-element lattice (Example
34
).
Note that
\{0\}
is absorbing with respect to
\wedge
. However,
\langle\{(0,1),(1,0)\}\rangle=\{0,1\}^{2}
contains
(1,1)
,
so
\{(0,0)\}
is not centrally absorbing.

We have the following transfer principle for centrally absorbing subalgebras.

Lemma 61.1
.

Let
\sim
be a congruence of
{\bf A}
and
suppose that
C\vartriangleleft_{Z}{\bf A}/{\sim}
.
Then
\bigcup C\vartriangleleft_{Z}{\bf A}
.

Proof.

We already know that
B:=\bigcup C\vartriangleleft{\bf A}
(Lemma
57.3
).
Let
a\in A\setminus B
.
Suppose for contradiction that
(a,a)\in\langle(\{a\}\times B)\cup(B\times\{a\})\rangle_{{\bf A}^{2}}
. Then there
are a term
t(x_{1},\dots,x_{k},y_{1},\dots,y_{l})
and
u_{1},\dots,u_{k},v_{1},\dots,v_{l}\in B

such that

(f^{{\bf A}}(a,\dots,a,v_{1},\dots,v_{l}),f^{{\bf A}}(u_{1},\dots,u_{k},a,\dots,a))=(a,a)
.
Note that
a/_{\sim}\notin B/_{\sim}=C

and that

\displaystyle(a/_{\sim},u_{1}/_{\sim}),\dots,(a/_{\sim},u_{k}/_{\sim})

\displaystyle\in\{a/_{\sim}\}\times C

\displaystyle(v_{1}/_{\sim},a/{\sim}),\dots,(v_{l}/_{\sim},a/_{\sim})

\displaystyle\in C\times\{a/_{\sim}\}

Thus,

\displaystyle(a/_{\sim},a/_{\sim})=\;

\displaystyle(f^{{\bf A}}(a,\dots,a,v_{1},\dots,v_{l}),f^{{\bf A}}(u_{1},\dots,u_{k},a,\dots,a))/{\sim}

\displaystyle\in\langle(\{a/_{\sim}\}\times C)\cup(C\times\{a/_{\sim}\})\rangle_{({\bf A}/_{\sim})^{2}}

which contradicts the assumption that
C\vartriangleleft_{Z}{\bf A}/{\sim}
.
∎

A more interesting source of centrally absorbing subalgebras
is the following proposition.

Proposition 61.2
.

Let
{\bf A}
and
{\bf B}
be finite idempotent
algebras such that
{\bf B}
is Taylor and has no proper 2-absorbing subuniverses.
Let
R\leq{\bf A}\times{\bf B}
with left center
C

be such that for every
a\in A
there exists
b\in B
such that
(a,b)\in R
. Then
C\vartriangleleft_{Z}{\bf A}
.

Proof.

By Corollary
58.16
we have that
C\vartriangleleft{\bf A}
. If
C
is not centrally absorbing, then for some
n,m\in{\mathbb{N}}
there exists
a\in A\setminus C
and
a term operation
t
of
{\bf A}
of arity
n+m

and
c_{1},\dots,c_{m},d_{1},\dots,d_{n}\in C

such that

t(a,\dots,a,c_{1},\dots,c_{m})=a=t(d_{1},\dots,d_{n},a,\dots,a).

Note that
\{a\}+R
(using the terminology from Exercise
195
) is a proper subalgebra of
{\bf B}
: we have
\{a\}+R\neq B
because
a\notin C
, and
\{a\}+R\neq\emptyset
by assumption.
Moreover,
\{a\}+R

is 2-absorbing with respect to
f
given by

f(x,y):=t(\underbrace{x,\dots,x}_{n},\underbrace{y,\dots,y}_{m}):

if
b\in\{a\}+R
and
u\in B
, note that

(c_{1},u),\dots,(c_{m},u)\in R
and
(a,b)\in R
,
and hence

\displaystyle f(b,u)=\;

\displaystyle t(b,\dots,b,u,\dots,u)

\displaystyle\in\{

\displaystyle t(a,\dots,a,c_{1},\dots,c_{m})\}+R=\{a\}+R.

Similarly,
f(u,b)=t(u,\dots,u,b,\dots,b)\in\{t(d_{1},\dots,d_{n},a,\dots,a)\}+R=\{a\}+R
.
This contradicts the assumption that
{\bf B}
has no proper 2-absorbing subuniverses.
∎

Interestingly, centrally absorbing subalgebras are 3-absorbing (Proposition
61.4
).
To prove this result, we need the following lemma about essential relations (Definition
58.3
).

Lemma 61.3

(Essential doubling)
.

Let
{\bf A}
be finite idempotent and let
C\vartriangleleft_{Z}{\bf A}
.
Suppose that
R\leq{\bf A}^{n}
, for
n\geq 3
, is
C
-essential.
Then there exists
R^{\prime}\leq{\bf A}^{2n-2}
which is
C
-essential.

Proof.

From all relations
R
that satisfy the assumptions given in the lemma for fixed
n
, choose
R
such that
B\leq{\bf A}
given by

B:=\pi_{n}(R\cap(C^{n-1}\times A))

has minimal size. Note that
B
is non-empty
and disjoint from
C
by the assumption that
R
is
C
-essential.
Also note that for every
b\in B
we have that
B^{\prime}:=\langle C\cup\{b\}\rangle_{{\bf A}}
contains
B
. To see this, suppose for contradiction that
d\in B\setminus B^{\prime}
.
Then we could replace

R
by the relation
\tilde{R}:=R\cap(A^{n-1}\times B^{\prime})
. Note that
\tilde{R}
is
C
-essential, and that

\pi_{n}(\tilde{R}\cap(C^{n-1}\times A))\subseteq B^{\prime}
does not contain
d
, in contradiction to the choice of
R
and
B
.

Pick
b\in B
and define

S:=\langle(\{b\}\times C)\cup(C\times\{b\})\rangle_{{\bf A}^{2}}.

Finally, let
R^{\prime}\leq{\bf A}^{2n-2}
be given as the set of all tuples
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1},y_{1},\dots,y_{n-1})

that satisfy

\displaystyle\exists x_{n},y_{n}\big(R(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\wedge S(x_{n},y_{n})\wedge R(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})\big).

(33)

We verify that
R^{\prime}
is
C
-essential.
First, for
i\in[2n-2]
, we need to show that

\displaystyle R^{\prime}\cap(C^{i-1}\times A\times C^{2n-2-i})\neq\emptyset.

(34)

If
i\in[n-1]
, we may choose
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in R\cap(C^{i-1}\times A\times C^{n-i})
since
R
is
C
-essential, and we may choose

(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})\in R\cap(C^{n-1}\times\{b\})

since
b\in B
. Note that
(x_{n},y_{n})=(x_{n},b)\in S
, so
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
satisfies the quantifier-free part of (
33
), which proves (
34
).
If
i\in\{n,\dots,2n-2\}
then the proof is analogous.

Second, we need to show that

R^{\prime}\cap C^{2n-2}=\emptyset,

which is equivalent to

S\cap B^{2}=\emptyset.

Suppose for contradiction that there is
(b_{1},b_{2})\in S\cap B^{2}
.
As we have observed earlier,
b\in\langle C\cup\{b_{1}\}\rangle_{{\bf A}}
.
Hence, for some
k\geq 1
there exists
f\in\Clo({\bf A})^{(k)}
and
d_{2},\dots,d_{k}\in C
such that
b=f(b_{1},d_{2},\dots,d_{k})
.
Let
b_{2}^{\prime}:=f(b_{2},b,\dots,b)\in B
.
Since
(b_{1},b_{2})\in S
and
(d_{2},b),\dots,(d_{k},b)\in S
we have that
(b,b_{2}^{\prime})\in S
.
We also have

b\in\langle C\cup\{b_{2}^{\prime}\}\rangle_{{\bf A}}
by the same argument as above,
and hence there exists
f^{\prime}\in\Clo({\bf A})^{(\ell)}
and

e_{2},\dots,e_{\ell}\in C
such that
b=f^{\prime}(b_{2}^{\prime},e_{2},\dots,e_{\ell})
.
Since
(b,b_{2}^{\prime}),(b,e_{2}),\dots,(b,e_{\ell})\in S
we have that
(f^{\prime}(b,b,\dots,b),f^{\prime}(b_{2}^{\prime},e_{2},\dots,e_{\ell}))=(b,b)\in S
. This contradicts the assumption that

C\vartriangleleft_{Z}{\bf A}
, which completes the verification that
R^{\prime}
is
C
-essential.
∎

Proposition 61.4
.

Let
{\bf A}
be finite idempotent such that
{\bf C}\vartriangleleft_{Z}{\bf A}
.
Then
{\bf C}
3-absorbs
{\bf A}
.

Proof.

Suppose for contradiction that

{\bf C}
does not 3-absorb
{\bf A}
. Then
there exists a
C
-essential relation
R\leq{\bf A}^{3}
by Theorem
58.8
.
Applying Lemma
61.3
sufficiently many times we may obtain
C
-essential relations of arbitrarily large arity.
Then Theorem
58.8
implies that

{\bf C}
is not absorbing, contrary to our assumptions.
∎

Combining these results with the proof from Section
58.2
, we
obtain a strengthened form of the Absorption Theorem (Theorem
59.2
).

Theorem 61.5
.

Let
{\bf A}
and
{\bf B}
be finite idempotent algebras with the same signature such that

{\bf B}
is Taylor.
Then
for every linked and subdirect
R\leq{\bf A}\times{\bf B}
one of the following is true:

1.

R=A\times B
;

2.

{\bf A}
or
{\bf B}
has a proper binary or centrally absorbing subuniverse.

Proof.

Suppose that

R\neq A\times B
, because otherwise item 1 of the theorem holds and we are done.
Let
C
be the left centre of
R
.
Note that
C\neq A
because
R\neq A\times B
.

Suppose that
{\bf B}
does not have a proper binary absorbing subuniverse.
Then Corollary
58.16
implies that
C\vartriangleleft{\bf A}
.
If
C
is non-empty, then by Proposition
61.2
we have found a proper centrally absorbing subuniverse of
{\bf A}
.

Otherwise, if
C=\emptyset
, then Proposition
59.1

implies that there
exists a subdirect
R^{\prime}\leq{\bf B}^{2}

whose left centre
C^{\prime}
is a proper absorbing subuniverse of
{\bf B}
. By Proposition
61.2

it is even centrally absorbing.
∎

61.1
Paper, Scissors, Stone

This section describes a fundamental example of a three-element algebra which shows some interesting behaviour and which provides important intuition for the abstract results in the following sections.
On the one hand, it is absorption-free, but on the other hand it is not hereditarily absorption-free.

Definition 61.6

(Paper-Scissors-Stone algebra)
.

Let
{\bf A}
be the algebra

with the domain

A:=\{0,1,2\}

and let

\cdot\colon A^{2}\to A

be the binary operation

given by the multiplication table on the right.

\cdot

0

1

2

0

0

1

0

1

1

1

2

2

0

2

2

Note that
{\bf A}
has the automorphism

\rho\colon x\mapsto x+1\mod 3.

Let
C_{3}:=\{(a,\rho(a))\mid a\in A\}
be the binary relation on
A
which denotes the graph of
\rho
. All three 2-element subsets of
A
are subuniverses of the algebra
{\bf A}=(A;\cdot)
, and in each of the corresponding subalgebras the operation
\cdot
denotes a semilattice operation; however,
\cdot
itself is not a semilattice operation.
We will see below (see Remark
61.10
) that none of the proper subalgebras of
{\bf A}
is absorbing.
However,
\{1\}
is a proper absorbing subuniverse of the subalgebra of
{\bf A}
with domain
\{0,1\}
, so
{\bf A}
is not HAF, and in particular not Abelian
59.7
.
Note that for any
a,b\in A

\displaystyle(a\cdot\rho^{-1}(b))\cdot b=b.

(35)

The algebra
{\bf A}
is simple. Indeed, if
C
is a congruence which contains
(0,1)
, then it must also contain
(0,1)\cdot(2,2)=(0,2)
,
and therefore also
(1,0)
and
(2,0)
by symmetry. By similar reasoning we conclude that
C=A^{2}
, which shows that
{\bf A}
has no proper congruences.

We first present an interesting relational description of
\Clo({\bf A})
.
Note that
\Inv({\bf A})
also contains the relation

R_{3}^{=}:=\big\{(x,y,z)\in A^{3}\mid x\in\{0,1\}\wedge(x=0\Rightarrow y=z)\big\}.

The relation
R_{3}^{=}\leq{\bf A}^{3}
is not subdirect, because the first argument cannot take value
2
.

Lemma 61.7
.

Let
R\leq{{\bf A}}^{n}
, for
n\geq 1
, be subdirect. Then
R

can be defined by a conjunction of atomic formulas over
(A;C_{3})
.

Proof.

Our proof is by induction on
n
.
For
n=1
we have
R=A
and hence
R
can be defined by
x=x
.
If
n=2
, then
R
is the graph
of an automorphism of
{\bf A}
or linked, because
{\bf A}
is simple (Exercise
201
).
In the first case, either
C_{3}(x,y)
,
C_{3}(y,x)
, or
x=y
defines
R
, and we are done, so let us assume that
R
is linked.

Claim.

R=A^{2}
. Indeed, if
(u,v)\in A^{2}
,
then the linkedness of
R
implies that
(u,v)\in R
or there exists a path

p_{1},\dots,p_{2k}\in A
for
k\geq 1
such that

(u,p_{1}),(p_{2},p_{1}),(p_{2},p_{3}),\dots,(p_{2k},v)\in R
. In the first case there is nothing to be shown. Otherwise, choose
k
as small as possible.
If
k=1
, we may assume that
p_{2}\neq u
and
p_{1}\neq v
, because otherwise we are in the first case.
Let
a,b\in A
be such that
\{u,p_{2},a\}=A=\{v,p_{1},b\}
. Since
R
is subdirect, there exist
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in A
such that
(a,a^{\prime}),(b^{\prime},b)\in R
.

Note that
(u,p_{1})\cdot(p_{2},v)\in\{(u,v),(u,p_{1}),(p_{2},p_{1}),(p_{2},v)\}
.
If
(u,p_{1})\cdot(p_{2},v)=(u,v)
then
(u,v)\in R
, contrary to the minimal choice of
k
.
If
(u,p_{1})\cdot(p_{2},v)=(p_{2},v)
, we consider the following subcases.

1.

a^{\prime}=v
. Then
(a,a^{\prime})\cdot(u,p_{1})=(u,a^{\prime})=(u,v)\in R
, contradiction.

2.

a^{\prime}=p_{1}
. Then
(a,a^{\prime})\cdot(p_{2},v)=(a,v)\in R
, and we are in the first subcase.

3.

a^{\prime}=b
. Then
(a,a^{\prime})\cdot(p_{2},p_{1})=(a,p_{1})\in R
and we are in the second subcase.

The case that
(u,p_{1})\cdot(p_{2},v)=(u,p_{1})
is similar.
Finally, suppose that
(u,p_{1})\cdot(p_{2},v)=(p_{2},p_{1})
. Again, we break into subcases.

1.

a^{\prime}=v
and
b^{\prime}=u
. Then

(a,a^{\prime})\cdot(b^{\prime},b)=(u,v)\in R
, a contradiction.

2.

a^{\prime}=p_{1}
and
b^{\prime}=p_{2}
. Then
(a,a^{\prime})\cdot(b^{\prime},b)=(a,b)
and
(a,b)\cdot(p_{2},v)=(a,v)\in R
. Moreover,
(u,p_{1})\cdot(a,b)=(u,b)\in R
.
Hence,
(a,v)\cdot(u,b)=(u,v)\in R
,
and we are done.

3.

a^{\prime}=v
and
b^{\prime}=p_{2}
. Then
(p_{2},p_{1})\cdot(a,a^{\prime})=(a,p_{1})\in R
and we are in subcase 2.

4.

a^{\prime}=p_{1}
and
b^{\prime}=u
. Then

(b^{\prime},b)\cdot(p_{2},p_{1})=(p_{2},b)\in R
and we are again in subcase 2.

5.

a^{\prime}=b
. Then
(a,a^{\prime})\cdot(p_{2},v)=(a,v)\in R
and
(a,a^{\prime})\cdot(u,p_{1})=(u,a^{\prime})\in R
, and we are in subcase number one.

Finally, if
k\geq 2
, then we may assume that
\{u,p_{2},p_{4}\}=A=\{v,p_{1},p_{3}\}
.
Then either
(u,p_{1})\cdot(p_{4},p_{3})

or
(u,p_{1})\cdot(p_{4},v)
is from

\{(u,v),(u,p_{3}),(p_{4},p_{1})\}
, and in each case we obtain a contradiction to the minimal choice of
k
.
This concludes the proof of the claim.

Now consider the case
n\geq 3
. If
R(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
implies
C_{3}(x_{i},x_{j})
for some
\{i,j\}\in{[n]\choose 2}
, then
R
has the definition
C_{3}(x_{i},x_{j})\wedge\psi
in
\mathfrak{A}
, where

\psi
is the definition of
\pi_{[n]\setminus\{j\}}(R)
in
\mathfrak{A}
, which exists by inductive assumption. Similarly we can treat the case that
x_{i}=x_{j}
is implied instead of
C_{3}(x_{i},x_{j})
.
Otherwise, we will show that
R=A^{n}
. Let
t\in A^{n}
. For any
a\in A
, the
(n-1)
-ary relation
R_{a}:=\{\bar{x}\mid(\bar{x},a)\in R\}
is
is preserved by
\cdot
.
Moreover, we will prove that it is subdirect. Indeed, let
b\in A
. Note that the binary relation
\pi_{2,3}(R)
equals
A^{2}
by the case
n=2
, and hence in particular contains
(b,a)
. Therefore, there exists
c^{\prime}\in A^{n-2}
such that
(c^{\prime},b,a)\in R
, and thus
(c^{\prime},b)\in R_{a}
. Similar arguments apply to the other arguments of
R_{a}
, showing that
R_{a}\leq{\bf A}^{n-1}
is subdirect.

First consider the case
n=3
. Since
R_{t_{3}}\leq{{\bf A}}^{3}
is subdirect, by the case
n=2
the
formula
R_{t_{3}}
equals
A^{2}
,
=_{A}
,
C_{3}
,
or
\{(y,x)\mid(x,y)\in C_{3}\}
.
In any case,
R_{t_{3}}
contains
(t_{1},t^{\prime}_{2})
and
(t_{1}^{\prime},t_{2})
for some
t_{1}^{\prime},t_{2}^{\prime}\in A
.
If
t_{1}^{\prime}=t_{1}
or
t_{2}^{\prime}=t_{2}
,
then
t\in R
and we are done.
If
t_{1}^{\prime}=\rho^{-1}(t_{1})
and
t_{2}^{\prime}=\rho^{-1}(t_{2})
, then
(t^{\prime}_{1},t_{2},t_{3})\cdot(t_{1},t^{\prime}_{2},t_{3})=t\in R
and we are again done.
Hence, up to reordering the arguments of
R
we may assume that
t_{2}^{\prime}=\rho(t_{2})
, and since
R_{t_{3}}
is preserved by
\rho^{-1}
we get that

(\rho^{-1}(t_{1}),t_{2})\in R_{t_{3}}
. Therefore,
t^{\prime}:=(\rho^{-1}(t_{1}),t_{2},t_{3})\in R
.
By similar reasoning with the relation
\{(x,y)\mid(t_{1},x,y)\in R\}
instead of
R_{t_{3}}
we obtain
that
t^{\prime\prime}:=(t_{1},\rho^{-1}(t_{2}),t_{3})\in R
or
t^{\prime\prime}:=(t_{1},t_{2},\rho^{-1}(t_{3}))\in R
. Applying
\cdot
to
t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime}\in R
, we again obtain
t\in R
.

Finally, consider the case
n>3
. Then for

i,j\in{[n-1]\choose 2}
we have that
\pi_{i,j,n}(R)=A^{3}
by the case
n=3
. Hence, for any
a\in A
we have
\pi_{i,j}(R_{a})=A^{2}
, and it follows from the case
n-1
that
R_{a}=A^{n-1}
. This means that
R=A^{n}
.
∎

Definition 61.8
.

A
pss-Horn clause
is a formula of the form

\bigwedge_{i\in[k]}x_{i}\in\{a_{i},\rho(a_{i})\}\wedge((\bigwedge_{i\in[k]}x_{i}=a_{i})\Rightarrow\psi)

where
a_{1},\dots,a_{k}\in A
are constants,

x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
are variables,
and where

\psi
is

•

of the form
y\in\{c,d\}
, for
c,d\in A
and a variable
y
,

•

of the form
C_{3}(y,z)
for variables
y
and
z
, or

•

of the form
y=z
for variables
y
and
z
.

Note that
k=0
is permitted and some variables may be equal.

Proposition 61.9
.

For every
R\subseteq A^{n}
, the following are equivalent.

1.

R
is preserved by
\cdot
;

2.

R
can be defined by a conjunction
of pss-Horn clauses;

3.

R
has a primitive positive definition in the structure
(\{0,1,2\};C_{3},R_{3}^{=})
.

Proof.

For the implication from 1. to 2., suppose that
R
is preserved by
\cdot
.
Let
\phi(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
be the conjunction over all pss-Horn clauses that are implied by
R(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
. We prove that
\phi
defines
R
. Suppose that
t
satisfies
\phi
.
Let
\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\}\subseteq[n]
be maximal such that

R(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
implies

\displaystyle x_{i_{1}}\in\{t_{i_{1}},\rho(t_{i_{1}})\}

\displaystyle\wedge\;

\displaystyle x_{i_{1}}=t_{i_{1}}\Rightarrow x_{i_{2}}\in\{t_{i_{2}},\rho(t_{i_{2}})\}

\displaystyle\wedge\;

\displaystyle(x_{i_{1}}=t_{i_{1}}\wedge x_{i_{2}}=t_{i_{2}})\Rightarrow x_{i_{3}}\in\{t_{i_{3}},\rho(t_{i_{3}})\}

\displaystyle\cdots\;\wedge\;

\displaystyle(x_{i_{1}}=t_{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge x_{i_{k-1}}=t_{i_{k-1}})\Rightarrow x_{i_{k}}\in\{t_{i_{k}},\rho(t_{i_{k}})\}.

For the sake of notation,
we assume that
i_{1}=n,\dots,i_{k}=n-k+1
,
which is without loss of generality, because otherwise we may reorder the arguments of
R
accordingly.
Define

R^{\prime}:=\{(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-k})\mid(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-k},t_{n-k+1},\dots,t_{n})\in R\}\leq{{\bf A}}^{n-k}.

Note that if
\pi_{i}(R^{\prime})=\{a,\rho(a)\}
, for
a\in A
and
i\in\{m+1,\dots,n-k\}
,
then
R(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
implies that

(x_{n-k+1}=t_{n-k+1}\wedge\cdots\wedge x_{n}=t_{n})\Rightarrow x_{i}\in\{a,\rho(a)\}
.
If
a=t_{i}
we obtain a contradiction to the maximality of
k
.
Hence, we must have
t_{i}=\rho(a)
.

Also note that
R^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
because
otherwise the following pss-Horn clause is implied by
R(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
.

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{i\in\{n-k+1,\dots,n\}}x_{i}\in\{t_{i},\rho(t_{i})\}\wedge\left(\bigwedge_{i\in\{n-k+1,\dots,n\}}x_{i}=t_{i}\right)\Rightarrow C(x_{n},x_{n})

But this formula is false for
t
, contrary to the assumptions that
t
satisfies
\phi
.
By further reordering the arguments of
R
we may additionally assume that there exists
m\in\{0,\dots,n-k\}
such that
\pi_{i}(R^{\prime})=A
for
i\in[m]

and
|\pi_{i}(R^{\prime})|\leq 2
for
i\in\{m+1,\dots,n-k\}
.

If
m\geq 1
, then

\pi_{[m]}(R^{\prime})
is subdirect in
{\bf A}^{m}
. Hence, Lemma
61.7

implies that
\pi_{[m]}(R^{\prime})
can be defined by a conjunction of atomic formulas
\psi
over
(A;C_{3})
.
Then for every conjunct
\chi
of
\psi
we have that
R(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
implies
the pss-Horn clause

\bigwedge_{j\in\{n-k+1,\dots,n\}}x_{j}\in\{t_{j},\rho(t_{j})\}\wedge\left(\bigwedge_{j\in\{n-k+1,\dots,n\}}x_{j}=t_{j}\right)\Rightarrow\chi.

It follows that

(t_{1},\dots,t_{m})\in\pi_{[m]}(R^{\prime})
satisfies
\psi
,
and hence
(\rho^{-1}(t_{1}),\dots,\rho^{-1}(t_{m}))\in\pi_{[m]}(R^{\prime})
since
C_{3}
is preserved by
\rho^{-1}
.
So
(t_{1},\dots,t_{m})
and

(\rho^{-1}(t_{1}),\dots,\rho^{-1}(t_{m}))
can be
extended to tuples
p,q\in R^{\prime}
, respectively.
If
m=0
, then we pick
p,q\in R^{\prime}
arbitrarily.

For
i\in[n-k]
, let
s^{i}\in R^{\prime}
be such
that
s^{i}_{i}=t_{i}
.
Define
s:=s^{m+1}\cdot(\cdots\cdot(s^{n-k-1}\cdot s^{n-k})\cdots)\in R^{\prime}
,
and note that
s_{i}=s^{i}_{i}=t_{i}
for all
i\in\{m+1,\dots,n-k\}
.
Observe that

(s_{i}\cdot\rho^{-1}(t_{i}))\cdot t_{i}=t_{i}

for
i\in[m]

using (
35
).
Also observe that

s_{i}\cdot q_{i}=s_{i}=t_{i}
and
that
s_{i}\cdot p_{i}=s_{i}=t_{i}

for
i\in\{m+1,\dots,n-k\}
, because
p_{i},q_{i}\in\{\rho^{-1}(t_{i}),t_{i}\}=\pi_{i}(R^{\prime})
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle(s\cdot q)\cdot p

\displaystyle=\big((s_{1}\cdot\rho^{-1}(t_{1}))\cdot t_{1},\dots,(s_{m}\cdot\rho^{-1}(t_{m}))\cdot t_{m},s_{m+1},\dots,s_{n-k}\big)

\displaystyle=(t_{1},\dots,t_{m},t_{m+1},\dots,t_{n-k})\in R^{\prime}.

This in turn shows that

t\in R
and concludes the proof of the implication from 1 to 2.

For the implication from 2. to 3. it suffices to show that every pss-Horn clause has a primitive positive definition in
(\{0,1,2\};C_{3},R_{3}^{=})
.
Note that

•

\{0,1\}
has the primitive positive definition
\psi(x)
given by

\exists y,z.R^{=}_{3}(x,y,z);

•

\{1,2\}
has the primitive positive definition

\exists y\big(C_{3}(y,x)\wedge y\in\{0,1\}\big);

•

similarly,
\{2,0\}
and hence also
\{0\}
,
\{1\}
, and
\{2\}
are primitively positively definable.

Next, for every
k\geq 1
, the relation

R^{=}_{k+2}\{(x_{1},\dots,x_{k},y,z)\in\{0,1\}^{k}\times A^{2}\mid x_{1}=\cdots=x_{k}=0\Rightarrow y=z\}

has the following primitive positive definition

\exists u_{1},\dots,u_{k+1}\big(R_{3}^{=}(x_{1},y,u_{1})\wedge R_{3}^{=}(x_{2},u_{1},u_{2})\wedge\cdots\wedge R^{=}_{3}(x_{k-1},u_{k-1},u_{k})\wedge R^{=}_{3}(x_{k},u_{k},z)\big).

This allows us to define
for every
a_{1},\dots,a_{k}\in A
the relation

R^{=}_{a_{1},\dots,a_{k}}:=\left\{(x_{1},\dots,x_{k},y,z)\in A^{k+2}\mid\bigwedge_{i=1}^{k}x_{i}\in\{a_{i},\rho(a_{i})\}\wedge\left(\left(\bigwedge_{i=1}^{k}x_{i}=a_{i}\right)\Rightarrow y=z\right)\right\}

by the formula

\exists u_{1},\dots u_{k}\;\left(R^{=}_{k+2}(u_{1},\dots,u_{k},y,z)\wedge\bigwedge_{i=1}^{k}\phi_{i}(x_{i},u_{i})\right),

where

\displaystyle\phi_{i}:=\begin{cases}x_{i}=u_{i}&\text{ if }a_{i}=0;\\
C_{3}(u_{i},x_{i})&\text{ if }a_{i}=1;\\
C_{3}(x_{i},u_{i})&\text{ if }a_{i}=2.\end{cases}

Finally, let
c,d\in A
. The pss-Horn clause

\bigwedge_{i=1}^{k}x_{i}\in\{a_{i},\rho(a_{i})\}\wedge\left(\left(\bigwedge_{i=1}^{k}x_{i}=a_{i}\right)\Rightarrow y\in\{c,d\}\right)

can be defined by

\exists u\;(R^{=}_{a_{1},\dots,a_{k}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k},y,u)\wedge u\in\{c,d\})

and the pss-Horn clause

\bigwedge_{i=1}^{k}x_{i}\in\{a_{i},\rho(a_{i})\}\wedge\left(\left(\bigwedge_{i=1}^{k}x_{i}=a_{i}\right)\Rightarrow C_{3}(y,z)\right)

by

\exists u\;\big(R^{=}_{a_{1},\dots,a_{k}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k},y,u)\wedge C_{3}(u,z)\big).

Finally, for the implication from
3.
to
1.
we verify that
C_{3}
and
R_{3}^{=}
are preserved by
\cdot
. For
C_{3}
, this is immediate from the fact that
\rho
is an automorphism of
{\bf A}
. If
(x_{1},y_{1},z_{1}),(x_{2},y_{2},z_{2})\in R^{=}_{3}
, we have to show that

(x_{0},y_{0},z_{0}):=(x_{1}\cdot x_{2},y_{1}\cdot y_{2},z_{1}\cdot z_{2})\in R^{=}_{3}
.
We have
x_{1},x_{2}\in\{0,1\}
and hence
x_{0}=x_{1}\cdot x_{2}\in\{0,1\}
.
If
x_{0}=1
, then
(x_{0},y_{0},z_{0})\in R^{=}_{3}
and we are done. Otherwise, we must have that
x_{1}=x_{2}=0
, and hence
y_{1}=z_{1}
and
y_{2}=z_{2}
. But then
y_{0}=y_{1}\cdot y_{2}=z_{1}\cdot z_{2}=z_{0}
and again
(x_{0},y_{0},z_{0})\in R^{=}_{3}
.
Hence, if
R

has a primitive positive definition in
(\{0,1,2\};C_{3},R^{=}_{3})
, it is preserved by
\cdot
, proving that 3. implies 1.
∎

Remark 61.10
.

The algebra
{\bf A}
is absorption free.
First note that

B=\{0,1\}
is not absorbing.
Indeed, for every
n\geq 1
the relation

R:=\{(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1},y)\in A^{n}\mid x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1}\in\{1,2\}\wedge x_{1}=\cdots=x_{n-1}=1\Rightarrow y=2\}

can be defined by a
pss-Horn clause
and hence
is a subalgebra of
{\bf A}^{n}
,
and is
B
-essential: for every
i\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}

we have

\displaystyle R\cap(B^{i}\times A\times B^{n-i-1})

\displaystyle=\{(1,\dots,1,2,1,\dots,1,0),(1,\dots,1,2,1,\dots,1,1)\}

\displaystyle R\cap(B^{n-1}\times A)

\displaystyle=\{(1,\dots,1,2)\},\text{ but }

\displaystyle R\cap B^{n}

\displaystyle=\emptyset.

Hence, Lemma
58.5
implies
that
B
is not absorbing.
The same argument shows that
C=\{1\}
is not absorbing.
Every other proper subuniverse is symmetric to
B
or
C
via
\rho
.

Algorithms to solve
\Csp(\{0,1,2\};C_{3},R_{3}^{=})
will be discussed in Section
65
.

Exercises.

205.

Show that
\Csp(\{0,1,2\};C_{3},R_{3}^{=})
can be solved by the
3
-consistency

procedure (see Exercise
89
).

206.

Let
{\bf A}=(\{0,1,2,3,4\};\circ)
be the idempotent algebra where
\circ
is given by

the rock, paper, scissors, lizzard, spock game:

•

spock smashes scissors and vaporises rock,

•

scissors cuts paper and decapitates lizard,

•

paper disproves spock and covers rock,

•

rock crushes scissors and rock, and

•

lizard eats paper and poisons spock.

Determine the proper subalgebras and proper congruences of
{\bf A}
. Which subalgebras are absorbing? Is
{\bf A}
Taylor, Abelian, absorption-free? Is
{\bf A}
subdirectly complete (Definition
61.11
)?

207.

Is there a structure
\mathfrak{A}
with a finite relational signature such that

a relation
R\subseteq A^{n}
is preserved by the operation
\circ
from the previous exercise

if and only if

R
has a
primitive positive definition in
\mathfrak{A}
?

61.2
The Four Cases for Taylor algebras

The property of the paper-scissors-stone algebra established in Lemma
61.7
is of general importance when studying finite idempotent Taylor algebras.

Definition 61.11
.

Let
{\bf A}
be an algebra and let
{\mathfrak{A}}
be
the relational structure with the same domain as
{\bf A}

whose relations are the graphs of the automorphisms of
{\bf A}
.
Then
{\bf A}
is called
subdirectly complete
if every subdirect
R\leq{{\bf A}}^{n}
, for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
can be defined by a conjunction of atomic formulas over
\mathfrak{A}
.

Example 62
.

The Paper-Scissors-Stone algebra (Definition
61.6
) is subdirectly complete (Lemma
61.7
).

We state a consequence of the Absorption Theorem for finite
simple
algebras. This result is called ‘Zhuk’s four cases’ in the lecture notes of Zarathustra Brady
[
98
]
, who cites
[
99
]
; a related predecessor result can be found in
[
12
, Theorem 38]
.

Theorem 62.1
.

Let
{\bf A}
be a simple finite idempotent Taylor algebra.
Then at least one of the following four cases applies.

1.

{\bf A}
has a proper binary absorbing subuniverse.

2.

{\bf A}
has a proper centrally absorbing subuniverse.

3.

{\bf A}
is affine.

4.

{\bf A}
is subdirectly complete.

Proof.

Suppose that
{\bf A}
has no proper binary or centrally absorbing subuniverse and is not affine.
We prove by induction on
n\geq 1
that every subdirect
R\leq{{\bf A}}^{n}
has a definition by a conjunction of atomic formulas in the structure
{\mathfrak{A}}
whose relations are the graphs of the automorphisms of
{\bf A}
. If
n=1
, then
R=A
since
R
is subdirect, and there is nothing to be shown.
If
n=2
,
then
R
is linked or the graph of an automorphism of
{\bf A}
, by the simplicity of

{\bf A}
(see Exercise
201
).
In the latter case we are done, so suppose that

R
is linked.
If
R=A^{2}
, then we are also done.
Otherwise,
the Absorption Theorem in its strengthend version (Theorem
61.5
) implies
that
{\bf A}
has a proper binary or centrally absorbing subuniverse.

Now suppose that
n\geq 3
.
We first consider the case that for some
\{i,j\}\in{[n]\choose{2}}
the relation
R^{\prime}:=\pi_{i,j}(R)
is the graph of an automorphism of
{\bf A}
.
For the sake of notation, suppose that
j=n
.
Then the relation

\pi_{[n-1]}(R)
is subdirect and by the inductive assumption has a definition
\phi(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1})
by a conjunction of atomic formulas over
\mathfrak{A}
.
Then
\phi(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1})\wedge R^{\prime}(x_{i},x_{j})
is a definition of
R
over
\mathfrak{A}
and we are done.
Therefore, we may assume that for every
\{i,j\}\in{[n]\choose{2}}
the relation
R^{\prime}:=\pi_{i,j}(R)
is
not
the graph of an automorphism, and hence
R^{\prime}=A^{2}
by the case
n=2
.
We have to show that
R=A^{n}
.

Note that for every
a\in A
the
(n-1)
-ary relation

R_{a}:=\{\bar{x}\mid(a,\bar{x})\in R\}

is a subuniverse of
{\bf A}^{n-1}
because
{\bf A}
is idempotent. Moreover,
R_{a}

is subdirect.
Indeed, let
b\in A
.
Note that the binary relation

\pi_{1,2}(R)
equals
A^{2}
by the case
n=2
,
and hence in particular it contains
(a,b)
.
Hence, there exists
c^{\prime}\in A^{n-2}
such that
(a,b,c^{\prime})\in R
, and thus
(b,c^{\prime})\in R_{a}
.
Similar arguments apply to the other arguments of
R_{a}
, showing that
R_{a}\leq{\bf A}^{n-1}
is subdirect.

We first consider the case
n=3
.
Since
R_{a}\leq{\bf A}^{2}
is subdirect, by the case
n=2
it is either the graph of an automorphism of
{\bf A}
or it equals
A^{2}
. First suppose that there exists an
a\in A
such that
R_{a}=A^{2}
.
Then

a
is an element of the left center
C
of
R
if
R
is considered as a subalgebra of
{\bf A}\times{\bf A}^{2}
.
If
C=A
then
R=A^{3}
and we are done. Otherwise,
C
is a proper subuniverse of
{\bf A}
.
Clearly,
{\bf A}^{2}
is Taylor since
{\bf A}
is Taylor.
Lemma
58.2
implies that

{\bf A}^{2}
does not have proper 2-absorbing subuniverses because
{\bf A}
has no 3-absorbing, and hence no 2-absorbing, subuniverses.
Since
R
is subdirect, Proposition
61.2
implies that

C\vartriangleleft_{Z}{{\bf A}}
.

Otherwise, for every
a\in A
the relation
R_{a}
is the graph of an automorphism of
{\bf A}
. A similar argument applies to
R
after permuting the arguments. So we may assume that for every
a\in A
and every
i\in\{1,2,3\}
the relation defined by
\exists x_{i}(R(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\wedge x_{i}=a)
is the graph of an automorphism of
{\bf A}
.
Then
{\bf A}
is abelian by
Proposition
52.1
, contrary to our assumptions.

Finally, suppose that
n>3
. Then for
i,j\in{\{2,\dots,n\}\choose 2}

we have that

\pi_{\{1,i,j\}}(R)=A^{3}

by the case
n=3
. Hence, for any
a\in A
we have
\pi_{\{i,j\}}(R_{a})=A^{2}
, and it follows from the case
n-1
that
R_{a}=A^{n-1}
. This means that
R=A^{n}
.
∎

We can use Theorem
62.1
to obtain a result about not necessarily simple finite idempotent Taylor algebras. This will not be needed in the proof of the cyclic terms theorem in Section
63
, but it will be used in the characterisation of finite-domain CSPs of bounded width in Section
65
.

Corollary 62.2
.

Let
{\bf A}
be a finite idempotent Taylor algebra with at least two elements.
Then at least one of the following is true.

1.

{\bf A}
has a proper binary absorbing subuniverse.

2.

{\bf A}
has a proper centrally absorbing subalgebra.

3.

{\bf A}
has an affine quotient with at least two elements.

4.

{\bf A}
has a subdirectly complete quotient with at least two elements.

Proof.

If
{\bf A}
is simple, then the statement follows from Theorem
62.1
.
Otherwise, there exists a proper congruence
\theta
;
choose
\theta
to be maximal with respect to inclusion,
so that
{\bf A}^{\prime}:={\bf A}/\theta
is simple. Note that
{\bf A}^{\prime}
has at least two elements because
\theta\neq A^{2}
.
Hence, Theorem
62.1
implies that
{\bf A}^{\prime}
has a proper binary or centrally absorbing subalgebra
{\bf B}
, is affine, or subdirectly complete.
If
{\bf A}^{\prime}
has a proper binary absorbing subalgebra, then
(the proof of) Lemma
57.3
shows that

{\bf A}
has a proper absorbing subalgebra as well.
If
{\bf A}^{\prime}
has a proper centrally absorbing subalgebra, then Lemma
61.1
shows that
{\bf A}
has a centrally absorbing subalgebra as well.
Otherwise,
{\bf A}
has an affine quotient or a subdirectly complete quotient with at least two elements.
∎

63
Cyclic Terms

An operation
c\colon A^{n}\to A
, for
n\geq 2
, is
cyclic
if it satisfies for all
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in A
that

c(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})=c(a_{2},\dots,a_{n},a_{1})
.
Cyclic operations are in particular Taylor operations.
Conversely, a result of Barto and Kozik (Theorem
63.4
below) implies that
every Taylor operation on a finite set generates
a cyclic operation.

We start with some easy but useful observations about cyclic terms.
The
cyclic composition

s\circlearrowleft t
of
s
and
t

is the operation (or term) of arity
q
defined by

(x_{1},\dots,x_{q})\mapsto s\big(t(x_{1},\dots,x_{q}),t(x_{2},\dots,x_{q},x_{1}),\dots\big).

The following is easy to see.

Lemma 63.1
.

Let
s\colon A^{k}\to A
and

t\colon A^{l}\to A
be operations.

•

If
s
is arbitrary and
t
is cyclic then
s\circlearrowleft t
is cyclic.

•

If
s
is cyclic,
t
is arbitrary, and
l
divides
k
then
s\circlearrowleft t
is cyclic.

{\bf A}=(\{0,1\};\min)

f\in\Clo({\bf A})^{(k)}

f(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})=\min(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}).

and

and

, respectively,

s*t
(Definition

)
is cyclic

after reordering the arguments, i.e.,
there is a permutation
\alpha
of
[kl]

such that
(s*t)^{\alpha}
is cyclic.

{\bf A}=(\{0,1\};\majority)
and
f\in\Clo({\bf A})^{(k)}
is cyclic.

•

k\geq 3
;

•

if
r>k/2
and
c\in A^{k}
is such that
c_{i}=a
for
i\leq r
and
c_{i}=b
otherwise, then
f(c)=a
;

•

if
r,s,t
are such that
r+s>t
,
s+t>r
, and
t+r>s
, then the operation

\displaystyle(x,y,z)\mapsto f(\underbrace{x,\dots,x}_{r},\underbrace{y,\dots,y}_{s},\underbrace{z,\dots,z}_{t})

(36)

is the ternary majority operation on
\{0,1\}
.

m\colon A^{3}\to A

63.1
Cyclic Relations

When
a=(a_{0},a_{1},\dots,a_{k-1})
is a
k
-tuple,
we write
\rho(a)
for the
k
-tuple
(a_{1},\dots,a_{k-1},a_{0})
.

Definition 63.2
.

An
n
-ary relation
R
on a set
A
is called
cyclic
if for all
a\in A^{k}

a\in R\Rightarrow\rho(a)\in R\;.

Lemma 63.3

(from
[
11
]
)
.

A finite idempotent algebra
{\bf A}
has a

k
-ary cyclic term if and only if
every nonempty cyclic subalgebra of

{\bf A}^{k}
contains a constant tuple.

Proof.

Let
\tau
be the signature of
{\bf A}
.
For the easy direction, suppose that
{\bf A}
has
a cyclic
\tau
-term
t(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})
. Let
a=(a_{0},a_{1},\dots,a_{k-1})
be an arbitrary tuple in a cyclic subalgebra
{\bf R}
of
{\bf A}^{k}
. As
R
is cyclic,
\rho(a),\dots,\rho^{k-1}(a)\in R
, and since
{\bf R}
is a subalgebra

b:=t^{{\bf A}}(a,\rho(a),\dots,\rho^{k-1}(a))\in R.

Since
t
is cyclic, the
k
-tuple
b
is constant.

To prove the converse direction, we assume that
every nonempty cyclic subalgebra of
{\bf A}^{k}
contains a constant tuple. For a
\tau
-term
f(x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{k-1})
, let
S(f)
be the set of all
a\in A^{k}

such that
f^{{\bf A}}(a)=f^{{\bf A}}(\rho(a))=\cdots=f^{{\bf A}}(\rho^{k-1}(a))
.
Choose
f
such that
|S(f)|
is maximal (here we use
the assumption that
A
is finite). If
|S(f)|=|A^{k}|
, then
f^{{\bf A}}
is cyclic and we are done.
Otherwise, arbitrarily pick

a=(a_{0},a_{1},\dots,a_{k-1})\in A^{k}\setminus S(f)
.
For
i\in\{0,\dots,k-1\}
, define
b_{i}:=f(\rho^{i}(a))
,
and let
B:=\{b,\rho(b),\dots,\rho^{k-1}(b)\}
.

We claim that the smallest subalgebra
{\bf C}
of
{\bf A}^{k}
that contains
B
is cyclic. So let
c\in C
be arbitrary.
Since
{\bf C}
is generated by
B
, there exists
a
\tau
-term
s(x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{k-1})
such that
c=s^{{\bf A}}(b,\rho(b),\dots,\rho^{k-1}(b))
. Then
\rho(c)=s^{{\bf A}}(\rho(b),\rho^{2}(b),\dots,\rho^{k-1}(b),b)\in C
, proving the claim.

Since
C
is cyclic, by our assumption it
contains a constant tuple
d
.
Then there exists a
\tau
-term
r(x_{0},\dots,x_{k-1})

such that
d=r^{{\bf C}}(b,\rho(b),\dots,\rho^{k-1}(b))
.
Note that

r^{{\bf A}}(b)=r^{{\bf A}}(\rho(b))=\cdots=r^{{\bf A}}(\rho^{k-1}(b))

since
d
is constant.
It follows that
b\in S(r)
.

Figure 21:
Diagram for the proof of Lemma
63.3
.

Now consider
the
\tau
-term
t(x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{k-1})
defined by

t(x):=r\circlearrowleft f=r(f(x),f(\rho(x)),\dots,f(\rho^{k-1}(x))).

where
x:=(x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{k-1})
.
We claim that
S(f)\subseteq S(t)
.
Let
e\in S(f)
.
To show that
e\in S(t)
, note that for all
i\in\{0,\dots,k-1\}

\displaystyle t^{{\bf A}}(\rho^{i}(e))=

\displaystyle\;r^{{\bf A}}\big(f^{{\bf A}}(\rho^{i}(e)),f^{{\bf A}}(\rho^{i+1}(e)),\dots,f^{{\bf A}}(\rho^{i-1}(e))\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;r^{{\bf A}}\big(f^{{\bf A}}(e),f^{{\bf A}}(\rho^{1}(e)),\dots,f^{{\bf A}}(\rho^{k-1}(e))\big)

(since
e\in S(f)
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;t^{{\bf A}}(e).

Moreover,
a\in S(t)
, because

\displaystyle t^{{\bf A}}(\rho^{i}(a))=

\displaystyle\;r^{{\bf A}}\big(f^{{\bf A}}(\rho^{i}(a)),f^{{\bf A}}(\rho^{i+1}(a)),\dots,f^{{\bf A}}(\rho^{i-1}(a))\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;r^{{\bf A}}(b_{i},b_{i+1},\dots,b_{i-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;r^{{\bf A}}(\rho^{i}(b))

is constant for all
i
by the choice of
r
. We obtain a contradiction to the maximality of
|S(f)|
.
∎

Exercises.
