C_{2}^{++}
from Exercise
77
has a ternary cyclic polymorphism.

63.2
The Cyclic Terms Theorem

In this section we prove the following theorem of Barto and Kozik
[
11
]
.

Theorem 63.4

(of
[
11
]
)
.

Let
{\bf A}
be a finite algebra. Then the following are equivalent.

1.

{\bf A}
has a Taylor term;

2.

{\bf A}
has a cyclic term;

3.

for all prime numbers
p>|A|
, the algebra
{\bf A}
has a
p
-ary cyclic term.

Proof.

The implication from 3 to 2 and from 2 to 1 are trivial. For the implication from 1 to 3, let
p>|A|
be prime. Our proof is by induction on
|A|
. We may assume that
{\bf A}
is idempotent (see Lemma
43.12
).
For
|A|=1
the statement is trivial.
For the induction step, we use Lemma
63.3
. Let
R\leq{\bf A}^{p}
be non-empty and cyclic. We have to show that
R
contains a constant tuple. We may assume that
R
is subdirect: indeed, if
\pi_{i}(R)
is a proper subuniverse of
{\bf A}
, for some
i\in[p]
, then
R\leq\pi_{i}(R)^{p}
contains a constant tuple
by the inductive assumption.

If there is
\{i,j\}\in{[p]\choose 2}
such that
\pi_{i,j}(R)
(Definition
24.1
) is the graph of an automorphism
\alpha
of
{\bf A}
, then

\pi_{j,2j-i}(R)
is the graph of
\alpha
as well, because
R
is cyclic, and the same applies to
\pi_{2j-i,3j-2i}(R)
, etc. Moreover,
\alpha^{p}=\id_{A}
since
R
is cyclic and of arity
p
.
Since
p>|A|
is prime, we must have
\alpha=\id_{A}
. This shows that
R

contains for every
a\in A
the constant tuple
(a,\dots,a)
: by subdirectness, there exists a tuple
t=(t_{1},\dots,t_{p})\in R
such that
t_{i}=a
; by what we have seen above,
t_{j}=a
,
t_{2j-i}=a
, etc, and since
p
is prime we obtain that
t=(a,\dots,a)
.

So we may suppose that for every
\{i,j\}\in{[p]\choose 2}
, the relation

\pi_{i,j}(R)
is not the graph of an automorphism of
{\bf A}
.

Suppose that
{\bf A}
has a proper congruence
C
.
Let
h
be the homomorphism from
{\bf A}

to
{\bf A}/C
. Since
C
is proper,
|A/C|
is strictly smaller than
|A|
.
Let
h^{*}
be the homomorphism from
{\bf A}^{p}
to
({\bf A}/C)^{p}
obtained by applying
h
componentwise. Then
h^{*}(R)\leq({\bf A}/C)^{p}
(Lemma
38.1
) is cyclic, so the inductive assumption implies that
h^{*}(R)
contains a constant tuple
(a/C,\dots,a/C)
.
Note that
a/C
is a nonempty proper subalgebra of
{\bf A}
since
{\bf A}
is idempotent and
C
is proper.
Then
R\cap(a/C)^{p}\leq{\bf A}^{p}
is non-empty and cyclic, and hence contains a constant tuple by the inductive assumption. Thus, if
{\bf A}
is not simple we are done.

Now suppose that
{\bf A}
is simple.
Note that in this case,
\pi_{i,j}(R)
is linked for every
\{i,j\}\in{[p]\choose 2}
.
Indeed, by assumption,
\pi_{i,j}(R)
is not the graph of an automorphism of
{\bf A}
.
Since
R
is subdirect, so is
\pi_{i,j}(R)
.
Hence, the simplicity of
{\bf A}
implies that

\pi_{i,j}(R)
is linked
(Exercise
201
).

Since
{\bf A}
is simple, one of the cases from Theorem
62.1
applies.
First we consider the case that
{\bf A}
is subdirectly complete.
By assumption, for every
\{i,j\}\in{[p]\choose 2}
, the relation

\pi_{i,j}(R)
is not the graph of an automorphism of
{\bf A}
, and by subdirect completeness equals
A^{2}
.
In particular,
R
contains a constant tuple.

If
{\bf A}
is affine with underlying abelian group
(A;+,-,0)
, then for all
k\geq 1
and
a_{1},\dots,a_{k}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
such that
a_{1}+\cdots+a_{k}\equiv 1\mod|A|
the operation

(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})\mapsto a_{1}x_{1}+\cdots+a_{k}x_{k}
is a term operation of
{\bf A}
(combine Theorem
51.2
and Exercise
145
).
Since
p>|A|
is prime, there
exists
i
such that
ip=1\mod|A|
.
In particular,

(x_{1},\dots,x_{p})\mapsto ix_{1}+\cdots+ix_{p}
is a term operation of
{\bf A}
, and clearly cyclic.

The final case is that
{\bf A}
has a proper 2-absorbing or centrally absorbing subalgebra
U
. In both cases,
U
is
3
-absorbing
(
2
-absorbing trivially implies
3
-absorbing, and centrally absorbing implies
3
-absorbing by Proposition
61.4
).
Define a directed graph
\mathfrak{D}
whose vertices are pairs
(i,B)
where
i\in[p]
and
B\vartriangleleft{\bf A}
is proper 3-absorbing, and whose edges are the pairs

((i,B),(j,B^{\prime}))
for distinct

i,j\in[p]
with
B+\pi_{i,j}(R)\subseteq B^{\prime}

(the notation has been introduced in Exercise
195
).
11
11

11

The author thanks Michael Pinsker for a suggestion how to simplify the argument in this case.

Clearly, the edge relation of
\mathfrak{D}
is transitive.
Also note that if
B\vartriangleleft{\bf A}
is 3-absorbing, then
B+\pi_{i,j}(R)\vartriangleleft{\bf A}
is 3-absorbing as well (Exercise
195
).

Claim.
If
\mathfrak{D}
contains the edge
((i,B),(j,B^{\prime}))
, then
((j,B^{\prime}),(i,B))
is not an edge. To prove this claim, suppose that
i,j\in[p]
are distinct such
that
B+\pi_{i,j}(R)\subseteq B^{\prime}
.
If
B^{\prime}+\pi_{j,i}(R)\subseteq B
, then
this is in contradiction to the fact that

\pi_{i,j}(R)
is linked.

The claim together with the transitivity of the edge relation implies that
\mathfrak{D}
is acyclic.
Since
\mathfrak{D}
is finite, non-empty, and acyclic, it must contain a sink
(i,B)
. Note that

B+\pi_{i,j}(R)=A
for all
j\in[p]\setminus\{i\}
. Since
R
is cyclic, this implies that
\pi_{I}(R)\cap B^{2}\neq\emptyset
for all
I\in{[p]\choose 2}
. Hence,
R^{\prime}:=R\cap B^{p}
is non-empty by
Corollary
58.7
,
because
B
is 3-absorbing. Since
|B|<|A|
, we obtain a constant tuple in
R^{\prime}\subseteq R
by the inductive assumption.
∎

Theorem 63.5

(Tractability Theorem, Version 5)
.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a relational structure with finite domain and finite signature.
If
\mathfrak{B}
has a cyclic polymorphism, then
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
is in P. Otherwise,

\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
is NP-complete.

Proof.

An immediate consequence of Theorem
63.4
and Theorem
20.1
.
∎

Exercises.

221.

Use the results presented in the text to show that a finite idempotent algebra
{\bf A}
has a Taylor term if and only if
it has a
weak near unanimity term
, i.e., a term
t
of arity
n\geq 2

such that
{\bf A}
satisfies

f(x,\dots,x,y)\approx f(x,\dots,x,y,x)\approx\cdots\approx f(y,x,\dots,x)

(this was first shown by Maroti and MacKenzie
[
83
]
; see Section
63.4
for

more facts about weak near unanimity operations).

222.

Show that a finite structure has a cyclic polymorphism if and only if it has

a weak near unanimity polymorphism (warning: the structure might have

non-idempotent operations!).

223.

Give an immediate proof (without using results from the text) that
K_{3}

does not have
weak near unanimity polymorphisms.

224.

Find a cyclic term in the algebra

({\mathbb{Z}}_{p};m)
where
m
is given by
(x,y,z)\mapsto x-y+z
.

225.

Show that if
{\bf A}
and
{\bf B}
are finite algebras, each with a cyclic term,

then
{\bf A}\times{\bf B}
has a cyclic term as well. How about the same statement

for Taylor terms?

63.3
Siggers Terms of Arity 4

Interestingly, whether
a finite algebra has a Taylor term (equivalently: a cyclic term, or a weak near unanimity term from Exercise
221
) can be tested by
searching for a single 4-ary term
s
that satisfies

s(x,x,y,z)\approx s(y,z,z,x),

a so-called
4-ary Siggers term
.
Note that this definition comes in numerous variants, because we may permute the arguments of
s
and rename the variables of the identity and obtain equivalent conditions. One such variant is

t(a,r,e,a)\approx t(r,a,r,e)\,.

Siggers originally found a 6-ary term (see Section
44.2
), which has been improved later to the 4-ary term given above.
The observation that this condition can be obtained by equating variables of a cyclic term of sufficiently high arity is from
[
73
]
; the proof below is based on a variant from
[
98
]

of their proof.

Theorem 63.6
.

A finite algebra has a cyclic term if and only if
it
has a 4-ary Siggers term.

Proof.

Suppose that
{\bf A}
has a cyclic term.
Let
c(x_{1},\dots,x_{p})
be a cyclic term of
{\bf A}
for some
p\geq 2
.
Then there are numbers
a,b\in{\mathbb{N}}

be such that
2a+3b=m
, and we
define
s(x,y,z,w)
to be the term

s(x,y,z,w):=c(\underbrace{x,\dots,x}_{b},\underbrace{y,\dots,y}_{a},\underbrace{z,\dots,z}_{b},\underbrace{w,\dots,w}_{a+b}).

Then

\displaystyle s(x,x,y,z)

\displaystyle=c(\underbrace{x,\dots,x}_{b},\underbrace{x,\dots,x}_{a},\underbrace{y,\dots,y}_{b},\underbrace{z,\dots,z}_{a+b})

\displaystyle\approx c(\underbrace{y,\dots,y}_{b},\underbrace{z,\dots,z}_{a},\underbrace{z,\dots,z}_{b},\underbrace{x,\dots,x}_{a+b})=s(y,z,z,x).

Conversely, a Siggers term is a Taylor term, and therefore the other direction follows from Theorem
63.4
.
∎

The previous result is optimal in the sense that
there is no equivalent characterisation
using a single ternary Taylor term
[
73
,
70
]
.
However, there is also a system of equations involving only ternary terms that characterises the existence of a Taylor term
[
68
]
. Computationally, checking whether a given finite structure has polymorphisms satisfying these identities is easier than checking for a 4-ary Siggers polymorphism (for computer experiments, see
[
24
]
).

Proposition 63.7

(from
[
68
]
)
.

Let
{\bf A}
be a finite algebra. Then
{\bf A}
has a Taylor term if and only if

{\bf A}
has terms
p,q
satisfying the following identities (‘
p
-
q
-terms’).

\displaystyle q(y,x,x)\approx\;

\displaystyle q(x,x,y)

(37)

\displaystyle q(x,x,y)\approx p(x,y,y)

(38)

\displaystyle p(x,y,x)\approx\;

\displaystyle q(x,y,x)

(39)

Proof.

First suppose that
{\bf A}
has a Taylor term and therefore a 4-ary Siggers term.
Define

p(x,y,z):=s(x,x,y,z)\text{ and }q(x,y,z):=s(y,z,x,x)

and observe that they
p
and
q
satisfy the equations from the statement.

\displaystyle q(y,x,x)

\displaystyle=s(y,y,x,x)\approx s(x,x,x,y)=q(x,x,y)

\displaystyle q(x,x,y)

\displaystyle=s(x,x,x,y)\approx s(y,y,x,x)=p(x,y,y)

\displaystyle p(x,y,x)

\displaystyle=s(y,x,x,x)\approx s(x,x,y,x)=q(x,y,x)

Conversely, let
{\bf A}
be a algebra that satisfies (
37
), (
38
) and (
39
). Then there is no
\xi\colon\Clo({\bf A})\to\Proj
, because otherwise

\displaystyle\xi(q)

\displaystyle=\pi^{3}_{2}

(because of
37
)

\displaystyle\xi(p)

\displaystyle=\pi^{3}_{1}

(because of
38
)

\displaystyle\xi(p)

\displaystyle=\pi^{3}_{2}

(because of
39
)

which is a contradiction unless
|A|=1
.
∎

Exercises.

226.

Show that every algebra with a Maltsev term has a 4-ary Siggers term (directly, without using other results).

63.4
Weak Near Unanimity Operations

A
weak near unanimity operation
(defined in Exercise
221
)
is an operation that satisfies

w(x,\dots,x,y)\approx w(x,\dots,y,x)\approx\cdots\approx w(y,x,\dots,x)\,.

We write
\WNU(k)
for
set of
k
-ary weak
near unanimity operations.
Recall that
a finite algebra has a Taylor term if and only if it has a weak near unanimity term (Exercise
221
).
Again, we warn the reader that some authors additionally assume that weak near unanimity operations are idempotent; we do not make this assumption since it gives us more flexibility of the terminology.

Example 64
.

The algebra
{\bf A}_{n}:=(\{0,\dots,n-1\};m)
where
m(x,y,z):=x-y+z
(see Exercise
145
) has an WNU
(k)
term if and only if
\gcd(k,n)=1
:

•

if
\gcd(k,n)=1
then there is an
a\in\{0,\dots,n-1\}
such that
ak\equiv 1\mod n
.
Hence,
\sum_{i}ax_{i}\in\text{WNU}(k)

and we have
\sum_{i}a=ka=1

so this operation is in
\Clo({\bf A}_{n})
.

•

Conversely, let
g\in
WNU
(k)
. In particular, we have

\displaystyle g(0,\dots,0,1)=a_{k}

\displaystyle\equiv\;

\displaystyle g(1,0,\dots,0)=a_{1}

and it follows that
a:=a_{0}\equiv\cdots\equiv a_{k-1}\mod n
.
But
1=\sum_{i}a_{i}=ka\mod n
,
which implies that
n
and
m
are pairwise prime.

For example,
\Clo({\bf A}_{6})
has a WNU
(5)
term, but not WNU
(k)
term for
k\leq 4
.

Exercises.

227.

Let
(A;+,-,0)
be a finite Abelian group.
Then every idempotent weak near-unanimity
operation that preserves the relation defined by
y_{1}+y_{2}=y_{3}+y_{4}
is of the form

(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\mapsto t\cdot(x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n})
for some
t\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\geq 1}
.

228.

Show that if a clone (not necessarily on a finite domain) contains
f\in\WNU(3)

and
g\in\WNU(4)
, then it also contains an operation in
\WNU(12)
.

229.

Show that if a clone (not necessarily on a finite domain) contains
3-4 weak near unanimity operations
, i.e., operations
f\in\WNU(3)
and
g\in\WNU(4)
satisfying

\forall x,y.\;f(y,x,x)=g(y,x,x,x),

then it also contains
h\in\WNU(13)
.

64.1
Summary of Equivalent Dichotomy Formulations

In the following we list all the equivalent conditions on a finite structure
\mathfrak{B}
with finite relational signature that imply that

\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
is in P. If
\mathfrak{B}
does not satisfy these conditions, then
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
is NP-complete.

Corollary 64.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a finite structure with core
\mathfrak{C}
, let
\mathfrak{D}
be the expansion of
\mathfrak{C}
by all singleton unary relations, and let
{\bf D}
be a polymorphism algebra of
\mathfrak{D}
.
Then the following are equivalent.

1.

K_{3}
does not have a primitive positive interpretation in
\mathfrak{D}
;

2.

\HSP({\bf D})
does not contain an at least 2-element algebra all of whose operations are projections;

3.

\HS({\bf D})
does not contain an at least 2-element algebra all of whose operations are projections (Corollary
42.16
);

4.

there is no clone homomorphism from
\Pol(\mathfrak{D})
to
\Proj
(Corollary
42.16
);

5.

{\bf D}
satisfies some non-trivial finite set of identities (Corollary
42.16
);

6.

\mathfrak{B}
does not pp-construct
K_{3}
, i..e.,
K_{3}\notin\HI(\mathfrak{B})
(Corollary
43.8
);

7.

does not pp-construct all finite structures (Corollary
43.8
);

8.

there is no minion homomorphism
from
\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
to
{\bf Proj}
(Corollary
43.18
);

9.

\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
contains a Taylor operation (Theorem
43.15
);

10.

\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
has a 6-ary Siggers operation (Theorem
44.5
);

11.

\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
contains a weak near-unanimity operation (Exercise
221
);

12.

\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
contains a cyclic operation (Theorem
63.4
);

13.

\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
contains for all prime numbers
p>|B|
a cyclic operation of arity
p
(Theorem
63.4
);

14.

\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
contains a 4-ary Siggers operation (Theorem
63.6
);

15.

\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
has
p
-
q
-operations (Proposition
63.7
).

64.2
Undirected Graphs Revisited

As another application of the cyclic term theorem, we obtain another proof (from
[
15
]
) of the classification of the complexity of
H
-colouring for finite undirected graphs
H
(Theorem
2.6
).

Proof.

If the core
G
of
H
equals
K_{2}
or has just one vertex,
then
\Csp(H)
can be solved in polynomial time, e.g. by the Path Consistency Procedure, Section
9
.
Otherwise,
G
is not bipartite
and there exists a cycle
a_{0},a_{1},\dots,a_{2k},a_{0}
of odd length in
H
.
If
H
has no Taylor polymorphism,
then by Theorem
43.18

\Csp(H)
is NP-hard.

Otherwise, if
H
has a Taylor polymorphism, then Theorem
63.4
asserts that there
exists a
p
-ary cyclic polymorphism
c
of
H
where
p
is a prime number greater than
\max\{2k,|A|\}
.
Since the edges in
H
are undirected, we can also find a cycle

a_{0},a_{1},\dots,a_{p-1},a_{0}
in
H
.
Then
c(a_{0},a_{1},\dots,a_{p-1})=c(a_{1},\dots,a_{p-1},a_{0})
, which implies that

H
contains a loop, a contradiction to the assumption that the core of

H
has more than one element.
∎

This proof naturally generalises to smooth digraphs that are strongly connected. In fact, the assumption that
H
is strongly connected can be dropped.

Theorem 64.2

(Barto, Kozik, Nieven
[
13
]
)
.

Let
H
be a smooth digraph. If
H
has a Taylor polymorphism,
then
H
is homomorphically equivalent
to a cycle.

In the proof we need the concept of
algebraic length
of a graph. It is the minimum number
k\geq 1
such that the graph contains a cycle of net length
k
.

Proof.

We only present a proof for the special case where
H
is strongly connected.
Let
p
be a prime larger than
|V(H)|
. If any two paths in
G
that start and end in the same vertex have the same net length modulo
n
, then
H\rightarrow\vec{C}_{n}
(Exercise
14
) and we are done. Otherwise,

H
has algebraic length one,
and since
H
is strongly connected we find a directed cycle of length
p
.
Theorem
63.4
asserts that there
exists a
p
-ary cyclic polymorphism
c
of
H
.
As in the proof above, we have
c(a_{0},a_{1},\dots,a_{p-1})=c(a_{1},\dots,a_{p-1},a_{0})
, which implies that

H
contains a loop and hence is homomorphically equivalent to a loop.
∎

Corollary 64.3

(Loop Lemma)
.

Let
{\bf A}
be a finite Taylor algebra and
let
R\leq{\bf A}^{2}
be subdirect.
If the digraph
(A,R)
has a connected component of algebraic length one, then
R
has a loop.

Proof.

The digraph
(A,R)
has no sources and sinks because
R
is subdirect and has the Taylor term operation of
{\bf A}
as a polymorphism. Hence,
Theorem
64.2
implies that

(A,R)
is homomorphically equivalent to a disjoint union of cycles. The only cycle that is homomorphically equivalent to a digraph of algebraic length one is the loop, so
(A,R)
is homomorphically equivalent to a structure that contains a loop, so it must contain a loop.
∎

If a graph
H
is homomorphically
equivalent to a disjoint union of cycles, then

\Csp(H)
is in P (e.g., we can use the algorithm PC
H
to solve it; see Section
9
).
On the other hand, a digraph without a Taylor polymorphism has an NP-hard CSP.
Therefore,
Theorem
64.2
shows that the Feder-Vardi conjecture is true for digraphs without sources and sinks: their CSPs are in P or NP-complete.

As another consequence we present a second proof that Taylor algebras have a 4-ary Siggers term, which is the original proof from
[
70
]
.

Figure 22:
The smooth digraph leading to the Siggers terms of arity four.

Second proof of Theorem
63.6
.

Let
{\bf F}
be the free algebra with three generators
x,y,z
in the variety generated by
{\bf A}
(see Section
38.3
). Let
R\leq{\bf F}^{2}
be generated by

\left\{{x\choose y},{y\choose z},{z\choose x},{x\choose z}\right\}.

Then
{\bf R}
is subdirect and
(F,R)
is a smooth digraph of algebraic length 1 (see Figure
22
for the restriction of this digraph to
\{x,y,z\}
). Hence, the Loop Lemma (Corollary
64.3
) implies that
R
contains a pair
(f,f)
, so there exists a term
t(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})
such that

t^{{\bf F}^{2}}({x\choose y},{y\choose z},{z\choose x},{x\choose z})={f\choose f}.

Thus,
t^{{\bf F}}
satisfies
t(x,y,z,x)=f=t(y,z,x,z)
.
∎

Exercises.

230.

Let
G
and
H
be finite smooth digraphs.
Show that if
\Csp(G\times H)

can be

solved in polynomial time, then
\Csp(G)
or
\Csp(H)
can be solved

in polynomial time as well (so we cannot use them to solve Exercise
27
).

231.

Let
G:=(\{1,2,3,4\};E)
be the digraph given by

E:=\{(1,2),(1,3),(2,3),(3,2),(2,4),(3,4)\}.

Show that every finite structure has a primitive positive interpretation in
G
.

65
Bounded Width

Equipped with the universal-algebraic approach,
we come back to one of the questions that occupied
us at the beginning of the course:
which
H
-colouring problems can be solved by the path-consistency procedure
(PC
H
, introduced in Section
9
)? We have seen in Section
9.2
that if
H
has a majority or a semilattice polymorphism, then PC
H
solves the
H
-colouring problem. But these were just sufficient, not necessary conditions.

A necessary and sufficient polymorphism condition for solvability by PC
H
has been found by Barto and Kozik
[
10
]
. Their result is much stronger:
it characterises not just the strength of PC
H
, but
more generally of
k
-consistency (introduced in Section
9
), and not just for
H
-colouring, but for
CSPs of finite relational structures in general (Section
9.1
).

Theorem 65.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a finite structure with a finite relational signature. Then
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
can be solved by the
k
-consistency procedure, for some
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, if and only if

\mathfrak{B}
has
3-4 weak near unanimity
polymorphisms, i.e., operations
f\in\WNU(3)
and
g\in\WNU(4)
satisfying

\forall x,y.\;f(y,x,x)=g(y,x,x,x).

Note that for a given
\mathfrak{B}
, the condition given in Theorem
65.1
is obviously decidable. Many other equivalent
characterisations of bounded width will be presented later
65.33
.

It turns out that if
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
can be solved by
k
-consistency, for some
k
, then it can already be solved by a particularly natural algorithm, namely by the singleton arc-consistency (SAC) procedure (Section
65.3
).
SAC is weaker than
3
-consistency in the sense that
if the SAC procedure detects that an instance is unsatisfiable, then so does the
3
-consistency procedure.
12
12

12

The converse of this statement in general does not hold,
so SAC is
strictly
weaker than
3
-consistency.
However, SAC has the advantage that, unlike
3
-consistency, it only requires linear work space and can be implemented to run in quadratic time.

65.1
k
-Consistency

We have already briefly introduced the
k
-consistency procedure for digraph homomorphism problems in Section
9.1
.
To discuss the idea of

k
-consistency for general
relational structures more precisely,
it is convenient to use a more flexible terminology.
When generalising the
3-consistency procedure for the
H
-colouring problem to
the
k
-consistency procedure for CSPs of arbitrary finite structures
\mathfrak{B}
,
there are two essential parameters:

•

The first is
the arity
l
of the relations maintained for all

l
-tuples of variables in the instance.
For PC
H
, for instance, we have
l=2
.

•

The second is the number of variables
considered at a time within the main loop of the algorithm.
For PC
H
, for instance, we have
k=3
.

Hence, for each pair
(l,k)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}
, we obtain
a different form of consistency procedure, called
(l,k)
-consistency procedure.

Note that it is easy to come up with finite
structures
\mathfrak{B}
whose CSP cannot
be solved by the
(l,k)
-consistency procedure if
\mathfrak{B}
might contain relations of arity larger than
k

(there is no possibility of the
(l,k)
-consistency
algorithm to take constraints into account that are imposed on more than
k
variables).
We say that
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
has
width
(l,k)
if it can be solved by the
(l,k)
-consistency procedure. We say that it has
bounded width
if it
has width
(l,k)

for some
l,k\in\mathbb{N}
, and otherwise we say that it has
unbounded width
.

Remark 65.2
.

A CSP has bounded width if and
only if unsatisfiability of an instance of
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
can be detected by a
Datalog program
(see
[
55
]
).

The following lemma suggests that
the universal-algebraic approach
can be used to study the question
for which structures
\mathfrak{B}

the problem
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
has bounded width.

Lemma 65.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
be structures with finite relational signature such that
\mathfrak{A}\in\HI(\mathfrak{B})
. If
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
has bounded width, then so does
\Csp(\mathfrak{A})
.

Proof.

Let
\tau
be the signature of
\mathfrak{A}

and
\sigma
the signature of
\mathfrak{B}
.
Suppose that
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
has width

(l,k)
.
Let
d
be the dimension of the primitive positive
interpretation
I
of
\mathfrak{A}
in

\mathfrak{B}
, let
\delta_{I}(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})
be the domain formula, and
let
h\colon D\rightarrow A
be the coordinate map where
D:=\{(b_{1},\dots,b_{d})\in B^{d}\mid\mathfrak{B}\models\delta_{I}(b_{1},\dots,b_{d})\}
.
Let
\phi
be an unsatisfiable instance of
\Csp(\mathfrak{A})
with variable set
U=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
.
From
\phi
we construct an unsatisfiable
instance
\psi
of
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
, and hence
the
(l,k)
-consistency algorithm derives the empty set. This derivation
will be used as a “guide” when we inductively show that
(l,k)
-consistency derives false on
\phi
.

For fresh and pairwise distinct variables

V:=\{y^{i}_{j}\mid 1\leq i\leq d,1\leq j\leq n\}

let

\psi_{1}
be

\bigwedge_{1\leq i\leq n}\delta_{I}(y_{i}^{1},\dots,y_{i}^{d})\;.

Let
\psi_{2}
be the conjunction of
the formulas
\theta_{I}(y_{i_{1}}^{1},\dots,y_{i_{1}}^{d},\dots,y_{i_{k}}^{1},\dots,y_{i_{k}}^{d})

over all conjuncts
\theta=R(x_{i_{1}},\dots,x_{i_{k}})
of
\phi
.
By moving existential quantifiers to the front, the sentence

\exists y_{1}^{1},\dots,y_{n}^{d}\;(\psi_{1}\wedge\psi_{2})

can be re-written to a primitive positive
\sigma
-formula
\psi
.

We claim that
\psi
is unsatisfiable in
\mathfrak{B}
. Suppose for contradiction that
f\colon V\rightarrow B
satisfies all conjuncts of
\psi
in
\mathfrak{B}
.
By construction of
\psi
, if
\phi
has a conjunct

\theta=R(x_{i_{1}},\dots,x_{i_{k}})
, then

\mathfrak{B}\models\theta_{I}((f(y_{i_{1}}^{1}),\dots,f(y_{i_{1}}^{d})),\dots,(f(y_{i_{k}}^{1}),\dots,f(y_{i_{k}}^{d})))\;.

By the definition of interpretations,
this implies that

\mathfrak{A}\models R(h(f(y_{i_{1}}^{1}),\dots,f(y_{i_{1}}^{d})),\dots,h(f(y_{i_{k}}^{1}),\dots,f(y_{i_{k}}^{d})))\;.

Hence, the mapping
g\colon U\rightarrow A
that sends

x_{i}
to
h(f(y_{i}^{1}),\dots,f(y_{i}^{d}))
satisfies all conjuncts of
\phi
in
\mathfrak{A}
, in contradiction to the assumption that
\phi
is unsatisfiable.

Since
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
has width
(l,k)
we consequently have that the
(l,k)
-consistency procedure applied to
\psi
derives
{\it false}
.
This derivation can be used to show that
the
(l,k)
-consistency procedure applied to
\phi
derives
{\it false}
, too. We leave the details to the reader.

∎

Remark 65.4
.

Recall from Corollary
19.10
that
every structure
\mathfrak{B}
with finite relational
signature there exists a structure
\mathfrak{C}
with maximal arity
2
such that
\mathfrak{B}
and
\mathfrak{C}
are primitively positively bi-interpretable. It therefore follows from
Lemma
65.3
that
we may assume without loss of generality that
\mathfrak{B}
has maximal arity
2
when proving Theorem
65.1
.

65.2
Unbounded Width

In this section we prove that
CSPs for solving
linear equations
over non-trivial abelian groups
has unbounded width, i.e.,
for every
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
there exists an unsatisfiable instance
\mathfrak{A}
such that the
k
-consistency procedure
does not derive false on
\mathfrak{A}
.
Two proof sketches of this fact can be found
in
[
55
]
. A stronger non-expressibility can be
found in
[
4
]

(the given CSP is not even expressible
in least fixed point logic with counting quantifiers).
The presentation in this section, however, is from
[
20
]
.

The
girth
of an undirected graph
G
is
the length of a shortest cycle in
G
.
A graph is called
k
-regular
if every vertex has precisely
k
neighbours. A 3-regular graph
is also called
cubic
.
A great deal is known about the existence of finite graphs of high girth.

Theorem 65.5
.

For every
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
there exists

•

a finite four-regular graph of girth at least
k
(it is even known that there are such graphs of exponential size in
k

[
66
]
);

•

a finite cubic graph of girth at least
k
with a Hamiltonian cycle (see
the comments after the proof of Theorem 3.2
in
[
17
]
).

Definition 65.6
.

Let
\tau
be a relational structure.
Then the
incidence graph

G(\mathfrak{A})
of
\mathfrak{A}

is the graph whose set of vertices is
the disjoint union of
A
and the set of tuples in relations from
\mathfrak{A}
,
and where an element from
A
is connected to a tuple if and only if the element appears in that tuple.

Note that
G(\mathfrak{A})
is bipartite.
We say that
\mathfrak{A}
has
girth
k
if all tuples in relations from
\mathfrak{A}
have pairwise distinct entries and the shortest
cycle of
G(\mathfrak{A})
has
2k
edges.
The following is a special case of Lemma 8.6.6. in
[
20
]
.

Lemma 65.7
.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a finite structure
whose
signature
\tau
consists of finitely many relation symbols
of arity at least 3
such that for every relation
R
of
\mathfrak{B}
of arity
r
, every
(r-1)
-ary projection of
R
equals the full relation
B^{r-1}
.
Suppose that
there exist unsatisfiable instances of
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
of arbitrarily large girth.
Then
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
has unbounded width.

Let
G
be an abelian group and
c\in G
.
We define

\displaystyle R^{k}_{c}

\displaystyle:=\{(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})\in G^{k}\mid x_{1}+\cdots+x_{k}=c\}.

Theorem 65.8

(Feder and Vardi
[
55
]
)
.

Let
G
be an abelian group with at least two elements and let

\mathfrak{G}
be the structure with domain
G

that contains the relation
R^{3}_{c}
for every
c\in A
.
Then the problem
\Csp(\mathfrak{G})
is not in Datalog.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be the structure with domain
G
which contains
for every function
f\colon\{1,2,3\}\to\{-1,1\}
and every
a\in G
the relation

R_{f,a}:=\{(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in G^{3}\mid f(x_{1})x_{1}+f(2)x_{2}+f(3)x_{3}=a\}.

These relations are primitively positively definable in
\mathfrak{G}
and by Lemma
65.3

it suffices to show that
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
is not in Datalog.
All the relations of
\mathfrak{B}
satisfy the requirements from Lemma
65.7
. It thus suffices to construct an unsatisfiable instance
\mathfrak{A}
of
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
of girth at least
k
. Let
(V;E)
be a cubic graph of girth at least
k
(Theorem
65.5
).
Orient the edges
E
arbitrarily.
The domain of
\mathfrak{A}
is

E
.
For each
v\in V

with the three
incident (oriented)
edges
e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}

we add the constraint
R_{f,0}(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3})
to
\mathfrak{A}
where
f(i)=-1
if
v
is the first vertex of
e_{i}
, and
f(i)=1
if
v
is the second vertex of
e_{i}
.
Then we move exactly one of the tuples from
R_{f,0}
in
\mathfrak{A}
to the relation
R_{f,a}
for some
a\in B\setminus\{0\}
.
Suppose for contradiction that
s\colon A\to B
is a solution for
\mathfrak{A}
.
Sum over all constraints. Since each edge appears once positively and once negatively, the left-hand side is
0
.
On the right-hand side we obtain
a
since we have precisely one tuple in
\mathfrak{A}
from
R_{f,0}

to
R_{f,a}
.
Hence,
s
cannot be a homomorphism, a contradiction.
∎

Corollary 65.9
.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a finite structure with finite relational signature. If

\mathfrak{B}
pp-constructs
({\mathbb{Z}}_{p};+,1)
,
for some prime
p
, then
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
has unbounded width.

Proof.

Having unbounded width is preserved by pp-interpretations (Theorem
65.3
)
and homomorphic equivalence.
For every
c\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
, the relation
R^{3}_{c}
, is pp-definable in
({\mathbb{Z}}_{p};+,1)
.
Hence, the statement follows from Theorem
65.8
.
∎

Using Corollary
62.2
,
the assumption to
not
pp-construct

({\mathbb{Z}}_{p};+,1)
can be transformed into a useful property.

Corollary 65.10
.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a finite structure which for every prime
p
does
not
pp-construct

({\mathbb{Z}}_{p};+,1)
, and suppose that

\Pol(\mathfrak{B})=\Clo({\bf B})
for some idempotent algebra
{\bf B}
with at least two elements. Then either
{\bf B}
has a proper binary absorbing subuniverse, a proper centrally absorbing subuniverse, or
a subdirectly complete quotient with at least two elements.

65.3
Singleton AC

Singleton Arc-Consistency
(SAC) comes close to strategies that humans perform when solving Sudoku puzzles.
It relies on the arc-consistency procedure from Section
5
, generalised to finite relational structures (Exercise
86
, Definition
43.22
).

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a finite structure with a finite relational signature
\tau
.
The algorithm
\SAC_{\mathfrak{B}}

takes as input a
\tau
-structure
\mathfrak{A}

and works as follows.
First, we run
\AC_{\mathfrak{B}}
on
\mathfrak{A}
; if it detects an inconsistency, we reject. Then we repeatedly perform the following steps.

•

First create a copy
L^{\prime}
of every list
L
computed by
\AC_{\mathfrak{B}}
.

•

Pick a variable
a
and set
L^{\prime}(a)
to
\{b\}
for some value
b\in L(a)
.

•

Again establish arc-consistency starting with the lists
L^{\prime}
.

•

If AC detects an inconsistency, we permanently remove the value
b
from
L(a)
.

This is done for all pairs
(a,b)\in A\times B
until the lists
L
do not change any more.
If in this way one of the lists becomes empty, the procedure returns
No
, otherwise
Yes
.
Obviously, if the algorithm returns
No
, then there is no homomorphism from
\mathfrak{A}
to
\mathfrak{B}
.
The pseudo-code is
displayed in Figure
23
.

\SAC_{\mathfrak{B}}(\mathfrak{A})

Input: a finite structure
\mathfrak{A}
with finite relational signature.

Data structure: a list
L(a)\subseteq B
for each
a\in A
, initially set to
B
.

Do

Run
\AC_{\mathfrak{B}}(\mathfrak{A})
starting with the lists
L
; if it derives
{\it false}
, return
\bf No
.

Create a copy
L^{\prime}
of the lists
L
computed by AC.

Pick some
a\in A
and some value
b\in L(a)
and set
L^{\prime}(a):=\{b\}
.

Run AC starting with the lists
L^{\prime}
.

If it derives false, remove
b
from
L(a)
.

Loop until for no pair
(a,b)\in A\times B
the value
b
is removed from
L(a)
.

Return
\bf Yes
.

Figure 23:
The singleton arc-consistency procedure for
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
.

Remark 65.11
.

If
k
is the maximal arity of
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
, and
\SAC_{\mathfrak{B}}
returns
No
, then the

(2,k)
-consistency procedure returns
No
as well.

Definition 65.12

(Singleton arc-consistency)
.

An instance
\mathfrak{A}
of
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
together with sets
L(a)\subseteq B
for every
a\in A
is called
singleton arc-consistent (with respect to
\mathfrak{B}
)

if for every
b\in L(a)
, the instance
\mathfrak{A}
together with
L^{\prime}(a):=\{b\}
and
L^{\prime}(a^{\prime}):=L(a^{\prime})
for all
a^{\prime}\in A\setminus\{a\}
,
is arc-consistent (Definition
43.23
).

Remark 65.13
.

Note that
\mathfrak{A}
together with the sets
L(a)
, for
a\in A
computed by
\SAC_{\mathfrak{B}}
at the final stage of the algorithm, is singleton arc-consistent.

In this section we present an algebraic characterisation of the power of
\SAC_{\mathfrak{B}}
, similar to our characterisation of the power of
\AC_{\mathfrak{B}}
in Theorem
43.24
.
We start with an analog of the concept of the powerset graph
P(H)
from Section
5.1
and the powerset structure
P(\mathfrak{B})
from Exercise
86
.
The following construction was found by Chen, Dalmau, and Grussien
[
43
]
.

Definition 65.14
.

Let
I
be a set.
Then

P_{\SAC}^{I}(\mathfrak{B})

is the substructure of
P({\mathfrak{B}})^{I}
whose universe contains

s\colon I\to P({\mathfrak{B}})

if and only if

\bigcup_{i\in I}s(i)=\bigcup_{i\in I,|s(i)|=1}s(i).

The following is the analog of Lemma
5.3
for SAC instead of AC.

Lemma 65.15
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
be structures with finite relational signature
\tau
and finite domains.
Then
\SAC_{\mathfrak{B}}
accepts
\mathfrak{A}
if and only if

\mathfrak{A}\to P^{A\times B}_{\SAC}(\mathfrak{B})
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\SAC_{\mathfrak{B}}
accepts an instance
\mathfrak{A}
.
For
a\in A
, let
L(a)

denote the (non-empty) set computed by the algorithm at the point of termination.
Let
b\in L(a)
.
By the definition of the algorithm and Lemma
5.3
, there exists a homomorphism
h_{a,b}
from

(\mathfrak{A},\{a_{1}\},\{a_{2}\},\dots,\{a_{n}\},\{a\})

to
P((\mathfrak{B},L(a_{1}),\dots,L(a_{n}),\{b\}))
.
For
b\in B\setminus L(a)
, we define

h_{a,b}
equal to
h_{a,b^{\prime}}
for some
b^{\prime}\in L(a)
.

Define
h(a^{\prime})
to be the function that maps

(a,b)
to
h_{a,b}(a^{\prime})\subseteq B
.
Since
h_{a,b}
is in particular a homomorphism from
\mathfrak{A}
to
P(\mathfrak{B})
,
we have that
h
is a homomorphism from

\mathfrak{A}
to
P(\mathfrak{B})^{A\times B}
. In fact, it is even a homomorphism to
P^{A\times B}_{\SAC}(\mathfrak{B})
, because for every
a^{\prime}\in A
we have

\displaystyle\bigcup_{a\in A,b\in B}h(a^{\prime})(a,b)

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{a\in A,b\in L(a)}h_{a,b}(a^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{a\in A,b\in L(a),|h_{a,b}(a^{\prime})|=1}h_{a,b}(a^{\prime})

(40)

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{a\in A,b\in B,|h(a^{\prime})(a,b)|=1}h(a^{\prime})(a,b).

To see the inclusion

\subseteq
in (
40
), let
b^{\prime}\in h_{a,b}(a^{\prime})
. It follows that
b^{\prime}\in L(a^{\prime})
, and that

\{b^{\prime}\}\in h_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}(a^{\prime})
, so
b^{\prime}
is also contained in the union shown in (
40
).

Conversely, suppose that
h
is a homomorphism from
\mathfrak{A}
to
P^{A\times B}_{\SAC}(\mathfrak{B})
. Let
L(a^{\prime})
be the list computed for
a^{\prime}\in A
by
\SAC_{\mathfrak{B}}

at the final stage of the execution of

\SAC_{\mathfrak{B}}
on the instance
\mathfrak{A}
. We know that
h(a^{\prime})\in P^{A\times B}_{\SAC}(\mathfrak{B})
is such that there exists
(a,b)\in A\times B
such that
|h(a^{\prime})(a,b)|=1
.
Let
b^{\prime}\in B
be such that
h(a^{\prime})(a,b)=\{b^{\prime}\}
.
We claim that
L(a^{\prime})
contains
b^{\prime}
, and in particular that it is non-empty.

The claim is trivially true after initialisation.
It also holds after the execution of

\AC_{\mathfrak{B}}(\mathfrak{A})
, because

a^{\prime}\mapsto h(a^{\prime})(a,b)
is a homomorphism from
\mathfrak{A}
to
P(\mathfrak{B})
, and hence the claim follows from Lemma
5.3
.
This homomorphism maps
a^{\prime}
to
b^{\prime}
, and hence the element
b^{\prime}
is not removed from
L(a^{\prime})
. This concludes the proof of the claim.
∎

Proposition 65.16
.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a structure with finite signature and finite domain.
Then

\SAC_{\mathfrak{B}}
solves

\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
if and only if

P^{I}_{\SAC}(\mathfrak{B})\to\mathfrak{B}
for every finite set
I
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\SAC_{\mathfrak{B}}
solves

\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
, and let
I
be a finite set. To show that
P^{I}_{\SAC}(\mathfrak{B})\to\mathfrak{B}
, we view
P^{I}_{\SAC}(\mathfrak{B})
as an instance of
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
.
Clearly,
P^{I}_{\SAC}(\mathfrak{B})\to P^{I}_{\SAC}(\mathfrak{B})
, and hence Lemma
65.15
implies that

\SAC_{\mathfrak{B}}
accepts
P^{I}_{\SAC}(\mathfrak{B})
.
Thus,
P^{I}_{\SAC}(\mathfrak{B})\to\mathfrak{B}
, because

\SAC_{\mathfrak{B}}
solves

\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
.

For the other direction,
let
\mathfrak{A}
be a finite structure with the same signature as
\mathfrak{B}
.
If
\SAC_{\mathfrak{B}}
rejects
\mathfrak{A}
, then clearly there is no homomorphism
\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
.
Otherwise, by Lemma
65.15
there exists a homomorphism from
\mathfrak{A}
to
P^{A\times B}_{\SAC}(\mathfrak{B})
.
By assumption, there exists a homomorphism
h\colon P^{A\times B}_{\SAC}(\mathfrak{B})\to\mathfrak{B}
.
By composing with
h
we obtain a homomorphism from
\mathfrak{A}
to

\mathfrak{B}
.
∎

65.4
Palette Totally Symmetric Polymorphisms

Similarly as the equivalence of the existence of a homomorphism from
P(\mathfrak{B})
to
\mathfrak{B}

and the existence of totally symmetric polymorphisms of all arities of
\mathfrak{B}
(Theorem
7.1
),
we can characterise the existence of homomorphisms from
P^{I}_{\SAC}(\mathfrak{B})

to
\mathfrak{B}
for all finite sets
I
in terms of a polymorphism condition (this result is missing in
[
43
]
). We use slightly simplified terminology of Zhuk
[
100
]
.

Definition 65.17

(Palette tuples)
.

Let
\ell,k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\geq 1}
.
A matrix
t\in B^{\ell\times k}
is called

palette
if for every
i\in[\ell]

and
j\in[k]
there exists
i^{\prime}\in[\ell]
such that
the
i^{\prime}
-th column of
t
equals
(t_{i,j},\dots,t_{i,j})
.

Definition 65.18
.

Let
\ell,k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\geq 1}
.
An operation
f\colon B^{\ell\times k}\to B
is called
(\ell\times k)
-palette totally symmetric

if
f(s)=f(t)

for all palette matrices
s,t\in B^{\ell\times k}
and
i\in[\ell]
such that

•

the
i
-th column of
s
and the
i
-th column of
t
have the same sets of entries, and

•

in all other columns,
s
and
t
have the same entries.

The following is similar to one of the implications in Theorem
7.1
.

Proposition 65.19
.

Let
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a structure
with
domain
B=\{b_{1},\dots,b_{n}\}
,
maximal arity
m
, and
an
(\ell\times mn)
-palette totally
symmetric polymorphism
f
.
Then

P^{[{\ell}]}_{\SAC}(\mathfrak{B})\to\mathfrak{B}
.

Proof.

Let
s\colon[{\ell}]\to P(\mathfrak{B})
be an element of

P^{[{\ell}]}_{\SAC}(\mathfrak{B})
.
For
i\in[{\ell}]
let
k_{i}\in[n]
be such that

s(i)=\{c^{i}_{1},\dots,c^{i}_{k_{i}}\}
.
Define

\displaystyle h(s):=f(\underbrace{c^{1}_{1},\dots,c^{1}_{k_{1}},\dots,c^{1}_{k_{1}}}_{\text{length }mn},\dots,\underbrace{c^{\ell}_{1},\dots,c^{\ell}_{k_{n}},\dots,c^{\ell}_{k_{n}}}_{\text{length }mn})

(41)

i.e., for every
i\in[{\ell}]
the element
c^{i}_{k_{i}}
is repeated so that the tuple
(c^{i}_{1},\dots,c^{i}_{k_{i}},\dots,c^{i}_{k_{i}})
has length
mn
. We view the tuple
c:=(c^{1}_{1},\dots,c^{\ell}_{mn})
in (
41
) as a matrix from
B^{\ell\times mn}
.

Claim 1.

h
is well defined. For this, we need to argue that for every
i\in[\ell]
,
the result of
h(s)
does not depend on the order and multiplicities of the arguments
c^{i}_{1},\dots,c^{i}_{k_{i}}
.
Since
s
is an element of
P^{[{\ell}]}_{\SAC}(\mathfrak{B})
,
for every

p\in[k_{i}]
we have
c^{i}_{p}\in\bigcup_{i^{\prime}\in[\ell]}s(i^{\prime})=\bigcup_{i^{\prime}\in[\ell],|s(i^{\prime})|=1}s(i^{\prime})
,
and hence there exists
i^{\prime}\in[\ell]
such that
s(i^{\prime})=\{c^{i}_{p}\}
, i.e.,
the
i^{\prime}
-th column of
c

equals

(c^{i}_{p},\dots,c^{i}_{p})
. Hence,
c
is palette.
It follows that the definition of
h
does not depend on the order and the multiplicities of the entries in each block.

Claim 2.

h
is a
homomorphism from

P^{[\ell]}_{\SAC}(\mathfrak{B})
to
\mathfrak{B}
. The argument is similar as in the proof of the implication
3.\Rightarrow 1.
of Theorem
7.1
.
Let
R\in\tau
; we may assume without loss of generality that
R
has arity
m
.
Let
(s_{1},\dots,s_{m})\in R^{P^{[\ell]}_{\SAC}(\mathfrak{B})}
.
Let
i\in[\ell]
,
j\in[m]
,
and
k_{i,j}\in[n]
be such that

s_{j}(i)=\{c^{i,j}_{1},\dots,c^{i,j}_{k_{i,j}}\}
.
Define
c^{i,j}_{k_{i,j}+1}=\dots=c^{i,j}_{mn}:=c^{i,j}_{k_{i,j}}
.
For each
k\in[mn]
and
p\in[m]
,
we can choose
c_{k,p}^{i}\in s_{k}(b_{i})
, with

c_{i,j}^{k}=c^{i,j}_{k}
, such that

(c_{k,1}^{i},\dots,c_{k,m}^{i})\in R^{\mathfrak{B}}
.
For
i\in[\ell]
and
k\in[mn]
, let

c^{i}_{k}
be the tuple

(c^{i}_{k,1},\dots,c^{i}_{k,m})
.
Applying
f
to the tuples

c^{1}_{1},\dots,c^{1}_{mn},\dots,c^{\ell}_{1},\dots,c^{\ell}_{mn}

yields a tuple
t
in
R^{\mathfrak{B}}
, because
f
is a polymorphism.
However,
t=h(s_{1},\dots,s_{m})
, since
f
is
(\ell\times mn)
-palette totally symmetric,
so
h
is indeed a homomorphism
from

P_{\SAC}(\mathfrak{B})
to
\mathfrak{B}
.
∎

The converse implication in Theorem
65.19
also holds; this was originally shown by Zhuk
[
100
]
. His argument involved additionally another algorithm (the ‘constraint singleton arc consistency’ procedure) and the Hales-Jewett theorem from Ramsey theory; our proof is elementary.

Theorem 65.20
.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a finite structure
with
domain
B=\{b_{1},\dots,b_{n}\}

and
maximal arity
m
.
Then the following are equivalent.

1.

\mathfrak{B}
has for all
\ell,k\in{\mathbb{N}}
an
(\ell\times k)
-palette totally symmetric polymorphism.

2.

\mathfrak{B}
has for every
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
an
(\ell\times nm)
-palette totally
symmetric polymorphism.

3.

P^{I}_{\SAC}(\mathfrak{B})\to\mathfrak{B}
for every finite set
I
.

4.

\SAC_{\mathfrak{B}}
solves
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
.

Proof.

The implication from 1 to 2 is trivial, and from 2 to 3 it is Proposition
65.19
.
The equivalence of 3 and 4 is Proposition
65.16
. For the implication from 3 to 1,
let
\ell,k\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Let
h\colon P^{[\ell]}_{\SAC}(\mathfrak{B})\to\mathfrak{B}
be a homomorphism.
To define
f\colon B^{l\times k}\to B
, let

x\in B^{l\times k}
.
Let
s\colon[\ell]\to P(\mathfrak{B})
be defined by

s(i):=\{x_{i,1},\dots,x_{i,k}\}
for
i\in[\ell]
. Define

f(x_{1,1},\dots,x_{1,k},x_{2,1},\dots,x_{2,k},\dots,x_{\ell,1},\dots,x_{\ell,k}):=h(s).

We have to verify that
f
is
(\ell,k)
-palette totally symmetric, and a polymorphism of
\mathfrak{B}
.
To verify that
f
is
(\ell,k)
-palette totally symmetric, let
t,t^{\prime}\in B^{\ell\times k}
and
i\in[\ell]
be such that

•

\{t_{i,1},\dots,t_{i,k}\}=\{t^{\prime}_{i,1},\dots,t^{\prime}_{i,k}\}
, and

•

t_{i^{\prime},j}=t^{\prime}_{i^{\prime},j}
for
i^{\prime}\in[\ell]\setminus\{i\}
and
j\in[k]
.

We have to show that
f(t)=f(t^{\prime})
.
Let
s,s^{\prime}\colon[\ell]\to P(\mathfrak{B})
be defined for
i\in[\ell]
by

\displaystyle s(i)

\displaystyle:=\{t_{i,1},\dots,t_{i,k}\}

\displaystyle\text{ and }\quad s^{\prime}(i)

\displaystyle:=\{t^{\prime}_{i,1},\dots,t^{\prime}_{i,k}\}.

By our assumptions for
t
and
t^{\prime}
, we have
s=s^{\prime}
, and hence
f(t)=h(s)=h(s^{\prime})=f(t^{\prime})
.

To verify that
f
is a polymorphism of
\mathfrak{B}
,
we have to verify that it preserves
R^{\mathfrak{B}}
for every
R\in\tau
. We may assume without loss of generality that
R
has arity
m
. Let
t_{1},\dots,t_{k}
be
m
-tuples from
R^{\mathfrak{B}}
.
For each
j\in[m]
,
let
s_{j}\colon[\ell]\to P(\mathfrak{B})
be the map that sends
i\in[\ell]
to the set
\{(t_{i})_{1},\dots,(t_{i})_{k}\}\in P(\mathfrak{B})
.
Note that for each
i\in[\ell]
,
we have
(s_{1}(i),\dots,s_{m}(i))\in R^{P(\mathfrak{B})}
, and thus
(s_{1},\dots,s_{m})\in P^{[\ell]}_{\SAC}(\mathfrak{B})
. Since
h
is a homomorphism, it follows that

f(t_{1},\dots,t_{k})=(h(s_{1}),\dots,h(s_{m}))\in R^{\mathfrak{B}}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

65.5
Tree Formulas

Our goal is to prove that if for every prime
p
the structure
({\mathbb{Z}}_{p};+,1)
does not have a primitive positive construction in
\mathfrak{B}
, then
\SAC_{\mathfrak{B}}
solves
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
. For this, we need some preparatory work in the next subsections. The content of this section can be seen as a generalisation of the content of Section
5.2
about the connection between tree duality and arc-consistency.

Throughout, let
\mathfrak{B}
be a finite structure with a finite relational signature
\tau
, and let
{\bf B}
be an algebra such that

\Clo({\bf B})=\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
.
We may assume without loss of generality that
\mathfrak{B}
contains all unary relations that are primitively positively definable in
\mathfrak{B}
;
adding these does not change the computational complexity of
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
,
and also does not change whether

\SAC_{\mathfrak{B}}
solves
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
, by Theorem
65.20
.
However, it is useful for formulating some consistency conditions and algorithms.

Definition 65.21
.

If
\mathfrak{A}
is an instance of
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
and
a\in A
, then we write
B^{\mathfrak{A}}_{a}\subseteq B
for the intersection of
R^{\mathfrak{B}}
for all unary
R\in\tau
such that
a\in R^{\mathfrak{A}}
; we call it the
variable domain
of
a
.

Remark 65.22
.

By our assumption on
\mathfrak{B}
above, we can use the unary relations of
\mathfrak{B}
to represent the lists derived by the arc-consistency procedure: if
\mathfrak{A}
is an instance of
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
, and
\AC
computes
the list
L(a)\subseteq B
, then
we have
L(a)\leq{\bf B}
(Exercise
162
), and hence

L(a)
is primitively positively definable in
\mathfrak{B}
. Therefore,
\mathfrak{B}
contains a unary relation
R
such that
R^{\mathfrak{B}}=L(a)
, and we may add
a
to
R^{\mathfrak{A}}
without changing the set of homomorphisms from
\mathfrak{A}
to
\mathfrak{B}
. In this case, we say that we
restrict
a
to
R^{\mathfrak{B}}
, and that we
reduce the variable domain of
a
.
From now on we therefore assume that
\AC
computes for a given instance
{\bf A}
of
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})

another instance
\mathfrak{A}^{*}
of
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})

such that
a\in R^{\mathfrak{A}^{*}}
with

R^{\mathfrak{B}}=L(a)
for every
a\in A
.
Consequently, the notion of ‘arc consistency’ (Definition
43.23
) applies to instances
\mathfrak{A}
of
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
and no longer refers to an additional set of subsets of
B
(the expression
b_{i}\in L(a_{i})
in Definition
43.23
can be replaced by ‘
b_{i}\in B^{\mathfrak{A}}_{a_{i}}
’).

Definition 65.23

(the incidence graph)
.

Let
\phi
be a primitive positive
\tau
-formula without equality conjuncts.
The
incidence graph

of
\phi
is the
following bipartite graph.
One of the two colour classes is the set
V
of variables of
\phi
, and the other colour class consists of all atomic formulas of
\phi
. There is an edge between
x\in V
and an atomic formula
R(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})
if
x=x_{i}
for
some
i\in[k]
.

In the following definition we slightly deviate from the presentation in
[
72
,
98
]
; we work with primitive positive formulas rather than structures.
If
\phi
is a tree formula, then
\tilde{\phi}
is the formula obtained from
\phi
by removing all conjuncts formed with unary relations.

Definition 65.24

(tree formulas, path formulas, series-parallel formulas)
.

Let
\phi
be
a primitive positive
\tau
-formula
with variables
V
such that for every atomic formula

R(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})
of
\phi
all
variables
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
are pairwise distinct. Then
\phi
is called a

•

tree formula
if
the incidence graph of
\phi
is a tree;

•

a
path formula
if in the incidence graph of
\tilde{\phi}

each free variable has degree one (called a
leaf of
\phi
), and every other vertex from
V
has degree one or two;

Observation 65.25
.

Corollary
5.9
can be generalised from digraphs to relational structures (also see Exercise
87
) as follows:
the arc-consistency procedure for
\mathfrak{B}
does not derive false on
\mathfrak{A}
if and only if
for every tree sentence
\phi
, if
\mathfrak{A}\models\phi
, then
\mathfrak{B}\models\phi
.

Definition 65.26

(Cycle-consistency)
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a finite
\tau
-structure. Then
\mathfrak{A}

is called
cycle-consistent (with respect to
\mathfrak{B}
)
if for every path formula
\phi(x,y)
,
if
\mathfrak{A}\models\phi(a,a)
for some
a\in A
, then
\mathfrak{B}\models\phi(b,b)
for every
b\in B^{\mathfrak{A}}_{a}
.

Remark 65.27
.

With the convention from Remark
65.22
,
singleton arc-consistency (Definition
65.12
) implies cycle-consistency, and cycle consistency implies arc-consistency (Definition
43.23
).
In particular, there is a polynomial-time algorithm that computes for a given instance
\mathfrak{A}
of
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
a cycle-consistent instance
\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}
of
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
which has the same set of solution as
\mathfrak{A}
, namely the singleton arc-consistency procedure (Figure
23
).

Exercises.

232.

Prove the claims in Remark
65.27
.

233.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be the expansion of
K_{3}
by singleton unary relations. Find an instance of
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
which is singleton arc-consistent, but not cycle-consistent.

234.

A
series-parallel formula

it is obtained recursively from atomic formulas as follows: if
\phi_{1}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})
and
\phi_{2}(y_{1},\dots,y_{\ell})
are series-parallel formulas, then the following formulas are series-parallel as well:

•

\phi_{1}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})\wedge\phi_{2}(x_{k},y_{2},\dots,y_{\ell})
(series composition), and

•

\phi_{1}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})\wedge\phi_{2}(x_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{\ell-1},x_{k})

(parallel composition).

Prove that
for every finite
\tau
-structure
\mathfrak{A}
, the following are equivalent.

(a)

\mathfrak{A}
is singleton arc-consistent with respect to
\mathfrak{B}
.

(b)

Every series-parallel sentence which holds in
\mathfrak{A}
also holds in
\mathfrak{B}
.

235.

We can use standard graph-theoretic terminology in the context of general relational structure by applying it to the incidence graph.
A graph
G
is called a
minor
of a graph
H
if
G
is isomorphic to a graph obtained from a subgraph of
H
by a sequence of edge contractions.

•

Prove that a connected graph
H
is a tree if and only if
K_{3}
is not a minor of
H
.

•

Prove that a graph
H
with a non-empty vertex set does not have
K_{4}
as a minor if and only if it is
series-parallel
(in the graph-theoretic sense), i.e., can be obtained
recursively from the one-element graph by a sequence of the following two operations.
For both operations, we start with
two series-parallel graphs
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
with
|V(G_{1})|,|V(G_{2})|<V(H)
, arbitrarily select
s_{1},t_{1}\in V(G_{1})
and
s_{2},t_{2}\in V(G_{2})
,
and them form a

–

parallel composition:

H
is obtained from
G_{1}\uplus G_{2}
by merging
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
and merging
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
.

–

series composition:
H
is obtained from
G_{1}\uplus G_{2}
by merging
t_{2}
and
s_{1}
.

•

Prove that a formula is series-parallel (Definition
65.24
) if and only if its incidence graph is series-parallel in the graph-theoretic sense.

236.

The incidence graph of a relational
\tau
-structure
\mathfrak{A}
should not be confused with the
Gaifman graph
of
\mathfrak{A}
, whose vertex set is
A
, and where
a_{1},a_{2}
are connected if there exists
R\in\tau

and
t\in R^{\mathfrak{A}}
with entries
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
. Prove that

•

if the maximal arity of the relations in
\tau
is two, then the Gaifman graph is acyclic if and only if the incidence graph is acyclic.

•

if the maximal arity of the relations in
\tau
is two, then the Gaifman graph is series-parallel if and only if the incidence graph is series-parallel.

•

Show that for unrestricted relational signatures, the above statements are false.

65.6
Reducing Variable Domains

In this section we will assume that
\mathfrak{B}
is a finite structure which does not pp-construct
({\mathbb{Z}}_{p};+,1)
for every prime
p
, and such that
\Pol(\mathfrak{B})=\Clo({\bf B})
for some idempotent algebra with at least two elements.
Recall from Corollary
65.10
that in this case
{\bf B}
and all its subuniverses with at least two elements have a proper binary or centrally absorbing subalgebra
or a subdirectly complete quotient.
Each of these cases is useful to reduce
the variable domains (see Remark
65.22
) in
instances
\mathfrak{A}
of
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
which satisfy various forms of consistency, while preserving some form of consistency.
The details of this reduction process
are subtle.
The proof is inspired by ideas from
[
99
]
.

The simplest case is the one where a variable domain
has a proper
binary
absorbing subalgebra.

Lemma 65.28
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be an instance of
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
,
c\in A
,
C
a proper 2-absorbing subalgebra of
B_{c}^{\mathfrak{A}}\leq{\bf B}
, and
\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}
the instance obtained from
\mathfrak{A}
by
restricting the variable
c
to
C
.
If
\mathfrak{A}
is arc-consistent,
then the arc-consistency procedure does not derive false on
\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Suppose for contradiction that the arc-consistency procedure derives false on
\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}
. By Observation
65.25
,
there exists a tree sentence
\phi

such that
\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}\models\phi
and
\mathfrak{B}\not\models\phi
.
Since
\mathfrak{A}
is arc-consistent,
\phi
must involve conjuncts of the form

R(x)
where
R^{\mathfrak{B}}=C
; choose
\phi
such that the number of such conjuncts in minimal. Let
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
be the variables
x
such that
\phi
contains the conjunct
R(x)
.
Let
S\subseteq B^{k}
be the relation defined by the formula obtained from
\phi
by removing
all conjuncts of the form
R(x)
and removing the quantifiers for the variables
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
, so that

x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
are the free variables (the other variables are still existentially quantified).
Note that
S\cap C^{k}=\emptyset
by our assumptions on
\phi
.
If
k=1
, then
S
is unary,
and
B_{c}^{\mathfrak{A}}\subseteq S
by the assumption that
\mathfrak{A}
is arc-consistent. Then
\emptyset\neq C\subseteq B_{c}^{\mathfrak{A}}\subseteq S
is in contradiction to
S\cap C=\emptyset
.

So suppose that
k\geq 2
.
By the minimality assumption for
k
we have

S\cap(C\times\dots\times C\times\underbrace{B}_{i}\times C\times\cdots\times C)\neq\emptyset

for every
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, so
S
is
C
-essential.
However,
C
is
2
-absorbing, hence
k
-absorbing, so we reach a contradiction with Lemma
58.5
.
∎

We can now apply the reduction process from Lemma
65.28
repeatedly to prove the following lemma.

Corollary 65.29
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a finite structure which is arc-consistent such that for every
a\in A
the subuniverse
B_{a}^{\mathfrak{A}}
of
{\bf B}
is non-empty. For every
a\in A
, let

C_{a}
be a minimal 2-absorbing subuniverse of
B_{a}^{\mathfrak{A}}\leq{\bf B}
. Then the instance obtained from
\mathfrak{A}
by restricting each
a\in A
to
C_{a}
is arc-consistent.

Proof.

If for every
a\in A
, we have
C_{a}=B_{a}^{\mathfrak{B}}
, then there is nothing to be shown. Otherwise, let
c\in A
be such that
C_{c}
is a proper 2-absorbing subuniverse of
B_{c}^{\mathfrak{A}}\leq{\bf B}
.
Let

\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}
be the instance obtained from
\mathfrak{A}
by restricting
c
to
C_{c}
, and let
\mathfrak{A}^{*}
be the arc-consistent instance computed from
\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}
by the arc-consistency procedure. Note that
B^{\mathfrak{A}^{*}}_{c}\subseteq C_{c}
,
and we even have equality by the minimality of
C_{c}
.
By Lemma
65.28
,
we have
B^{\mathfrak{A}^{*}}_{a}\neq\emptyset
for every
a\in A
.
We may then repeat the argument with
\mathfrak{A}^{*}
instead of
\mathfrak{A}
.
Since
\mathfrak{A}
is finite, this terminates after finitely many steps.
∎

Next, we consider the case that a variable domain has a proper centrally absorbing subuniverse.
Recall that central absorption implies 3-absorption
61.4
.
We would like to proceed as in Lemma
65.28
.
If
\mathfrak{A}
is arc-consistent and

\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}
is the instance obtained by reducing a variable to a proper 3-absorbing subalgebra, then unfortunately the arc-consistency procedure might derive false on
\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}
.
If
\mathfrak{A}
is even singleton arc-consistent, then the instance
\mathfrak{A}^{\prime\prime}
computed from
\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}

by the arc-consistency procedure
is arc-consistent, but not necessarily singleton arc-consistent, so we cannot repeat the reduction as we did in Corollary
65.29
.

However, and quite remarkably, it suffices
that
\mathfrak{A}
is singleton arc-consistent and that
\mathfrak{A}^{\prime\prime}
is arc-consistent to further reduce variable domains of
\mathfrak{A}^{\prime\prime}
that have proper 3-absorbing subuniverses.
This motivates the formulation in the following lemma.

Lemma 65.30
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a singleton arc-consistent instance of
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
,
and let
\mathfrak{A}_{1}
be obtained from
\mathfrak{A}
by
restricting each
a\in A
to
S_{a}\leq B^{\mathfrak{A}}_{a}
.
Suppose that
\mathfrak{A}_{1}
is arc-consistent,
and that for some
c\in A
, the sub-universe

S_{c}\leq{\bf B}
has a proper 3-absorbing subuniverse
C
. Let
\mathfrak{A}_{2}
be the instance obtained from
\mathfrak{A}_{1}
by restricting the variable
c
to
C
.
Then the arc-consistency procedure does not derive false on
\mathfrak{A}_{2}
.

Proof.

Suppose for contradiction that the arc-consistency procedure derives false on

\mathfrak{A}_{2}
. By Observation
65.25
,
there exists a tree sentence
\phi

such that
\mathfrak{A}_{2}\models\phi
and
\mathfrak{B}\not\models\phi
.
Since
\mathfrak{A}_{1}
is arc-consistent,
\phi
must involve conjuncts of the form

R(x)
where
R^{\mathfrak{B}}=C
; choose
\phi
such that the number of such conjuncts in minimal. Let
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
be the variables
x
such that
\phi
contains the conjunct
R(x)
.
Let
\psi
be the formula obtained from
\phi
by removing
all conjuncts of the form
R(x)
and removing the quantifiers for the variables
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
, so that

x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
are the free variables (the other variables are still existentially quantified).
Let
S\subseteq B^{k}
be the relation defined by
\psi
in
\mathfrak{A}_{1}
.
Note that
S\cap C^{k}=\emptyset
by our assumptions on
\phi
. Similarly as
in the proof of Lemma
65.28

we can rule out that
k=1
.

If
k\geq 3
, then
we reach a contradiction with Lemma
58.5
, because
C
is
3
-absorbing, hence
k
-absorbing (again, this is similar as in the proof of Lemma
65.28
).

So we must have
k=2
.
Let
b\in S_{c}
.
Let
\tilde{\psi}(x_{1},x_{2})
be the
formula obtained from
\psi(x_{1},x_{2})
by removing all unary conjuncts. Clearly,

\tilde{\psi}(x_{1},x_{2})
is still a tree formula.
Note that
\mathfrak{A}\models\tilde{\phi}(c,c)
,
and hence
\mathfrak{B}\models\tilde{\phi}(b,b)
by
the assumption that
\mathfrak{A}
is singleton arc-consistent. Let
s
be a map from the variables of
\phi
to
B
that witnesses
that
\mathfrak{B}\models\tilde{\phi}(b,b)
; in
particular, we have
f(x_{1})=f(x_{2})=b
.
Since
\mathfrak{B}\not\models\phi
, there exists a conjunct
S(y)
of
\phi
such that

f(y)\notin S^{\mathfrak{B}}
. Since
\psi
is a tree formula,
\psi(x_{1},x_{2})
can
be written as

\exists y\big(\psi_{1}(x_{1},y)\wedge\psi_{2}(y,x_{2})\big)
for some tree formulas

\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
, such that

\mathfrak{B}\not\models\psi_{1}(b,f(y))

or
\mathfrak{B}\not\models\psi_{2}(f(y),b)
.
In both cases, this contradicts the assumption that
\mathfrak{A}_{1}
is arc-consistent.
∎

In the case that the variable domain of
c\in A
has a subdirectly complete quotient,
we restrict
c
to an arbitrary congruence class (which is a subalgebra of the variable domain for
c
).
Similarly as before, we only need that the original instance is singleton arc-consistent,
and that an instance obtained from the original instance by variable restriction is arc-consistent, to further reduce the variable domain of
c
, and to re-establish arc-consistency without deriving false.

Lemma 65.31
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a singleton arc-consistent instance of
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
,
and let
\mathfrak{A}_{1}
be obtained from
\mathfrak{A}
by
restricting each
a\in A
to
S_{a}\leq B^{\mathfrak{A}}_{a}
.
Suppose that
\mathfrak{A}_{1}
is arc-consistent,
and that for some
c\in A
, the
subuniverse

S_{c}\leq{\bf B}
has a subdirectly complete quotient
{\bf C}={\bf B}/\theta
. Let
D
be a congruence class of
\theta
.
Let
\mathfrak{A}_{2}
be the instance obtained from
\mathfrak{A}_{1}
by restricting
c
to
D
.
Then the arc-consistency procedure does not derive false on
\mathfrak{A}_{2}
.

Proof.

As in the proof of Lemma
65.30
, we suppose for contradiction that there exists a tree sentence
\phi

such that
\mathfrak{A}_{2}\models\phi
and
\mathfrak{B}\not\models\phi
. Following the argument
in the first paragraph of that proof, we get that the relation
S\subseteq(S_{c})^{k}
defined by
the formula
\psi(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})
in
\mathfrak{A}_{1}
satisfies

S\cap D^{k}=\emptyset
(for
k\geq 2
).

Since
S\leq(S_{c})^{k}
is non-empty and subdirect, the relation

S/\theta:=\{(b_{1}/\theta,\dots,b_{k}/\theta)\mid(b_{1},\dots,b_{k})\}\leq{\bf C}^{k}

is non-empty and subdirect. By assumption,
{\bf C}
is
subdirectly complete and hence the relation

S/\theta
can be defined by a conjunction of atomic formulas in the relational structure whose relations are graphs of automorphisms of
{\bf C}
. This conjunction must contain a conjunct for an
\alpha\in\Aut({\bf C})
such that
\alpha(D)\neq D
, because
S\cap D^{k}=\emptyset
.
Without loss of generality, we may assume that this conjunct has the variables
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
.

Let
b\in S_{c}
. Let
\tilde{\psi}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})
be the tree formula obtained from
\psi(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})
by removing all unary conjuncts. Note that

\mathfrak{A}\models\tilde{\phi}(c,\dots,c)
, and hence
\mathfrak{B}\models\tilde{\phi}(b,\dots,b)
by the assumption that
\mathfrak{A}
is singleton arc-consistent. Let
s
be a map from the variables of
\phi
to
B
that witnesses that
\mathfrak{B}\models\tilde{\phi}(b,\dots,b)
; in particular, we have that
f(x_{1})=\cdots=f(x_{k})=b
. Since
\mathfrak{B}\not\models\phi
, there exists a conjunct
S(y)
of
\phi
such that
f(y)\notin S^{\mathfrak{B}}
.
Since
\tilde{\psi}
is a tree formula,

\psi
can be written as

\exists y\big(\psi_{1}(x_{1},y)\wedge\psi_{2}(y,x_{2})\big)
for some tree formulas
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
such that

\mathfrak{B}\not\models\psi_{1}(b,f(y))
or
\mathfrak{B}\not\models\psi_{2}(f(y),b)
. In both cases, this contradicts the singleton arc-consistency of
\mathfrak{A}
.
∎

The lemmata in this section lead to the following theorem, which was first proved by Kozik
[
71
]
, but with a different proof.

Theorem 65.32
.

Suppose that for every prime
p

the structure
\mathfrak{B}
does not pp-construct
({\mathbb{Z}}_{p};+,1)
.
If
\mathfrak{A}
is a singleton arc-consistent instance of
\Csp(\mathfrak{A})
such that
B_{a}^{\mathfrak{A}}\neq\emptyset
for every
a\in A
, then there is a homomorphism from
\mathfrak{A}
to
\mathfrak{B}
. In particular,
\SAC
solves
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
.

Proof.

Suppose that there exists
a\in A
such that

|B_{a}^{\mathfrak{A}}|\geq 2
.
By Corollary
65.10
,

B^{\mathfrak{A}}_{a}\leq{\bf B}
has
a proper binary or centrally absorbing subuniverse
C
or a subdirectly complete quotient with at least two elements.
In the case of a proper binary absorbing subuniverse, we apply
Lemma
65.28
to properly reduce the domain of
a
.
In the case of a centrally absorbing subuniverse, recall that central absorption implies 3-absorption (Proposition
61.4
),
and hence we can apply Lemma
65.30
to properly reduce the domain of
a
.
In the case of asubdirectly complete quotient with at least two elements, we can use
Lemma
65.31
to properly reduce the domain of
a
. In all cases, we obtain a structure that again satisfies the assumptions, so we can continue until for every
a\in A
there exists
b_{a}\in B
such that
B_{a}^{\mathfrak{A}}=\{b_{a}\}
. In this case, the arc-consistency of
\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}
implies that
a\mapsto b_{a}
is a homomorphism
\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{B}
, and hence
\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
.
The final statement follows from Remark
65.27
.
∎

Exercises.

237.

Show that if we additionally assume in Lemma
65.28

that
\mathfrak{A}
is
singleton
arc-consistent,
then the
singleton
arc-consistency procedure does not derive false
\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}
.
Can we modify Lemma
65.30
in the same way?

65.7
The Bounded Width Theorem

The following theorem strengthens Theorem
65.1
.
The implication
\ref{eq:width-bounded}.\Rightarrow\ref{eq:width-lineq}.
is Corollary
65.9
.
The converse implication from
\ref{eq:width-lineq}.\Rightarrow\ref{eq:width-bounded}.
was open for many years and has been conjectured by Larose and Zadori
[
78
]
(and in equivalent form, by Feder and Vardi
[
55
]
; also see
[
77
]
),
and was proven by Barto and Kozik
[
10
]
.

Theorem 65.33
.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a finite structure.
Then the following are equivalent.

1.

\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
has width
(l,k)
for some
l,k\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

2.

For every prime number
p
, the structure
({\mathbb{Z}}_{p};+,1)
does not have a primitive positive construction in
\mathfrak{B}
.

3.

\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
can be solved
by the singleton arc-consistency procedure (SAC).

4.

\Csp(\mathfrak{B})

has width
(2,k)
, where
k
is the maximal arity of
\mathfrak{B}
.

5.

\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
contains
3-4 weak near unanimity operations
, i.e., operations
f\in\WNU(3)
and
g\in\WNU(4)
satisfying

f(y,x,x)\approx g(y,x,x,x)\,.

Proof.

The implication
\ref{eq:width-bounded}.\Rightarrow\ref{eq:width-lineq}.
is Corollary
65.9
.

The implication
\ref{eq:width-lineq}.\Rightarrow\ref{eq:width-slac}.
is Theorem
65.32
.

The implication
\ref{eq:width-slac}.\Rightarrow\ref{eq:width-2k}.
is easy: a derivation of
{\it false}
by SAC can be simulated
by a derivation of
{\it false}
by
(2,k)
-consistency where
k
is the maximal arity of the relations in
\mathfrak{B}
.

The implication
\ref{eq:width-2k}.\Rightarrow\ref{eq:width-bounded}.
is trivial.

We are left with the task to prove that
5
. is equivalent to the other items.
For the implication

\ref{eq:width-3/4}.\Rightarrow\ref{eq:width-lineq}.
,
first note that if
\mathfrak{B}
has a
\WNU(k)
-polymorphism, then so do have
all structures in
\HI(\mathfrak{B})
(recall that we do not require that the operations in
\WNU(k)
are idempotent).
But the structure
({\mathbb{Z}}_{p};+,1)
does not have
\WNU(k)
polymorphisms for both
k=3
and
k=4
(see Example
64
).

The elegant proof of the implication
\ref{eq:width-slac}.\Rightarrow\ref{eq:width-3/4}.
is taken from
[
73
]
.
Let
{\bf B}
be a polymorphism algebra of
\mathfrak{B}
, and let
{\bf F}
be the free algebra
for
\HSP({\bf B})
over
\{x,y\}
(Definition
38.10
).
Let
R
be a 3-ary and
S
a 4-ary relation symbol, and let
\mathfrak{F}
be the
\{R,S\}
-structure with domain
F
where

•

R^{\mathfrak{F}}
is the subalgebra of
{\bf F}^{3}
generated by
\{(y,x,x)(x,y,x),(x,x,y)\}

and

•

S^{\mathfrak{F}}
is the subalgebra of
{\bf F}^{4}
generated by
\{(y,x,x,x)(x,y,x,x),(x,x,y,x),(x,x,x,y)\}
.

Note
R^{\mathfrak{F}}
and
S^{\mathfrak{F}}
are symmetric in the sense that for every tuple in one of these relations, every tuple with permuted entries is also in that relation.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be the instance of
\Csp(\mathfrak{F})

with domain
A=\{1,\dots,3|F|+1\}
and the relations

\displaystyle R^{\mathfrak{A}}

\displaystyle:=\{(a,b,c):|\{a,b,c\}|=3\}

\displaystyle S^{\mathfrak{A}}

\displaystyle:=\{(a,b,c,d):|\{a,b,c,d\}|=4\}.

We claim that
\SAC_{\mathfrak{F}}
returns
Yes
on input
\mathfrak{A}
.
It suffices to show that
\mathfrak{A}
together with
L(a)=A
for every
a\in A
is singleton arc-consistent (see Remark
65.13
).
Let
a\in A
and
b\in F
. Let
L^{\prime}(a):=\{b\}
and
L^{\prime}(a^{\prime}):=L(a^{\prime})
for all
a^{\prime}\in A\setminus\{a\}
. There exists a term
t
such that
b=t(x,y)
. Then

t((x,y,x),(y,x,x))=(t(x,y),t(y,x),t(y,x))\in R^{\mathfrak{F}}
. Analogous reasoning for
R^{\mathfrak{F}}
shows that
\mathfrak{A}
together with the lists
L^{\prime}
is arc consistent.
Since
\mathfrak{A}
is symmetric, this completes the proof that
\mathfrak{A}
together with the lists
L
is singleton arc-consistent.

Hence,
3
. implies
that there exists a homomorphism
h\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{F}
. Since
|A|>3|F|
, there exists
\{a,b,c,d\}\subseteq A
with
|\{a,b,c,d\}|=4
such that
t:=h(a)=h(b)=h(c)=h(d)\in F
.
Since
(a,b,c,d)\in S^{\mathfrak{A}}
, we have
(t,t,t,t)\in S^{\mathfrak{F}}
.
By the definition of
S^{\mathfrak{F}}
,
there exists a term
g(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})

such that
g(y,x,x,x)=g(x,y,x,x)=g(x,x,y,x)=g(x,x,y,x)=t
.
Similarly,
(a,b,c)\in R^{\mathfrak{A}}
, so we have

(t,t,t)\in R^{\mathfrak{F}}
.
By the definition of
S^{\mathfrak{F}}
,
there exists a term
f(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})

such that
f(y,x,x)=f(x,y,x)=f(x,x,y)=t
.
The statement now follows from Lemma
38.11
.
∎

Remark 65.34
.

Another condition that is equivalent to the conditions listed in Theorem
65.33
is that
\mathfrak{B}
has a binary polymorphism
f_{2}
and polymorphisms
f_{n}\in\WNU(n)
for every
n\geq 3

and

f_{n}(x,y,\dots,y)\approx f_{2}(x,y)\,.

This follows from
[
68
, Proposition 4.1]
.

Remark 65.35
.

Yet another condition which is equivalent to the conditions listed in Theorem
65.33
is that
\mathfrak{B}
has ternary polymorphisms
p,q
such that
p\in\WNU(3)
and

\displaystyle p(x,x,y)\approx q(x,y,x)\quad\text{ and }\quad q(x,x,y)\approx q(x,y,y).

This condition from
[
68
, Corollary 3.3]
appears to be the most useful one for checking whether
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
has bounded width for a given finite structure
\mathfrak{B}
.

We close this section with some immediate consequences of Theorem
65.33
.

Corollary 65.36
.

Let
H
be a finite digraph.
Then strong path consistency solves

\Csp(H)
if and only if
H

has weak near unanimity polymorphisms
f
and
g

satisfying

g(y,x,x)\approx f(y,x,x,x).

Another remarkable consequence is that
for the
H
-colouring problem,

(2,3)
-consistency is as powerful as

(2,k)
-consistency for all
k\geq 3
(the
‘collapse of the bounded width hirarchy’
; we already stated this in Theorem
9.2
).

Quite remarkably, the results in this section imply strong results in universal algebra that were only discovered in the study of local consistency (but that can be stated without mentioning local consistency at all).

Corollary 65.37
.

Let
{\bf A}
be a finite idempotent algebra. Then the following are equivalent.

•

{\bf A}
has 3-4 weak near unanimity terms.

•

\HS({\bf A})
does not contain an affine algebra with at least two elements.

Exercises.

238.

Prove Corollary
65.37
.

239.

Show that
\Csp(\mathfrak{B})
has bounded width if and only if
\Pol(\mathfrak{B})
contains for every
n\geq 3
an operation from
\WNU(n)
.

66
Open Problems

The Feder and Vardi dichotomy conjecture
[
55
]
has been the outstanding open problem in the field; it was solved in 2017 by Bulatov
[
35
]
and, independently, by
Zhuk
[
101
]
. The boarder between polynomial and NP-hard cases has numerous equivalent logical and algebraic characterisations, for example characterisations based on primitive positive constructability and
characterisations based on identities that are satisfied by the polymorphism clone (see Corollary
64.1
).

There are many interesting problems in the field that are still left open.
We start with open research problems where all the relevant concepts have already been introduced in the course.

1.

Is there a
uniform
algorithm for finite-domain CSPs, i.e., is there a polynomial-time algorithm that takes as input a pair
(\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B})
of finite structures with the same finite relational signature, where
\mathfrak{B}
is promised to have a Taylor polymorphism (for equivalent promises, see Corollary
64.1
), that decides whether there is a homomorphism from
\mathfrak{A}
to
\mathfrak{B}
. This question is also open if we replace ‘Taylor polymorphism’ by ‘Maltsev polymorphism’.

2.

Is there a polynomial-time algorithm to determine whether a given core structure
\mathfrak{B}
has a Siggers polymorphism? Is this true for the special case where
\mathfrak{B}
is a digraph or an orientation of a tree? This problem is known to be NP-complete if
\mathfrak{B}
is not required to be a core structure
[
44
]
.
This problem is known to be in P if
\mathfrak{B}
is a core and there is a uniform algorithm for finite-domain CSPs (as in the previous question; see
[
44
]
).

3.

Is the class of all finite structures, ordered by pp-constructability and factored by the respective equivalence relation
[
29
,
30
,
85
]
, countably infinite or uncountably infinite? Is it a lattice? Are there infinite ascending chains? Can we answer these questions when restricting to finite directed graphs
[
28
,
27
,
95
]
?

4.

What is the computational complexity of determining whether
a given finite core structure
H
has tree duality? Is this problem in P? Is it in P if
H
is a digraph or even an orientation of a tree?

5.

(Bulín
[
39
]
) Is is true that the CSP of an orientation of a tree is in P if and only if it can be solved by
k
-consistency, for some
k
?

6.

Is it true that
most
orientations of finite trees are hard, i.e., is it true that
the probability that an orientation of a tree drawn uniformly at random from the set of all such trees with vertex set
\{1,\dots,n\}
is NP-hard tends to 1 as
n
tends to infinity
[
24
]
? The answer is yes if we ask the question for random labelled digraphs instead of random labelled trees
[
80
]
.

7.

Determine the smallest trees whose CSP is P-hard (assuming that
\operatorname{NL}\neq\operatorname{P}
).
It is known that they must have at least 16 vertices, since all smaller trees have a majority polymorphism and thus are in NL
[
24
]
.

8.

Is the CSP of most digraphs with a Taylor polymorphism P-hard?

9.

Is the CSP of most digraphs with a Taylor polymorphism not solvable by
k
-consistency, for all
k
?

The following open problems require knowledge of concepts that have not been covered in this course; however, references are provided where these concepts are defined formally.

1.

Prove that a finite-domain CSP is in P if and only if it can be expressed in
Choiceless Polynomial Time

[
19
]
.

2.

(Dalmau
[
47
]
) Is it true that if
\Csp(H)
is in NL, then
\Csp(H)
is in linear Datalog?
Is this at least true for digraphs
H
? The same question would already be interesting for orientations of trees.

3.

(Egri-Larose-Tesson
[
51
]
)
Is it true that if
\Csp(H)
is in L, then
\Csp(H)
is in symmetric Datalog? Is this at least true for digraphs
H
? It would already be interesting for orientations of trees.

4.

(Larose-Tesson
[
76
]
)
Is it true that if the polymorphism algebra of
H
generates a congruence join-semidistributive variety, then
\Csp(H)
is in linear Datalog? Is this at least true for digraphs
H
? It would already be interesting for orientations of trees.

5.

Is it true that if
\Csp(H)
is not P-hard under logspace reductions, then it is in NC?
It is known that NC is closed under logspace reductions, and it is believed that P is different from NP. Moreover, the CSP for the structure
(\{0,1\};\{0,1\}^{3}\setminus\{(1,1,0)\},\{0\},\{1\})
is P-hard (see Exercise
122
).
Is it true that if
\Csp(H)
does not pp-construct this structure then
\Csp(H)
is in NC?

Finally we pose some curious open questions for concrete finite digraphs.

1.

Is the CSP of the orientation of a tree displayed on the right

in NL
[
24
]
?

2.

What are the smallest digraphs with a Taylor polymorphism that

cannot be solved by Datalog?

For CSPs over infinite domains, there are numerous open problems,

and I invite
the reader to have a look at
[
20
]
.
